Ward Topic · Practical Management

Electrolyte Imbalances

Hypokalaemia · Hyperkalaemia · Hyponatraemia · Hypernatraemia
Overview
Hypokalaemia
Hyperkalaemia
Hyponatraemia
Hypernatraemia
Escalation
References
⚑ Core Principle Always treat the cause, not just the number. Electrolyte disturbances are a symptom of an underlying problem — correcting the value without identifying and addressing the cause will result in recurrence. Correct slowly and reassess frequently. Rapid correction of sodium imbalances in particular can cause serious and irreversible neurological harm.
0
Quick Reference — Normal Ranges & Escalation Thresholds
ElectrolyteNormal rangeMildModerateSevere — call senior staff
Potassium (K⁺)3.5–5.0 mmol/LLow: 3.0–3.4
High: 5.1–5.9		Low: 2.5–2.9
High: 6.0–6.4		Low: <2.5
High: ≥6.5 or any ECG changes
Sodium (Na⁺)135–145 mmol/LLow: 130–134
High: 146–149		Low: 125–129
High: 150–154		Low: <125 or symptomatic
High: ≥155 or symptomatic

Always check ECG in hyperkalaemia and severe hypo/hypokalaemia. Always check U&E, glucose, and clinical context before treating. Never treat a number in isolation.

1
Hypokalaemia (K⁺ <3.5 mmol/L)

Common causes in surgical patients

  • GI losses — vomiting, NG aspiration, diarrhoea, high stoma output, fistula
  • Inadequate replacement in IV fluids
  • Diuretics (furosemide, thiazides)
  • Refeeding syndrome
  • Post-operative urinary losses

Clinical features

  • Mild: weakness, fatigue, constipation, cramps
  • Moderate/severe: muscle weakness, ileus, cardiac arrhythmias, respiratory muscle weakness
  • ECG: flattened T waves, prominent U waves, prolonged QT, arrhythmias

⚠ Check magnesium first

Hypomagnesaemia is the commonest cause of refractory hypokalaemia. If K⁺ fails to correct with replacement, check Mg²⁺ and correct it — potassium will not correct until magnesium is replete.

Management

SeverityRouteRegimen
Mild (3.0–3.5)Oral (preferred)Oral KCl (Sando-K or equivalent) 2–4 tablets TDS. Encourage dietary potassium. Monitor U&E daily.
Moderate (2.5–3.0) or unable to take oralIV20 mmol KCl in 500 ml balanced crystalloid over ≥2 hours. Max rate 10 mmol/hour peripherally. Recheck K⁺ at 4 hours. Repeat as needed. (See IV fluids protocol — Adding K⁺ section)
Severe (<2.5) or symptomatic / ECG changesIV — senior staffCall senior staff. Higher rates or central line may be needed. Cardiac monitoring mandatory. Concurrent Mg²⁺ replacement.

10 mmol of KCl raises serum K⁺ by approximately 0.1 mmol/L only — correction of a significant deficit requires several replacements over time. Do not rush.

2
Hyperkalaemia (K⁺ >5.0 mmol/L)

Common causes in surgical patients

  • Renal impairment / AKI — most common
  • Tissue breakdown — crush injury, rhabdomyolysis, haemolysis, bowel ischaemia
  • Medications — ACE inhibitors, potassium-sparing diuretics, NSAIDs, LMWH
  • Metabolic acidosis — K⁺ shifts extracellularly
  • Excessive K⁺ replacement
  • Pseudohyperkalaemia — haemolysed sample; repeat before treating if patient is asymptomatic

🚨 Do ECG first — always

ECG changes dictate urgency. Peaked T waves → widened QRS → sine wave pattern → VF/asystole. Any ECG change beyond peaked T waves = emergency — call senior staff immediately.

ECG changes by severity

  • Peaked (tall, narrow, tented) T waves — earliest sign; often K⁺ 5.5–6.5
  • Prolonged PR, widened QRS — moderate/severe; K⁺ usually >6.5
  • Sine wave pattern, loss of P waves — life-threatening; imminent arrest

Management — stepwise (the four actions)

Step 1 — Protect the heart (if ECG changes present)Calcium gluconate 10 ml of 10% IV over 5–10 minutes. Stabilises the cardiac membrane. Onset within 1–3 min; lasts 30–60 min. Does NOT lower potassium — buys time for definitive treatment. Can repeat after 5 minutes if ECG unchanged. Use calcium chloride 10 ml of 10% if central access available — more potent.
Step 2 — Shift K⁺ into cells (insulin-dextrose cocktail)10 units soluble insulin mixed with 50 ml of 50% dextrose, given IV over 15–30 minutes. [1,2] Onset 15–30 min; peaks 30–60 min; lasts 2–6 hours. Lowers K⁺ by ~0.6–1.0 mmol/L [1,3].

⚠ Hypoglycaemia risk is significant — prevalence 7–75% in studies, may occur 60–120 minutes after administration [1,2,3] and last up to 6 hours (longer in renal failure). Monitor capillary blood glucose (CBG — fingerprick bedside glucometer reading) at 30 min, 1 hour, and 2 hours after administration. If CBG <4 mmol/L treat immediately.

If pre-treatment glucose is low (<7 mmol/L): consider reducing insulin to 5 units (with 25 ml of 50% dextrose) to reduce hypoglycaemia risk — emerging evidence supports 5 units as non-inferior for mild-moderate hyperkalaemia [2].

Note: 50% dextrose is highly osmolar and irritant — administer via a large vein; watch for extravasation and phlebitis.
Step 3 — Nebulised salbutamol (adjunct)10–20 mg nebulised salbutamol (use 10 mg if ischaemic heart disease or significant tachycardia). Works synergistically with insulin-dextrose to lower K⁺ by an additional 0.5–1.0 mmol/L. Onset 15–30 min; lasts ~2 hours. Useful adjunct — not a standalone treatment. Note: may be less effective in patients on beta-blockers.
Step 4 — Remove K⁺ from the bodySteps 1–3 shift or stabilise K⁺ only — they do not remove it. Definitive removal requires: Furosemide IV (if adequate renal function and not anuric — promotes urinary K⁺ excretion); calcium resonium (ion exchange resin — oral or enema, slow acting); or dialysis (for severe refractory hyperkalaemia or oligoanuric renal failure — senior and nephrology decision).

Treat the cause in parallel

  • Stop all potassium-containing fluids and medications contributing to hyperkalaemia
  • Withhold ACE inhibitors, ARBs, potassium-sparing diuretics, NSAIDs
  • Treat acidosis if present — correcting pH will drive K⁺ back into cells
  • Recheck K⁺ at 1–2 hours and again at 4–6 hours after treatment
3
Hyponatraemia (Na⁺ <135 mmol/L)

Common causes in surgical patients

  • Post-operative — ADH release causes water retention; over-administration of hypotonic fluids
  • GI losses replaced with hypotonic fluid
  • SIADH — pain, nausea, opioids, CNS pathology, pulmonary disease, drugs
  • Diuretics (especially thiazides)
  • Cardiac, hepatic, or renal failure with fluid overload

Clinical features (by rate of onset, not just level)

  • Mild/chronic: often asymptomatic; nausea, malaise, headache
  • Moderate: confusion, lethargy, falls
  • Severe/acute: seizures, reduced GCS, respiratory arrest — emergency

🚨 Symptomatic (seizures/reduced GCS) hyponatraemia — emergency

Call senior staff immediately. Controlled correction with hypertonic saline (1.8% or 3% NaCl) may be required — this is a senior-led decision requiring close monitoring. Target: raise Na⁺ by 1–2 mmol/L per hour until symptoms resolve, then slow to <8–10 mmol/24 h.

Management — asymptomatic/mild-moderate

⚠ The golden rule — correct slowly

Rapid correction of chronic hyponatraemia risks osmotic demyelination syndrome (ODS) [5,7] — an irreversible and often fatal neurological complication. Do not raise Na⁺ by more than 8–10 mmol/L in any 24-hour period [5,7].

  • Assess volume status first — hypovolaemic, euvolaemic, or hypervolaemic hyponatraemia each have different treatments
  • Hypovolaemic (low sodium + low volume): cautious IV 0.9% saline — replaces volume and sodium
  • Euvolaemic (SIADH): fluid restriction 1–1.5 L/day; treat the cause; avoid hypotonic fluids
  • Hypervolaemic (oedematous states): fluid restriction + treat underlying cause (diuresis in heart failure, etc.)
  • Monitor Na⁺ every 4–6 hours when actively correcting
  • Correct any concurrent hypokalaemia — K⁺ replacement also raises Na⁺
4
Hypernatraemia (Na⁺ >145 mmol/L)

Common causes in surgical patients

  • Inadequate free water intake — NBM, impaired thirst, reduced consciousness
  • Excessive water losses — fever, sweating, diarrhoea, polyuria (diabetes insipidus, osmotic diuresis)
  • Excessive hypertonic sodium administration

Clinical features

  • Thirst, dry mucous membranes, reduced skin turgor
  • Confusion, lethargy, irritability
  • Severe: seizures, coma, intracranial haemorrhage (from brain shrinkage)

Management

⚠ Correct slowly — same principle as hyponatraemia

Rapid correction of hypernatraemia risks cerebral oedema. Do not lower Na⁺ by more than 8–10 mmol/L in any 24-hour period [5,7] in chronic hypernatraemia (>48 hours). Acute hypernatraemia (<48 hours) may be corrected more quickly — senior decision.

  • Assess and correct volume status first — if hypovolaemic, start with 0.9% saline to restore circulation, then switch to hypotonic fluid once stable
  • Free water replacement: oral water (preferred if tolerated) or IV 5% dextrose or 0.45% saline
  • Calculate free water deficit: Free water deficit (L) = 0.6 × weight (kg) [6] × [(Na⁺ ÷ 140) − 1]
  • Replace deficit over 48–72 hours — half in first 24 hours, remainder over next 24–48 hours
  • Monitor Na⁺ every 4–6 hours when actively correcting
  • Identify and treat the underlying cause — particularly diabetes insipidus if suspected

In the post-operative surgical patient, hypernatraemia most commonly reflects inadequate free water provision while NBM. The fix is usually simple: ensure adequate maintenance fluids include free water, and encourage oral intake as soon as appropriate.

5
Escalation Triggers

Call senior staff immediately

  • K⁺ ≥6.5 mmol/L or any ECG changes beyond peaked T waves
  • K⁺ <2.5 mmol/L or symptomatic hypokalaemia (arrhythmia, weakness, paralysis)
  • Na⁺ <125 mmol/L or any symptomatic hyponatraemia (seizures, confusion, reduced GCS)
  • Na⁺ ≥155 mmol/L or any symptomatic hypernatraemia
  • Hypoglycaemia after insulin-dextrose cocktail unresponsive to treatment
  • Electrolyte disturbance not correcting with standard treatment
  • Any uncertainty about rate or route of correction
§
References

Peri-operative · Ward Level

Post-operative Assessment

Early post-op monitoring · fever · diet readiness · complications · escalation
Overview
Vitals & Monitoring
Post-op Fever
Diet Readiness
Wound & Drain
Fluids & Output
Analgesia
Escalation
References
⚑ Core Principle Most post-operative complications are predictable and detectable early. Regular, systematic assessment catches them before they become emergencies. Never assume deterioration is "normal post-op" without examining the patient and thinking through the differential. When in doubt — examine, investigate, and escalate.
0
Early Post-op Assessment — Frequency & Framework

Minimum assessment frequency

PeriodFrequencyKey focus
0–4 hours post-op (recovery/immediate ward)Every 30 minAirway, haemodynamic stability, pain, bleeding, urine output, conscious level
4–24 hoursEvery 2–4 hoursVitals, pain, fluid balance, wound, drain output, nausea/vomiting
Day 1–3At least twice daily (morning and evening ward round)Full assessment — fever, diet, mobility, VTE risk, wound, drain, fluid balance, bowel function
Day 3 onwardsDaily minimum; more if concernsComplications, diet advancement, mobility, discharge planning

Document every assessment. If you are not documenting it, it did not happen. Senior staff cannot act on information that is not recorded.

1
Vital Signs — What to Check and Why
ParameterNormal / targetConcern — investigate & escalate
Temperature36–37.5°C>38°C on any post-op day — use fever framework (Section 2). <36°C — hypothermia, consider sepsis
Heart rate60–100 bpm>100 — pain, hypovolaemia, fever, PE, bleeding, arrhythmia. <50 — bradycardia; check medications
Blood pressureWithin 20% of pre-op baselineSystolic <90 or drop >30 from baseline — haemorrhage, sepsis, PE, cardiac event
Respiratory rate12–20 /min>20 — pain, PE, pneumonia, sepsis, fluid overload. <10 — opioid effect
Oxygen saturation≥95% on air (or baseline if COPD)<94% — investigate cause; supplemental O₂ and review. Falling trend is a warning sign
Urine output≥0.5 ml/kg/hour<0.5 ml/kg/hour for >2 h — assess fluid status; may need IV fluid challenge; senior review if persists
Conscious levelAlert and orientedAny new confusion, agitation, reduced GCS — sepsis, hyponatraemia, opioid toxicity, PE, stroke; call senior
Pain score≤3/10 at rest>6/10 — inadequate analgesia; also consider new complication (wound dehiscence, abscess, anastomotic leak)
2
Post-operative Fever — The 5 Ws Framework

Post-operative fever is defined as temperature >38°C. The cause follows a predictable timeline — use the day of fever to guide your differential [1,2] diagnosis. A low-grade fever in the first 24–48 hours after major surgery is common [2] and often reflects normal inflammatory response — but always examine the patient and exclude serious causes before attributing it to this.

WPost-op dayCauseLook for / do
WindDay 1–2Pulmonary — pneumonia, aspiration; PE (any time). Note: atelectasis is classically taught as a cause but current evidence does not clearly support it as a cause of fever [2]Chest exam, SpO₂, CXR, sputum culture; incentive spirometry; early mobilisation
WaterDay 3–5Urinary tract infection — especially if catheterisedUrinalysis + urine MC&S; remove catheter as soon as no longer needed
WoundDay 5–7Surgical site infection — superficial or deepInspect wound; swab if discharge; consider imaging if deep collection suspected
(W)abscessDay 5–7Intra-abdominal or organ-space collection; anastomotic leakAbdominal exam; CT abdomen if suspected; drain if needed
WalkingDay 5+DVT / pulmonary embolism — risk persists weeks post-opLeg exam; CTPA if PE suspected; ensure VTE prophylaxis in place
Wonder drugsAny time (often Day 7+)Drug fever — reaction to antibiotic, blood product, anaesthetic agentReview drug chart; exclude all other causes first; stop offending drug if identified
Wing / WaterwayAny timeIV line infection, phlebitis, line-related bacteraemiaInspect all IV sites; remove unnecessary lines; send blood cultures

Fever workup — minimum when cause is not immediately clear

  • Full examination — lungs, abdomen, wound, all IV sites, legs
  • FBC, U&E (CRP if available)
  • Blood cultures ×2 (before antibiotics)
  • Urinalysis + urine MC&S if catheterised or urinary symptoms
  • CXR if respiratory symptoms or Day 1–2 fever
  • CT abdomen if intra-abdominal source suspected (especially Day 5+)

Do not miss

  • Anastomotic leak — Day 3–7, rising fever, tachycardia, worsening abdominal pain, failure to progress; CT with contrast urgently
  • Necrotising wound infection / gas gangrene — any day; rapidly spreading wound infection with crepitus or systemic toxicity; emergency theatre
  • PE — any day; sudden dyspnoea, pleuritic chest pain, tachycardia, desaturation; CTPA
3
Assessing Readiness for Diet

Early oral feeding is supported by ERAS (Enhanced Recovery After Surgery) evidence — the goal is to commence oral intake as soon as safely possible [3,4] after surgery. Do not keep patients unnecessarily NBM.

Ready to commence oral fluids — all should be true

  • Patient is alert and conscious level is adequate to protect airway
  • Nausea and vomiting controlled — not actively vomiting
  • No clinical signs of ileus — some bowel sounds present, not significantly distended, passing flatus (for bowel surgery)
  • No ongoing surgical concern that mandates NBM (e.g. pending re-operation, anastomotic leak concern)
  • Airway protective reflexes intact — not heavily sedated

Advancing diet — stepwise

Sips of waterFirst step — assess swallow and tolerance before advancing. If tolerated without vomiting, proceed.
Free oral fluidsWater, tea, juice. Ensure adequate volume before stepping up.
Light dietClear soups, toast, light foods. Advance as tolerated.
Normal dietFull diet when tolerating light diet well. Stop IV fluids once oral intake is adequate.

Special considerations by procedure

  • Upper GI / oesophageal surgery: diet advancement per surgical protocol; swallow assessment may be needed; do not advance without senior instruction
  • Bowel resection with anastomosis: typically await passage of flatus before advancing to solid diet; early oral fluids generally safe per ERAS
  • Hernia repair, appendicectomy, cholecystectomy: early oral feeding usually within hours of surgery if no concerns
  • NG tube in situ: do not advance oral diet until NG is removed or specifically instructed; aspirate NG before advancing
  • Stoma: ensure stoma functioning before advancing to solid diet

If a patient is not tolerating oral intake by Day 3–5 post major abdominal surgery, consider nutrition support — discuss with senior staff and consider dietitian referral. Refer to the Surgical Nutrition module when available.

4
Wound & Drain Assessment

Wound — check daily

  • Inspect dressing — is it dry, soaked, or leaking?
  • Surrounding skin — erythema, warmth, swelling extending beyond wound edges
  • Any discharge — serous (normal early), serosanguinous, purulent (infection)
  • Wound edges — apposed, dehiscing, or gaping
  • Sutures/staples — intact, intact but under tension, or breaking

Escalate immediately

  • Purulent discharge, spreading erythema, or wound breakdown with fever — wound infection / dehiscence
  • Fascial dehiscence — bowel or omentum visible; cover with moist sterile dressing and call senior staff urgently
  • Crepitus in wound — gas-forming infection; emergency

Drains — document at every assessment

  • Volume in preceding period — document on fluid balance chart
  • Character — serous, sanguinous, bilious, enteric, purulent
  • Drain site — secure, not kinked or blocked, no leakage around site
  • Criteria for removal — senior staff decision; do not remove drains without instruction

A sudden increase in drain output, change in character (e.g. bilious or enteric content), or drain running dry when a collection is expected — all warrant immediate senior review.

5
Fluid Balance & Urine Output
  • Record all inputs (IV fluids, oral intake, drug infusions) and all outputs (urine, drain, NG, stoma, vomit) on a strict fluid balance chart — calculate cumulative balance every 8–12 hours
  • Target urine output ≥0.5 ml/kg/hour — catheterise if accurate monitoring is needed
  • Review IV fluid prescription every 24 hours — stop IV fluids as soon as oral intake is adequate
  • Post-operative patients retain Na⁺ and water — do not over-prescribe; see IV Fluid Management protocol
  • Check daily U&E in the first 48–72 hours after major surgery or if there are concerns

Oliguria post-operatively — think before giving fluid

Not all post-operative oliguria is hypovolaemia. Before giving a fluid bolus, assess: is the patient clinically dry (tachycardic, hypotensive, poor skin turgor)? Or are they oedematous and fluid-overloaded with poor cardiac output? Fluid in the wrong setting makes things worse. If uncertain — call senior staff.

6
Analgesia Assessment
  • Assess pain score at every review — at rest and on movement/coughing
  • Inadequate analgesia impairs breathing, mobilisation, coughing, and recovery
  • Use a multimodal approach — regular paracetamol, NSAIDs (if not contraindicated), opioids for breakthrough; step down as recovery progresses
  • Worsening pain after initial improvement is a red flag — think complication, not just inadequate analgesia

Opioid caution

  • Monitor respiratory rate and conscious level in patients on opioids — RR <10 or reduced GCS with opioid on board — hold opioid and call senior staff; have naloxone available
  • Opioids cause constipation and ileus — prescribe laxatives alongside; reduce dose as soon as possible
7
Escalation Triggers

Call senior staff

  • Haemodynamic instability — hypotension, tachycardia not explained by pain or fever alone
  • Falling oxygen saturation or increased oxygen requirement
  • Fever with systemic toxicity — tachycardia, hypotension, confusion
  • Worsening abdominal pain or peritonism after abdominal surgery — suspect anastomotic leak or collection
  • Wound fascial dehiscence or bowel visible
  • Sudden increase or change in character of drain output
  • Oliguria not responding to a fluid challenge
  • Any new confusion or reduced conscious level
  • Failure to progress as expected — not tolerating diet by Day 3–5, not mobilising, persistent ileus

If you are reviewing a post-operative patient and something feels wrong but you cannot identify what — call senior staff. Clinical unease is a valid reason to escalate.

§
References

  2. Davenport B, Nelson AR. Postoperative fever. StatPearls [Internet]. Treasure Island (FL): StatPearls Publishing; 2024. Available from: ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK560667 (current review: fever definition, causes, timeline, atelectasis evidence critique)

Acute Presentation · Surgical Emergency

Perforated Peptic Ulcer

Duodenal & gastric perforation · resuscitation · operative management
Overview
Assessment
Resuscitation
Non-operative
Surgery — DU
Surgery — GU
Post-op & Discharge
Escalation
References
⚠ Surgical Emergency Perforated peptic ulcer carries a 30-day mortality of approximately 23.5% [4] — the most common indication for emergency peptic ulcer surgery [4] and responsible for ~40% of all ulcer-related deaths [4]. Prompt diagnosis, rapid resuscitation, and timely operative intervention are the key determinants of outcome. Do not delay — call senior staff immediately.
0
Overview — Duodenal vs Gastric Perforation
FeatureDuodenal perforationGastric perforation
Common siteAnterior wall of first part of duodenumLesser curve or antrum (most common); greater curve (consider NSAIDs/ischaemia)
Age / sexYounger; male predominanceOlder; more equal sex distribution
Malignancy riskVery low — duodenal ulcers essentially have no malignant potentialSignificant — historically ~9–16% of perforated gastric ulcers; contemporary series lower (~3–9%), but still far from negligible. All gastric perforations need definitive histology[1,6]
Operative approachSimple closure ± omental patchExcision of ulcer edges + closure ± omental patch; resection if large or suspicious
MortalityLower (~5% in uncomplicated)Higher (~10–20%); higher complication rate
H. pyloriMost commonly H. pylori or NSAID-relatedH. pylori, NSAIDs, stress — test and treat all

The perforation:bleeding ratio is approximately 1:6 — perforation is less common but more immediately life-threatening and the most common indication for emergency surgery in peptic ulcer disease.

1
Clinical Assessment & Diagnosis

History

  • Sudden onset severe epigastric pain — often described as "knife-like" or "tearing"; rapidly becomes generalised
  • Prior history of peptic ulcer disease, dyspepsia, NSAID or aspirin use, steroid use, H. pylori
  • Nausea, vomiting; patient often lies very still (peritonism)
  • Time of onset — important for prognosis (Boey score)

Examination

  • Generalised peritonism — board-like rigidity, guarding, rebound tenderness
  • Absent or reduced bowel sounds
  • Liver dullness may be absent (free gas under diaphragm)
  • Systemic: tachycardia, fever, hypotension in late/septic presentation

Investigations

  • FBC, U&E, LFTs, amylase (exclude pancreatitis), serum lactate, blood cultures if septic
  • Group & save; coagulation
  • Erect CXR — free gas under diaphragm in ~70% of cases; absence does not exclude perforation

🚨 Pneumoperitoneum + peritonitis = operate. Do not delay for CT.

If the erect CXR demonstrates free gas under the diaphragm and the patient has clinical peritonitis consistent with perforated peptic ulcer — the diagnosis is made. CT is not required before theatre in this scenario. Delaying surgery to obtain a CT scan in a patient with established peritonitis [1] and pneumoperitoneum increases morbidity and mortality. Resuscitate and proceed to theatre.

When CT is appropriate

  • No pneumoperitoneum on CXR — CT is more sensitive and may demonstrate free gas, free fluid, or perforation site not visible on plain film
  • Diagnostic uncertainty — clinical picture is equivocal and alternative diagnoses are being considered
  • Patient is stable and a candidate for non-operative management (NOM) — CT is required to confirm a sealed small perforation before committing to NOM (see Section 3)
  • Planning complex operative approach — anatomy clarification in selected cases, provided no delay to theatre

Remember: contrast safety applies — confirm adequate hydration, acceptable renal function, and no contrast allergy before IV contrast administration.

  • Urinary catheter for fluid balance monitoring

Boey Risk Score — predicts operative mortality

Risk factorPoints
Shock on admission (systolic BP <90 mmHg)1
Significant medical comorbidity (ASA ≥3)1
Duration of perforation >24 hours1

Boey score interpretation

  • Score 0: mortality ~0–1% [5] — proceed to surgery
  • Score 1: mortality ~10% — surgery still indicated; optimise pre-op
  • Score 2: mortality ~45% — high risk; consider carefully; senior-led decision
  • Score 3: mortality ~100% — extremely high risk; individualised senior decision; non-operative management may be considered
2
Resuscitation — Pre-operative

Resuscitation and theatre preparation run in parallel — do not delay surgery waiting for perfect parameters in the unstable patient.

  • IV access ×2 large bore; IV fluids — balanced crystalloid resuscitation
  • NG tube insertion — decompress the stomach; reduce ongoing contamination
  • Urinary catheter — strict fluid balance; target urine output ≥0.5 ml/kg/hour
  • IV broad-spectrum antibiotics — start immediately after blood cultures; cover gram-negative and anaerobic organisms per local policy
  • IV proton pump inhibitor (PPI) — given pre-operatively as standard practice to reduce gastric acid secretion and limit further mucosal injury [1]. Note: the evidence that pre-operative IV PPI changes operative outcomes or mortality in perforated peptic ulcer specifically is weak — it is given on a sound physiological rationale and expert consensus, not RCT evidence. Do not delay theatre for PPI administration.
  • Analgesia — IV opioid analgesia; do not withhold adequate pain relief
  • Nil by mouth
  • Consent — explain procedure, risks including re-perforation, leak, stoma, death; Boey score informs risk discussion
  • Inform theatre and anaesthetics — this is an emergency case

Goal of resuscitation: correct haemodynamic instability sufficiently to safely proceed to anaesthesia. In the shocked patient, damage control resuscitation and expedited surgery take priority over achieving normal parameters.

3
Non-operative Management — Selected Cases Only

Non-operative management (NOM) is a senior-led decision, appropriate only in very selected patients. Surgery remains the standard of care for the majority.

NOM may be considered when ALL of the following apply

  • CT confirms small, sealed perforation with no ongoing leak
  • Patient is haemodynamically stable and not peritonitic
  • Symptoms have been present <24 hours
  • No evidence of generalised peritonitis on clinical examination
  • Boey score 0–1 only
  • Senior surgeon has made and documented the decision

NOM regimen

  • Strict NBM; NG on free drainage
  • IV fluids, IV PPI, IV antibiotics
  • Serial abdominal examinations every 4–6 hours
  • Any deterioration → immediate surgery; do not persist with NOM in a deteriorating patient
  • Repeat CT at 24–48 hours if continuing NOM

NOM failure rate is significant. If there is any doubt, operate. A timely operation in a stable patient is far safer than an emergency re-laparotomy in a patient who has deteriorated on NOM.

4
Operative Management — Duodenal Perforation

Standard approach — suture closure of perforation

Closure of the perforation is the recommended standard operative technique for duodenal perforation. Two main techniques are used — both are acceptable and the choice depends on perforation size, tissue quality, and surgeon preference. There is currently no high-level evidence mandating one over the other. [1,2,3]

Technique — two approaches

Option A — Modified Graham's patch (Cellan-Jones repair) — primary suture closure + omental buttress

What it is: The perforation is first closed with interrupted absorbable sutures, then a tag of viable omentum is laid over the closed repair and sutured in place as a buttress.

Advantage: Primary closure reduces leak risk — when both techniques are feasible, sutured closure is associated with lower leak rates in meta-analysis. More intuitive technique for most surgeons.

Disadvantage: Slightly longer operative time; in friable or ischaemic tissue, sutures may cut through.

Option B — True Graham's patch (plug technique) — omentum without primary suture closure

What it is: A tag of omentum is placed directly over the perforation and held with sutures passing through the full thickness of the duodenal wall on either side — the perforation itself is not primarily closed; the omentum plugs it.

Advantage: Simpler to perform, particularly in the laparoscopic setting or where perforation edges are friable and will not hold sutures; shorter operative time.

Disadvantage: Leak rate 3.3–6.7%; some evidence of higher leak rate than modified Graham's in smaller perforations.

📋 Evidence position

Both techniques are acceptable and widely used. Modified Graham's (Cellan-Jones) is preferred when tissue quality is good and primary closure is feasible — evidence suggests lower leak rates with sutured closure. True Graham's plug is preferred when tissue is friable, ischaemic, or oedematous and sutures will not hold, or where operative time must be minimised. The WSES guideline supports either technique — no formal recommendation mandates one over the other. Use surgeon judgement based on the intraoperative findings.

Approach and additional steps

  • Approach: laparoscopic (preferred where expertise available) or open midline laparotomy — both acceptable; open equally well-supported in resource-limited settings
  • Peritoneal lavage: thorough washout with warm saline — irrigate all quadrants
  • Drain placement: subhepatic drain near the repair — assists detection of post-operative leak

Large perforations (≥2 cm) — expanded operative options

📋 Evidence position on large perforations

Large perforations (≥2 cm) present the greatest operative challenge. The key evidence points to guide decision-making:

  • Omental patch repair for large PPU achieves comparable outcomes to gastrectomy in terms of leak, collection, re-operation, and mortality — yet emergency gastrectomy carries significantly higher morbidity and mortality in its own right
  • Every 10 mm increase in ulcer size increases leak rate 3.3-fold [7] — perforations ≥25 mm carry the highest omental patch leak risk
  • The operative decision should not be based on size alone — intraoperative findings (tissue quality, patient stability, perforation anatomy, surgeon expertise) must all be weighed
  • WSES recommends a tailored approach based on ulcer location — gastric resection for large gastric ulcers, repair ± pyloric exclusion for large duodenal ulcers

Large duodenal perforation (≥2 cm) — operative options

OptionWhen to considerNotes
Omental patch repair (modified Graham's/Cellan-Jones)First choice attempt even for large perforations — if tissue quality allows and margins can be approximated without tensionComparable outcomes to resection in published series; faster; less morbidity than gastrectomy
Pyloric exclusion + gastrojejunostomyWhen primary closure of the duodenum is not possible; diverts gastric content away from the repairPylorus is closed (stapled or sutured from inside via gastrotomy) and gastrojejunostomy constructed; bile drainage may be added; re-opens spontaneously over weeks
Jejunal serosal patchFriable tissue, inadequate omentum, or failed primary repairLoop of jejunum sutured as a biological patch over the defect; reasonable leak rates reported
Antrectomy ± D1–D2 resection with diversionLarge perforation with malignancy suspicion, or when duodenum cannot be repaired by any other meansOnly first portion of duodenum can be resected safely without risking bile duct or pancreatic head injury; high morbidity — senior decision
Duodenostomy (tube drainage)Extreme damage control — cannot close or reconstructWSES: only in extreme circumstances; high complication rate; bridges to re-operation

Large gastric perforation (≥2 cm) — operative options

OptionWhen to considerNotes
Omental patch repairHaemodynamically unstable patient; damage control; tissue allows patchComparable outcomes to gastrectomy in published series; faster; always biopsy edges
Wedge resection / ulcer excision + closureLarge ulcer on lesser curve or anterior wall where local resection is feasibleRemoves ulcer for histology; definitive for benign disease; simpler than formal gastrectomy
Partial/distal gastrectomy (Billroth I or II)Large perforation, malignancy suspected or confirmed, or where local repair is not feasibleWSES preferred for large gastric ulcers; allows definitive histology; higher morbidity than patch repair; requires reconstruction skill
Jejunal serosal patchInadequate omentum or poor local tissue qualityAlternative biological patch; similar leak rates to omental repair in some series
Falciform ligament patchNo viable omentum availableReasonable alternative to omental patch; published with good outcomes

All large perforation decisions are consultant-led. The operating team must consider: is the patient stable enough for a longer procedure? Is the tissue quality adequate for the chosen repair? Is malignancy likely? What reconstruction skill is available? A staged approach — damage control closure now, formal resection later when stable — is always a valid option in the critically unwell patient.

Definitive anti-ulcer surgery

  • Definitive surgery (e.g. vagotomy) is no longer routinely recommended in the emergency setting — modern H. pylori eradication therapy and PPI treatment are the definitive medical management
  • H. pylori testing post-operatively and eradication therapy is essential to prevent recurrence

Laparoscopic repair is the preferred approach where expertise is available — comparable outcomes to open repair with less wound morbidity. In our setting where laparoscopic expertise may not always be available, open repair via midline laparotomy is equally acceptable and well-supported by evidence.

5
Operative Management — Gastric Perforation

🔑 Critical difference — always biopsy a gastric ulcer perforation

Gastric ulcers carry a real risk of underlying malignancy — historically reported in ~9–16% of perforated gastric ulcers, with contemporary series lower (~3–9%) but still far from negligible — versus essentially nil for duodenal ulcers.[1,6] Every gastric perforation must have definitive histology obtained — this is non-negotiable. Closing a perforated gastric malignancy without tissue diagnosis delays treatment and worsens prognosis.

Honest nuance: some recent prospective series found no malignancy on intraoperative biopsy and have questioned routine intraoperative biopsy, favouring closure plus early endoscopic biopsy on recovery — and the local rate depends on background gastric-cancer epidemiology. But the safe, widely-held surgical rule stands: obtain tissue from a perforated gastric ulcer, whether at operation or by guaranteed early endoscopic follow-up. Never assume benign.[6]

Operative approach

  • Small perforation (<2 cm), no malignancy suspicion: excise ulcer edges (to obtain tissue for histology) + primary closure ± omental patch + peritoneal lavage + drain
  • Large perforation (≥2 cm) or suspicious for malignancy: resection with frozen section histology where available; if frozen section not available, close the perforation, take generous biopsies, and plan formal staging and definitive resection once histology is available
  • All gastric perforations: peritoneal washout + subhepatic drain

Post-operative considerations specific to gastric perforation

  • Higher complication rate and mortality than duodenal perforation — monitor closely
  • Ensure biopsy results are followed up — do not allow histology to be lost in the system
  • If malignancy confirmed: refer for formal oncological staging and definitive management
  • H. pylori test and treat post-operatively in all cases
  • Continue IV PPI; step down to oral PPI on diet resumption

Gastric perforations have approximately double the mortality of duodenal perforations. This should be reflected in the pre-operative consent discussion.

6
Post-operative Care & Post-discharge Plan

Immediate post-operative care (in-hospital)

  • Continue IV antibiotics for 3–5 days post-op (or until clinically improving, afebrile, tolerating oral intake) then step down to oral per local policy
  • Continue IV PPI until oral intake resumes; then high-dose oral PPI (omeprazole 40 mg BD) — this is the better-evidenced component; strongly guideline-supported for mucosal healing and recurrence prevention
  • NG tube — remove when aspirate minimal and bowel sounds return
  • Diet: commence sips of water once NG removed and bowel sounds present; advance slowly
  • Drain — remove when output <50 ml/day and no clinical concern about leak; senior decision
  • Monitor for post-operative leak: rising fever, tachycardia, worsening abdominal pain, increasing drain output → CT and senior review urgently
  • VTE prophylaxis: LMWH + TED stockings; commence when haemostasis confirmed post-op
  • NSAID review: discontinue if possible; if essential, ensure with PPI cover on discharge

Post-discharge — H. pylori eradication

Key points for the perforated ulcer

  • H. pylori eradication is the most important post-discharge intervention — it reduces ulcer recurrence from ~80%/year to <5%, and separately cuts the risk of recurrent ulcer complications (re-perforation/rebleeding) which otherwise remains high while the infection persists
  • If an intra-operative CLO test or histology was positive, treat without waiting for further testing; otherwise treat empirically where testing is not readily available
  • Prescribe the eradication course and continue PPI for ulcer healing, then confirm eradication with a test of cure — mandatory after a perforated ulcer

↗ Full protocol

For the complete eradication protocol — test-vs-empirical detail, the BQT regimen and alternatives, the local bismuth-availability workaround, local prevalence data, and test-of-cure timing (≥4 weeks post-antibiotics, ≥2 weeks off PPI) — see H. pylori Management in Ward Topics. The eradication protocol is identical whether the ulcer perforated, bled, or presented uncomplicated.

Post-discharge endoscopy — who needs it and when?

ScenarioEndoscopy?Timing
Perforated duodenal ulcer — benign intraoperative biopsiesNot routinely requiredOnly if symptoms persist/recur
Perforated gastric ulcer — adequate benign biopsiesYes — recommended6–12 weeks post-op
Perforated gastric ulcer — inadequate or no biopsiesYes — essential6–8 weeks post-op urgently
Perforated gastric ulcer — malignancy confirmed/suspectedYes — urgent oncological workupAs soon as patient is fit
Any PPU — persistent or recurrent symptoms post-dischargeYesEarly — do not wait

Why endoscopy is NOT routinely needed after a benign duodenal perforation

Two reasons, both specific to the duodenum: (1) negligible malignancy risk — duodenal ulcers are essentially never malignant, so unlike a gastric ulcer there is no cancer to exclude with follow-up biopsies; and (2) a reliable cure pathway — a benign duodenal ulcer treated with H. pylori eradication (± a PPI course) and NSAID avoidance heals dependably, and eradication prevents relapse after simple repair.[1,6] Guidelines therefore do not recommend routine follow-up endoscopy for a duodenal ulcer — it is reserved for persistent or recurrent symptoms, or any alarm features (bleeding, weight loss, anaemia, vomiting), which would prompt endoscopy to look for an alternative or complicating diagnosis.[1]

Why follow-up endoscopy after a gastric perforation — even with a "benign" intraoperative biopsy?

The rationale is the unreliability of intraoperative biopsy in the emergency setting, not a precisely-quantified miss rate. At perforation the ulcer edge is necrotic, inflamed and oedematous, and a significant proportion of intraoperative biopsies yield anatomically incorrect or non-diagnosable tissue — so a "benign" result may simply reflect a poor sample rather than true absence of malignancy.[6] Because of this, modern evidence favours closure plus guaranteed early endoscopic biopsy on recovery (good-quality tissue, elective conditions, multiple targeted samples) as the dependable way to exclude malignancy — increasingly preferred over relying on intraoperative biopsy alone.[6]

Honest note on the numbers: a benign intraoperative biopsy is reassuring but not definitive. Contemporary malignancy rates in perforated gastric ulcers are ~3–9% (lower than the older 9–23% figures), and prospective series that followed patients up found few or no cancers missed — but the diagnostic quality of emergency biopsy is the weak link, which is why a guaranteed follow-up scope is the safe rule. The principle is unchanged: never let a gastric perforation pass without securing definitive histology, whether intra-operatively or by guaranteed early endoscopy.[6]

Discharge summary — minimum plan before leaving hospital

  • High-dose oral PPI (omeprazole 40 mg BD) for 8 weeks prescribed
  • H. pylori eradication course prescribed and explained to patient
  • Test of cure (urea breath test or stool antigen test) — patient informed it must be done ≥4 weeks post-antibiotics and ≥2 weeks post-PPI
  • Follow-up endoscopy arranged or referenced in discharge letter (all gastric; selected duodenal)
  • NSAID cessation confirmed or PPI cover prescribed if NSAIDs unavoidable
  • Smoking and alcohol counselling — both impair healing and increase recurrence
  • All of the above documented in discharge letter
7
Escalation Triggers

Call senior staff immediately

  • Any patient with suspected perforated peptic ulcer — this is always a senior-led case
  • Haemodynamic instability or septic shock on presentation
  • Generalised peritonitis — expedite theatre
  • Deterioration on non-operative management — operate without delay
  • Post-operative fever with worsening abdominal pain — suspect leak; CT urgently
  • Drain output changing character or increasing significantly post-op
  • Histology returns showing malignancy — ensure patient reviewed and referred
§
References

Acute Presentation · Vascular Emergency

Acute Limb Ischaemia

ALI · acute-on-chronic · Rutherford classification · revascularisation
Definitions
The 6 Ps
Rutherford
Embolism vs Thrombosis
Investigations
Initial Rx
Revascularisation
Acute-on-Chronic
Complications
Escalation
References
⚠ Vascular Emergency Acute limb ischaemia is limb and life threatening. Irreversible ischaemic changes can occur within hours. Time from onset to revascularisation is the primary determinant of outcome. Call senior staff and vascular surgery immediately — do not delay for imaging if the limb is clearly threatened.
0
Definitions & Classification
ConditionDefinitionKey features
Acute Limb Ischaemia (ALI)Sudden decrease in limb perfusion causing a potential threat to limb viability — symptoms onset within 14 days [1,2]Sudden onset; no or minimal collateral development; immediate threat to viability; vascular emergency
Acute-on-Chronic Limb IschaemiaAcute ischaemic event superimposed on pre-existing peripheral arterial disease (PAD) — e.g. acute thrombosis on background atherosclerosisHistory of claudication or known PAD; some collateral development; less dramatic presentation than pure ALI; thrombotic cause most likely
Critical Limb Threatening Ischaemia (CLTI)Limb-threatening ischaemia characterised by rest pain, ulceration, or gangrene — symptoms >2 weeks; previously termed critical limb ischaemia (CLI)Chronic course; rest pain, tissue loss; ABI <0.4 typically; requires urgent but not necessarily emergency revascularisation

The 14-day threshold distinguishes ALI from CLTI — onset within 14 days = acute limb ischaemia. The distinction matters because ALI is managed as an emergency revascularisation, while CLTI allows slightly more time for planning. Acute-on-chronic sits between — treat as ALI until proven otherwise.

1
Clinical Features — The 6 Ps
PFeatureClinical significance
PainSudden, severe, unrelenting; not relieved by restALI is not relieved by rest — this distinguishes from claudication. Pain may diminish in late-stage irreversible ischaemia (nerve death) — a false reassurance sign
PallorSkin pallor, mottling below the level of occlusionMottling that blanches = viable. Mottling that does not blanch + woody muscles = irreversible
PulselessnessAbsent distal pulses below the occlusionAssess with handheld Doppler — absent Doppler signal confirms ischaemia; compare with contralateral limb
ParesthesiaNumbness, tingling, sensory lossEarly neurological involvement — indicates threatened limb; worse than pure pain alone
ParalysisMotor weakness or complete loss of movementLate and severe — indicates immediate threat to viability (Rutherford IIb or III); immediate revascularisation
PoikilothermiaCold limb — temperature change at level of occlusionFeel up the limb to find the level of temperature change — this approximates the level of occlusion

The absence of all 6 Ps does not rule out ALI. Use the Rutherford classification to grade severity and guide urgency, not just the number of Ps present.

2
Rutherford Classification — Grading Severity

The Rutherford classification guides urgency and management decisions. Assess sensory loss, motor deficit, and Doppler signals.

ClassViabilitySensory lossMotor deficitDoppler (arterial)Management
IViable — not immediately threatenedNoneNoneAudibleUrgent investigation and planning; anticoagulate; does not require immediate surgery
IIaMarginally threatened — salvageable if promptly treatedMinimal (toes only)NoneOften inaudibleUrgent revascularisation — hours; thrombolysis may be considered
IIbImmediately threatened — salvageable with immediate interventionMore than toes; rest painMild to moderateInaudibleEmergency revascularisation immediately — surgical embolectomy/thrombectomy preferred over thrombolysis given time pressure
IIIIrreversible — major tissue loss inevitableProfound, anaestheticProfound, paralysedInaudibleAmputation or palliation — revascularisation of an irreversible limb risks fatal reperfusion injury and metabolic complications

Rutherford III — do not attempt revascularisation

A limb with non-blanching mottling, woody rigid muscles, profound sensory and motor loss is irreversibly ischaemic. Revascularisation of dead tissue causes massive reperfusion injury — hyperkalaemia, acidosis, renal failure, cardiac arrest. Amputation is the life-saving intervention. This is a senior surgeon decision made immediately.

📋 Predictive validity of the Rutherford classification

The Rutherford classification is not just descriptive — it has been validated as a genuine outcome predictor. A 2025 prospective study (n=252, 3-year follow-up) found: amputation rates 31.9% vs 7.2% in higher vs lower Rutherford classes (p<0.001); mortality 22.6% vs 7.2% [7] (p<0.001); with the most critical transition point between classes 2 and 3 for reoperations, and classes 4–5 for amputation. In ALI specifically, Rutherford Class IIb is an independent predictor of death or amputation (OR 2.44) [8]. This validates using the classification to drive urgency — the worse the class, the faster you must act.

Most Rutherford validation data uses the chronic limb ischaemia scale (Classes 0–6) rather than the ALI-specific scale (Classes I–III) — the principle is consistent but ALI-specific validation evidence is more limited. The classification is nonetheless the accepted standard and ESVS-recommended tool for ALI management decisions [1].

3
Embolism vs Thrombosis — Critical Distinction

Distinguishing the cause drives the operative approach — embolectomy for embolism, thrombolysis or bypass for thrombosis on atherosclerosis.

FeatureEmbolismThrombosis (acute-on-chronic)
OnsetSudden — minutes to hoursMore gradual — hours to days; often subacute
Background historyNo prior claudication or PAD symptomsHistory of claudication, known peripheral arterial disease
Cardiac sourceOften present — AF, recent MI, valvular disease (85% cardiac origin [3])Usually absent
Contralateral pulsesUsually normal — disease is focalOften diminished — bilateral PAD
Angiographic appearanceSharp cutoff ("Fontaine sign"); minimal collaterals; multiple occlusions [3]Diffuse atherosclerosis; irregular cutoff; well-developed collaterals
Skin/tissueNormal looking except at ischaemia levelChronic skin changes — hair loss, dystrophic nails, atrophy
Preferred treatmentSurgical embolectomy (Fogarty catheter)Thrombolysis or surgical bypass; embolectomy often not sufficient

In practice, many cases are difficult to distinguish clinically. When in doubt, treat as ALI and proceed to angiography or operative exploration — the intraoperative findings (sharp vs irregular cutoff, back bleeding quality) often clarify the cause.

4
Investigations

Do not delay revascularisation for investigations in Rutherford IIb or III

In a clearly threatened limb, anticoagulate and proceed to theatre. Investigations are done in parallel, not sequentially.

Bedside — immediate

  • Handheld Doppler both limbs — best initial test [1]; confirm absence of signal; compare bilateral
  • ECG — look for atrial fibrillation (embolic source)
  • Ankle-brachial index (ABI) if time allows — <0.4 suggests critical ischaemia

Blood tests

  • FBC, U&E, coagulation profile, group & save
  • Serum lactate — marker of ischaemia severity
  • CK/myoglobin — rhabdomyolysis risk post-revascularisation; baseline renal function
  • Thrombophilia screen — if patient <50 years with no identifiable cause; send before heparin
  • Cardiac enzymes and ECG — if embolic source suspected

Imaging

  • Duplex ultrasound: confirms occlusion level and flow; quick bedside tool
  • CT angiography: gold standard for anatomy; identifies occlusion site, collaterals, atherosclerotic burden; guides operative or endovascular approach. Contrast safety applies — hydration, renal function, allergy check
  • Conventional angiography: in theatre, as part of operative planning or intervention
  • Echocardiography: to identify cardiac embolic source (thrombus, valvular disease) — arrange post-stabilisation
5
Initial Treatment — All ALI
Call senior staff and vascular surgery immediatelyALI is always a senior-led case. Do not manage alone. Notify theatre early.
AnalgesiaIV opioid analgesia — do not withhold. Pain is severe and management of pain is humanitarian and reduces physiological stress.
Anticoagulation — start immediatelyIV unfractionated heparin (UFH): 80–100 units/kg bolus then 18 units/kg/hour infusion, adjusted to maintain aPTT 1.5–2.5× normal [5]. Start as soon as ALI is suspected and before imaging where possible. Prevents propagation of thrombus and protects collaterals. Do not delay for coagulation results unless haemorrhagic risk is high.
Nil by mouthIn anticipation of surgery.
IV access and fluid resuscitationLarge bore IV access; IV fluids — maintain urine output ≥0.5 ml/kg/hour; aggressive hydration protects kidneys from myoglobinuria post-revascularisation.
Urinary catheterStrict fluid balance; watch for myoglobinuria (tea-coloured urine) as early sign of reperfusion injury.
Limb positionPosition the affected limb at heart level or slightly dependent (not elevated) — elevation reduces perfusion pressure; heat lamps are contraindicated as they increase metabolic demand in ischaemic tissue.

Heparin does not lyse clot — it prevents extension and protects collateral flow [1,5] while revascularisation is prepared. It is not a substitute for revascularisation.

6
Revascularisation — Options by Rutherford Class
Rutherford classPreferred approachNotes
Class I — viableAnticoagulation; urgent imaging; plan definitive revascularisationTime allows angiography and planning; thrombolysis or elective surgical bypass depending on anatomy
Class IIa — marginally threatenedCatheter-directed thrombolysis (CDT) if embolic/thrombotic and symptom onset <14 days; surgical if CDT unavailableThrombolysis often used first-line; requires ICU monitoring; takes 12–24 hours; avoid if time-critical
Class IIb — immediately threatenedEmergency surgical revascularisation — embolectomy (embolic) or bypass/thrombectomy (thrombotic)Thrombolysis too slow for IIb; surgical embolectomy under local or GA; Fogarty balloon catheter technique
Class III — irreversibleAmputation or palliationRevascularisation of irreversible limb = fatal reperfusion; senior decision required immediately

Surgical embolectomy — key principles

  • Fogarty balloon catheter through groin arteriotomy [1,3] (common femoral artery) — most common approach for lower limb embolism
  • Pass catheter both proximally and distally to retrieve clot; repeat until good back-bleeding and forward flow confirmed
  • On-table angiogram after embolectomy to confirm adequate revascularisation and identify residual clot
  • Fasciotomy — must be considered after prolonged ischaemia (>4–6 hours) [6] to prevent compartment syndrome on reperfusion
  • Continue anticoagulation post-operatively; identify and treat embolic source (e.g. rate control + anticoagulation for AF)

In our resource-limited setting where endovascular capability may not be available, open surgical embolectomy is the primary revascularisation tool and is well-supported by evidence for embolic ALI. Thrombolysis requires catheter-directed delivery and ICU monitoring — factor in local availability before planning.

7
Acute-on-Chronic Limb Ischaemia

This is the most common presentation of ALI in a general surgical setting — acute thrombosis superimposed on background peripheral arterial disease. Typically older patients with known PAD, diabetes, smoking history, presenting with sudden worsening of a limb that was already compromised.

Key differences from pure ALI

  • Presentation is often less dramatic — pre-existing collaterals provide some residual flow and may partially compensate
  • The limb may not appear as acutely ischaemic as pure embolic ALI — do not be falsely reassured
  • Embolectomy alone is usually insufficient — the underlying atherosclerosis is not removed by a Fogarty catheter; surgical bypass or angioplasty/stenting addresses the disease
  • Thrombolysis is often the first-line where the limb is viable/marginally threatened (Class I–IIa) — it can lyse the acute thrombus and reveal the underlying arterial anatomy
  • Operative mortality and morbidity are higher than pure embolic ALI — these patients have significant comorbidity

Management approach

  • Rutherford classification applies equally — grade the limb and manage by class
  • Anticoagulate immediately with IV heparin
  • CT angiography to map the arterial tree and plan the revascularisation approach
  • Class I–IIa: catheter-directed thrombolysis to lyse acute thrombus; then angioplasty/stenting of underlying stenosis
  • Class IIb: emergency surgical bypass or hybrid approach; embolectomy alone unlikely to be sufficient
  • Class III: amputation
  • Optimise all cardiovascular risk factors post-revascularisation — antiplatelet therapy, statin, blood pressure, diabetes management
8
Complications to Anticipate

Reperfusion injury — anticipate in all revascularised limbs

On restoration of blood flow, ischaemic muscle releases myoglobin, potassium, and metabolic acids. This causes: hyperkalaemia (cardiac arrhythmia risk), metabolic acidosis, acute kidney injury (myoglobin precipitation), systemic inflammatory response. Monitor closely post-revascularisation.

Post-revascularisation monitoring

  • Monitor urine output hourly — target ≥0.5 ml/kg/hr; tea-coloured urine = myoglobinuria → aggressive IV hydration
  • ECG monitoring — hyperkalaemia may cause arrhythmia; treat per electrolyte protocol
  • Repeat U&E, creatinine, CK/myoglobin at 4 and 12 hours post-revascularisation
  • Alkalinise urine if myoglobinuria confirmed — sodium bicarbonate IV (senior decision)
  • Assess limb hourly — improving warmth, colour, return of Doppler signal, pain resolution

Compartment syndrome

A serious and preventable complication after revascularisation of a significantly ischaemic limb. Reperfusion oedema raises intracompartmental pressure → secondary ischaemia. Signs: severe pain on passive stretch, tense swollen compartments, paraesthesia, weakness. Prophylactic or therapeutic 4-compartment fasciotomy is indicated after prolonged ischaemia (>4–6 hours) [6] — senior decision; do not wait for signs to be obvious.

  • Overall mortality of ALI is approximately 20% at 30 days [1,3] — largely from the underlying cardiac disease that caused the embolism
  • Amputation rate remains 10–30% even after revascularisation [1,4] — realistic prognosis discussion with patient and family is essential
9
Escalation Triggers

Call senior staff immediately — all ALI is senior-led

  • Any suspected acute limb ischaemia — do not manage alone
  • Motor deficit or paralysis — Rutherford IIb or III; emergency theatre
  • Non-blanching mottling + woody muscles — Rutherford III; do not revascularise; amputation decision
  • Haemodynamic instability — concurrent cardiac event or massive embolism
  • Post-revascularisation deterioration — worsening ischaemia, loss of Doppler signal, rising compartment pressure
  • Tea-coloured urine / rising creatinine post-revascularisation — myoglobinuria; aggressive management
  • Hyperkalaemia or arrhythmia post-revascularisation — reperfusion injury
§

Ward Topic · Perioperative

Acute Hypertension

Perioperative BP control · emergency vs asymptomatic · bridge to the internist
Core Message
Classification
Emergency vs Asymptomatic
Reversible Causes
Hypertensive Emergency
Pre-operative
Post-operative
Agents
Medications

References
⚑ Core Principle In the surgical setting our role is short-term perioperative blood pressure control — not long-term management. The single most important decision is distinguishing a hypertensive emergency (end-organ damage — treat) from asymptomatic elevated BP (where aggressive acute treatment causes harm). Find and fix the reversible surgical drivers first, then hand long-term control to the internist.
★
The Surgical Context

Hypertension is common on the surgical ward — patients admitted for other reasons, off their usual medications, in pain, anxious, or NPO. Many are undiagnosed. Our job is to control blood pressure safely through the perioperative period and arrange definitive long-term management.

Firm C approach in three principles

  • Don't reflexively treat a number. Asymptomatic elevated BP rarely needs acute drug treatment — and aggressive treatment can harm
  • Find the reversible cause first — pain, NPO/missed medications, anxiety, urinary retention are the usual surgical culprits
  • Bridge, don't manage long-term. Achieve safe perioperative control, then refer to the internist for long-term control and discharge medications
1
Classifying Blood Pressure — The Two Scales

Two separate questions, two separate sets of numbers. Confusing them is the commonest source of error. Decide which question you are answering first.

Scale A — Is this person chronically hypertensive? (diagnosis & discharge planning)

This is about labelling and long-term treatment — relevant when optimising a patient for discharge. Note the two guideline systems differ, and both are in current use:

CategoryACC/AHA (US, 2017→)ESH (Europe, 2023)
Normal<120/80<130/85
Elevated / High-normal120–129 / <80130–139 / 85–89
Stage 1 / Grade 1130–139 or 80–89140–159 or 90–99
Stage 2 / Grade 2≥140 or ≥90160–179 or 100–109
Grade 3 (ESH)— (within Stage 2)≥180 or ≥110

The threshold to diagnose hypertension is ≥130/80 (US) or ≥140/90 (Europe). Diagnosis requires an average of ≥2 readings on ≥2 occasions, not a single ward reading.[8]

Scale B — Is this an acute crisis right now? (the surgical/acute domain)

This is a different question with different numbers — and it is the one that drives acute ward decisions.

TermDefinitionAction
Markedly elevated BP
(was "hypertensive urgency" — term abandoned)		SBP/DBP >180/110–120 WITHOUT target-organ damageDo not drop acutely with IV agents. Find reversible causes; restart/optimise oral meds; arrange follow-up[1]
Hypertensive emergencyUsually >180/110–120 WITH new/worsening target-organ damageImmediate controlled IV reduction, HDU/ICU — see Section 4[1]

The 180/110–120 figure is an acute crisis threshold — not the threshold for diagnosing chronic hypertension. The deciding factor between "markedly elevated" and "emergency" is the presence of target-organ damage, not the number itself.[1]

How this applies to surgical discharge — known/chronic hypertensives

For a patient who is a known hypertensive prior to admission or surgery, or who meets the chronic hypertension definitions above, follow this sequence:

  • First — re-establish their usual medication. Confirm they are actually receiving their regular antihypertensives. Poor ward control is very often simply missed doses (NPO, admitted without their medication, or omitted on the drug chart) rather than true treatment failure. Restart what they normally take and reassess the response.
  • Then — if not responding favourably to their re-established usual medication, discuss with the specialist / internist for long-term control before discharge. Do not send a chronic hypertensive home poorly controlled, and do not start an entirely new regimen yourself without internist input — the role here is to confirm and restart usual therapy, and escalate if that is insufficient.

A true hypertensive crisis (Scale B, with target-organ damage) is managed before discharge and is never sent home uncontrolled. The aim before handing over to the internist is safe, reasonable control on the patient's usual therapy — not the initiation of new long-term treatment on the surgical ward.

🔑 Does the patient need their BP controlled BEFORE discharge? The dividing line is organ damage, NOT the grade

A common and important question: must a patient have a near-normal BP before going home, or can they be discharged on the internist's plan? The answer is governed by target-organ damage / symptoms — not by which grade they sit at on Scale A:

  • No absolute normal BP is required before discharge for any grade. Grades 1, 2, and 3 do not need to be normalised on the ward — once referred, reversible causes addressed, and usual medication restarted, they can be discharged on the internist's plan with close follow-up. The AHA explicitly favours restarting home medication and arranging close outpatient follow-up over inpatient normalisation of asymptomatic elevated BP, and advises a high threshold for initiating or intensifying therapy[1]
  • Asymptomatic severe hypertension (grade 3, ≥180/110) without organ damage does NOT require acute intervention. The evidence is clear: acutely lowering asymptomatic severe BP shows no short-term benefit and is associated with increased cardiovascular events, acute kidney injury, longer stay, and mortality. Manage it like grades 1–2 — restart/optimise oral medication, refer, follow up[1]
  • The patients who MUST be controlled before discharge are those with a hypertensive emergency — severe BP with new/worsening target-organ damage, or any symptomatic crisis. These are never sent home uncontrolled (see Section 4). Note: organ damage can occur even below 180/110, so the symptom/organ-damage assessment — not the number — is what decides[1]

The clean rule: asymptomatic, no organ damage → all grades dischargeable on the internist's plan (no acute treatment needed); symptomatic or organ damage → intervene and control before discharge, regardless of the number.

2
The Critical Distinction

The 2024 AHA scientific statement reframed how we think about elevated inpatient BP. The old term "hypertensive urgency" has been abandoned — what matters is whether there is target-organ damage, not the number alone.[1]

Hypertensive EmergencyAsymptomatic Elevated BP
DefinitionSBP/DBP usually >180/110–120 WITH new/worsening target-organ damageElevated BP (even markedly, >180/120) WITHOUT evidence of target-organ damage
TreatmentImmediate, controlled IV reduction — usually ICU/HDUDo not acutely treat with IV agents. Identify reversible causes; restart oral medications; close monitoring
EvidenceClear benefit from prompt controlled loweringNo evidence treatment improves outcomes; observational data show harm from acute treatment

🚨 Aggressive treatment of asymptomatic elevated BP causes harm

Observational studies show that acutely treating asymptomatic inpatient hypertension — especially with IV medications — is associated with hypotension, rapid BP lowering, myocardial injury, kidney injury, and death.[1,2] In the absence of a hypertensive emergency, IV antihypertensives are not recommended. The AHA advises a high threshold for initiating or intensifying antihypertensive therapy in asymptomatic patients.[1]

Target-organ damage — what to look for

  • Neurological: headache, confusion, visual disturbance, focal deficit, seizure (encephalopathy, stroke)
  • Cardiac: chest pain, dyspnoea (ACS, acute heart failure, pulmonary oedema)
  • Renal: rising creatinine, oliguria, haematuria (acute kidney injury)
  • Vascular: tearing chest/back pain (aortic dissection)
  • Eyes: new retinal haemorrhages, papilloedema
  • Obstetric: pre-eclampsia/eclampsia in the pregnant patient
3
Find Reversible Causes First

Before reaching for an antihypertensive in an asymptomatic patient, work through the reversible surgical drivers — treating these often resolves the BP without any antihypertensive at all.

DriverAction
PainAssess and treat pain adequately — undertreated post-op pain is the commonest cause of ward hypertension
Missed usual medicationsHas the patient missed their regular antihypertensives because they were NPO or admitted without them? Restart as soon as safe — this is frequently the whole problem
Urinary retentionExamine for a palpable bladder; a full bladder drives marked hypertension. Catheterise/relieve and recheck
Anxiety / white-coat / sleep deprivationReassure; recheck when settled; consider environment
Measurement errorWrong cuff size, wrong position, cuff over clothing — re-measure correctly with an appropriately sized cuff, arm supported at heart level, after rest
OtherHypoxia, hypercapnia, fluid overload, medications (steroids, vasopressors), alcohol withdrawal, illicit substances

Repeat the measurement before acting. A single elevated reading — particularly in a patient who is in pain, anxious, or has a full bladder — is not an indication to start drug treatment. Re-measure correctly after addressing reversible factors.[1]

4
Hypertensive Emergency — Management

This is a true emergency — call senior staff and the medical team immediately

Markedly elevated BP with new or worsening target-organ damage requires immediate, controlled IV reduction, usually in an HDU/ICU setting with continuous monitoring.

Principles of controlled reduction

  • Do not lower BP too fast. Aim to reduce mean arterial pressure by no more than ~25% in the first hour, then to ~160/100–110 over the next 2–6 hours, then cautiously to normal over 24–48 hours
  • Over-rapid reduction risks cerebral, coronary, and renal hypoperfusion — watch for ischaemia
  • Use a titratable IV agent with continuous BP monitoring — not bolus PRN dosing
  • Exceptions with specific targets: aortic dissection (rapid reduction to SBP <120 and HR <60), and certain neurological emergencies — these are specialist-led and have their own targets

Identify the specific emergency

The agent and target depend on the type of end-organ damage — acute heart failure, ACS, stroke, dissection, and eclampsia each have specific approaches. This is a senior- and specialist-led decision. The junior role is recognition, escalation, and supportive care while the team is mobilised.

IV titration — agents & doses

Use a single titratable IV agent with continuous BP monitoring (ideally HDU/ICU). Titrate to the staged targets above, not to "normal". Choice is senior/specialist-led and depends on the specific emergency.

AgentDose & titrationOnset / durationNotes
Labetalol
(alpha/beta blocker)		20 mg IV bolus, then double every 10 min as needed (max ~300 mg); OR infusion 0.5–2 mg/min5–10 min / 3–6 hWorkhorse, widely available. Avoid in asthma, bradycardia, decompensated heart failure, heart block[6]
GTN (nitroglycerin)
infusion		5–200 mcg/min IV, titrated1–5 min / shortPreferred where ACS or pulmonary oedema coexists; venodilator[6]
Esmolol
(ultra-short beta blocker)		500 mcg/kg bolus, then 50–300 mcg/kg/min infusion1–2 min / 10–30 minVery titratable; useful in dissection (rate + pressure control) and intra-operative spikes[6]
Hydralazine5–10 mg IV, repeat every 4–6 h as needed10–30 min / 2–6 hUse with caution / generally not first-line. Unpredictable, sometimes precipitous and prolonged drops; reflex tachycardia — avoid in ischaemic heart disease and aortic dissection. Still widely used in obstetric (pre-eclampsia) practice[5,6]
Nicardipine
(if available)		5 mg/h IV infusion, increase by 2.5 mg/h every 5–15 min (max 15 mg/h)5–15 min / 4–6 hHighly titratable; reaches target faster than labetalol in trials. Often unavailable in our setting[6]

⚠ Agent cautions

  • Hydralazine: its onset can be delayed (10–30 min) and effect prolonged/unpredictable — do not redose prematurely chasing the BP, or you may cause a precipitous fall. Avoid in IHD and dissection (reflex tachycardia)[5,6]
  • Sodium nitroprusside: effective but cyanide toxicity risk — reserve for refractory cases under specialist/ICU control only
  • Never sublingual nifedipine (Section 7) — uncontrolled drops, stroke, MI, death[4]
  • Use one titratable agent and adjust it — do not stack multiple PRN boluses of different drugs, which causes unpredictable cumulative hypotension

Practical Firm C reality: where infusion pumps or nicardipine are unavailable, IV labetalol (bolus or infusion) is the most practical titratable agent, with GTN infusion where there is coexisting cardiac ischaemia or pulmonary oedema. Manage in the highest-monitoring environment available and involve the medical team early.

5
Pre-operative Pathway

Elective surgery — optimise before operating

  • Refer to the internist for blood pressure optimisation before elective surgery — once you have ascertained that the patient is compliant with their medication (poor control is often simply non-compliance)
  • It is not recommended to proceed with elective surgery on patients who have uncontrolled blood pressure
  • Allow time for optimisation; reschedule the elective case if needed

Urgent / emergency surgery — proceed by urgency

  • Refer to the internist, but the case may proceed depending on the urgency — a life- or limb-threatening emergency is not delayed for blood pressure optimisation
  • Inform anaesthetics of the BP and any end-organ concerns; they will guide intraoperative management
  • Exclude a hypertensive emergency (target-organ damage) — that changes the picture and requires control before anaesthesia where possible

What's an acceptable pre-op BP?

  • There is no universally agreed cut-off. Most guidance supports proceeding with elective surgery where BP is <180/110 in the absence of end-organ damage
  • Markedly elevated BP (≥180/110) without end-organ damage is not itself a hypertensive emergency, but for elective cases it warrants optimisation first
  • The decision to proceed is ultimately senior surgeon + anaesthetist, case by case
6
Post-operative Pathway

Post-operative hypertension is common and usually multifactorial. The approach is the same: rule out a hypertensive emergency, then treat reversible causes, then restart medications.

Step 1 — Exclude a hypertensive emergencyAny target-organ symptoms or signs? If yes → Section 4, call senior staff and medical team.
Step 2 — Treat reversible causesPain, urinary retention (full bladder), hypoxia, anxiety, fluid overload. Re-measure correctly. Often resolves without any antihypertensive.
Step 3 — Restart usual medicationsOnce tolerating oral intake, restart the patient's home antihypertensives — consider starting at a slightly lower dose for 1–2 days to ease the transition and avoid hypotension.[3] If still NPO and treatment is genuinely needed, use an appropriate agent (Section 7).
Step 4 — Persistent or newly-recognised hypertensionIf BP remains elevated despite the above, or the patient is newly found to be hypertensive, refer to the internist for assessment, long-term control, and discharge medications. Document the referral.

A patient who was well controlled at home and is hypertensive post-op is usually in pain, in retention, or simply hasn't had their morning dose — not in need of a new antihypertensive regimen. Find the cause.

🏠 Discharge when the home medication is unavailable in pharmacy

A common practical problem on our ward: the patient is surgically ready for discharge but has an asymptomatic high BP, and the pharmacy does not have their usual home medication — which can delay the discharge. A safe plan that does not require holding the bed:

  • Confirm it is asymptomatic with no target-organ damage first (quick symptom check — headache, visual change, chest pain, breathlessness, focal neurology). If any organ-damage feature is present, that is a hypertensive emergency and the patient stays (see Section 4). If asymptomatic, the high number alone is not a reason to prolong admission
  • If the home medication is available — restart it, discharge, refer to internist for follow-up
  • If the home medication is NOT available — do not wait for it. Discharge on an effective alternative from our formulary that gives reasonable single-agent cover. Amlodipine 5 mg once daily (max 10 mg) is a pragmatic choice — long-acting (half-life 30–50 h, true once-daily), no monitoring needed, no dose adjustment in renal impairment, and an established first-line agent[9]
  • Give a prescription the patient can fill at a community pharmacy for their actual home medication where the hospital pharmacy cannot supply it, so they are covered from the day they leave
  • Anchor with a defined internist follow-up — a date or clear referral pathway, not an open-ended "see the internist sometime." The internist reconciles the bridge agent with the patient's usual regimen and titrates for long-term control
  • Document in the discharge letter: the BP, that it was asymptomatic with no organ damage, what you started and why, and the follow-up arranged

The goal is effective bridge cover plus a clear handover — not perfect long-term drug selection, which is the internist's role. Substituting an available formulary agent solves the pharmacy gap without holding a surgically-ready patient.

7
Agents & Practical Use

⚠ Never use sublingual nifedipine

Sublingual (or bitten-capsule) nifedipine causes uncontrolled, precipitous BP drops and is associated with stroke, myocardial infarction, and death.[4] It must not be used. This is a common and dangerous error.

Oral — preferred when tolerating oral intake

  • Restart the patient's own usual medications first — the simplest and safest option
  • Consider a slightly reduced dose for the first 1–2 days post-op to avoid hypotension, then resume full dose[3]
  • Common oral agents (per local availability and internist guidance): amlodipine, an ACE inhibitor or ARB, a beta-blocker, or a thiazide — note amlodipine has a slow onset and is not for acute control[5]

IV — only when NPO AND treatment is genuinely indicated (emergency, or persistent significant hypertension not manageable orally)

AgentNotes
LabetalolThe workhorse IV agent — combined alpha/beta blockade, relatively fast onset, titratable. Avoid in asthma, significant bradycardia, decompensated heart failure[1,6]
HydralazineIV, intermittent dosing (e.g. 5–10 mg). Caution in ischaemic heart disease — reflex tachycardia can provoke ischaemia. Onset can be delayed (up to 20–25 min) — do not redose prematurely[5,6]
GTN (nitroglycerin) infusionUseful where pulmonary oedema or cardiac ischaemia coexists; titratable infusion
EsmololUltra-short-acting beta-blocker; useful where rapid, brief control is needed (e.g. intraoperative)[6]

IV agents in the ward setting are for hypertensive emergencies or genuinely problematic persistent hypertension in the NPO patient — not for asymptomatic elevated readings. When in doubt, treat the cause and restart oral medication rather than reaching for IV.

8
Perioperative Medication Management

How to handle the patient's existing antihypertensive classes around surgery (per the 2024 ACC/AHA perioperative guideline)[7]:

ClassPerioperative handling
Beta-blockersContinue throughout — do not stop abruptly. Acute withdrawal risks rebound tachycardia, ischaemia, and is associated with morbidity/mortality[7]
ACE inhibitors / ARBsIf taken for hypertension and BP controlled, withholding 24 h before surgery may limit intraoperative hypotension. Continue if taken for heart failure with reduced EF. Restart post-op once euvolaemic and stable[7]
ClonidineContinue if already taking — abrupt cessation causes rebound hypertension. Do not start de novo before surgery[7]
Calcium channel blockersGenerally continued perioperatively
DiureticsOften withheld on the morning of surgery (volume/electrolyte effects); restart post-op per fluid status
SGLT2 inhibitors (if also diabetic)Withhold 3–4 days before surgery — euglycaemic ketoacidosis risk (cross-reference diabetic protocols)[7]

When restarting oral antihypertensives post-op, reduced initial dosing for 1–2 days provides a safer transition with less risk of hypotension or bradycardia.[3]

9
Escalation & Internist Referral

Call senior staff / medical team immediately

  • Any sign of a hypertensive emergency (target-organ damage) — this is an emergency
  • Severely elevated BP not responding to treatment of reversible causes and oral medication
  • Pregnant patient with elevated BP — pre-eclampsia/eclampsia pathway
  • Aortic dissection suspected — tearing chest/back pain — emergency

Refer to the internist for

  • Newly recognised hypertension confirmed on repeated correct measurement
  • Established hypertension that is poorly controlled despite restarting usual medications
  • Long-term blood pressure management and choice of discharge medications

Our perioperative role is to control BP safely through the surgical episode and to recognise and escalate emergencies — the internist owns long-term control and the discharge antihypertensive plan. Document the referral and the perioperative measures taken.

§

Ward Topic · Venous Thromboembolism

DVT — Suspected & Confirmed

Index of suspicion · Wells score · Doppler · anticoagulation · surgical context
