IVC Filter
Escalation
References
⚑ Core Principle Surgical inpatients are high-risk for DVT — surgery, immobility, and the inflammatory state all drive it. The index of suspicion should be low. The standard diagnostic pathway uses the Wells score with D-dimer and compression ultrasound — but note that in our setting D-dimer is not readily available and is unhelpful post-operatively anyway, so compression ultrasound is the practical confirmatory test. Balance the need to anticoagulate against post-operative bleeding risk, and involve senior staff in that decision.
0
When to Suspect — Risk Factors

DVT risk is the sum of background (patient) factors and acute (surgical/hospital) factors. The surgical inpatient frequently has several at once.

Surgical / acute factorsPatient / background factors
Recent major surgery (especially pelvic, abdominal, orthopaedic)Previous VTE — strongest single risk factor
Immobility / bed rest ≥3 daysActive malignancy
Lower limb plaster/immobilisationObesity (BMI >30)
Trauma, especially lower limb/pelvisAge >60
Central venous catheterThrombophilia (inherited or acquired)
Sepsis, dehydrationPregnancy/puerperium; oestrogen therapy (COCP/HRT)

A swollen, painful calf in a post-operative patient is a DVT until proven otherwise. Do not attribute leg symptoms to "just post-op" without considering DVT.

1
Clinical Features
  • Unilateral limb swelling — the most useful sign; measure and compare calf circumference (≥3 cm difference 10 cm below tibial tuberosity is significant)
  • Pain and tenderness — typically along the deep venous system (calf, popliteal, thigh)
  • Warmth and erythema of the affected limb
  • Pitting oedema confined to the symptomatic leg
  • Dilated non-varicose superficial collateral veins
  • Limb may be tense; in extensive iliofemoral DVT, a swollen blue (phlegmasia cerulea dolens) or white (phlegmasia alba dolens) leg is a limb-threatening emergency

Mimics to consider

Cellulitis, ruptured Baker's cyst, calf muscle tear/haematoma, superficial thrombophlebitis, chronic venous insufficiency, lymphoedema, and post-operative swelling. Bilateral leg swelling more often reflects a systemic cause (cardiac, renal, hepatic) than DVT.

2
Two-Level Wells Score for DVT
Clinical featurePoints
Active cancer (treatment ongoing, within 6 months, or palliative)+1
Paralysis, paresis, or recent plaster immobilisation of the leg+1
Recently bedridden ≥3 days, or major surgery within 12 weeks+1
Localised tenderness along the deep venous system+1
Entire leg swollen+1
Calf swelling ≥3 cm vs asymptomatic side+1
Pitting oedema confined to the symptomatic leg+1
Collateral superficial (non-varicose) veins+1
Previously documented DVT+1
Alternative diagnosis at least as likely as DVT−2

Interpretation (2-level)

  • ≥2 points — DVT "likely" → proceed to ultrasound
  • ≤1 point — DVT "unlikely" → D-dimer first (in suitable patients — see surgical caveat)

The Wells score was developed and validated in outpatients. Its accuracy is reduced in hospital inpatients, pregnancy, recurrent DVT, and patients already anticoagulated.[2]

3
Investigation Pathway

Compression ultrasound — the diagnostic test

  • Proximal leg vein compression ultrasound is the gold standard — non-compressibility of the vein is the key sign of thrombus[1,4]
  • Ensure the request explicitly covers the thigh (proximal) veins, not just the calf. "Proximal" DVT means the popliteal vein and above — femoral and iliofemoral. These proximal/thigh thromboses are the most dangerous, carrying the highest risk of embolising to cause PE; distal (calf) DVTs are lower-risk. When ordering, specify whole-leg or proximal assessment so the thigh segments are scanned
  • Request as "ultrasound for possible DVT" so the correct technique is used
  • If "DVT likely" (Wells ≥2): obtain ultrasound, ideally within 4 hours; if not available within 4 hours, give an interim therapeutic dose of anticoagulant and scan within 24 hours[1]
  • If proximal scan negative but clinical suspicion persists: repeat scan in 1 week (a small distal clot may propagate proximally), or perform whole-leg scanning[1]

D-dimer — understanding the test (educational)

D-dimer is a fibrin degradation product — it rises whenever clot is being formed and broken down. It is a rule-out test, valued for its high sensitivity and negative predictive value, not for confirming a clot.

  • How it is used in the standard pathway: in a patient assessed as "DVT unlikely" on the 2-level Wells score, a negative high-sensitivity D-dimer safely excludes DVT without the need for imaging[1,2]
  • Why a positive result means little: D-dimer is non-specific — it is also raised by surgery, trauma, infection, malignancy, pregnancy, and increasing age. A positive result does not confirm DVT; it only means imaging is needed
  • Age-adjusted cut-offs (age × 10 µg/L over age 50) improve specificity in older patients

Local availability — important practical caveat

In our setting, D-dimer is not readily available — it is generally a send-out to private laboratories with a turnaround often exceeding 24 hours, which is far too slow for an acute diagnosis. Combined with the fact that it is almost always elevated post-operatively (and so unhelpful in the surgical inpatient), this means D-dimer does not feature in our practical pathway. Compression ultrasound is the confirmatory test we rely on (see Section 4). Knowing how D-dimer works remains important — it is central to the diagnostic pathway in settings where it is rapidly available.

4
The Surgical Inpatient — Key Caveats

D-dimer in our setting — not readily available, and unhelpful post-op anyway

Two separate points reinforce the same conclusion:

  • Availability: D-dimer is not readily available at CRH — it is usually a send-out to private laboratories and the result often takes more than 24 hours. For an acute diagnosis that is far too slow to be useful.
  • Validity: even where available, D-dimer is almost always elevated after surgery, trauma, sepsis, or any inflammatory state, so a positive result tells you nothing in the post-operative inpatient. Risk stratification with D-dimer has not been validated in inpatients.[1]

Practical Firm C approach: do not wait on a D-dimer. Go straight to compression ultrasound — it is the confirmatory test in our setting.

Balancing anticoagulation against post-operative bleeding

Therapeutic anticoagulation in the early post-operative period carries real bleeding risk. The decision to start, delay, or withhold treatment — and at what dose — must weigh the confirmed/suspected DVT against the surgical bleeding risk. This is a senior/surgical decision, not an automatic protocol step.

  • If anticoagulation is too hazardous (active bleeding, very recent major surgery), consider mechanical measures and discuss the role of a temporary IVC filter with senior staff
  • If interim anticoagulation is needed while awaiting imaging, a short-acting agent (treatment-dose LMWH, or unfractionated heparin infusion — more readily available in our setting) is preferred over a DOAC because it can be stopped or reversed more readily if surgery or bleeding intervenes
5
Treatment — Anticoagulation

Once DVT is confirmed (or treatment started empirically while awaiting imaging), anticoagulation is the mainstay. Choice depends on bleeding risk, renal function, malignancy, and the surgical context.[6]

📋 Empirical vs confirmation-based treatment — where the evidence lies

A common and important question: do you start anticoagulation only once the scan confirms DVT, or empirically on clinical suspicion? The guidelines are clear and consistent:

  • The governing principle: anticoagulation that is effective for confirmed DVT is equally effective started empirically — so a diagnostic delay should not leave a probable clot untreated[1]
  • NICE: if DVT is "likely" (Wells ≥2) and the scan cannot be done within 4 hours, start interim therapeutic anticoagulation and scan within 24 hours[1]
  • CHEST: goes further — initiate anticoagulation in patients in whom DVT is suspected with high probability; investigations should not delay empirical treatment. Anticoagulation reduces VTE mortality substantially, and the risk of a short delay outweighs the bleeding risk of a few interim doses in most patients[3]

Practical rule: high clinical probability + any anticipated delay to imaging → treat empirically (provided bleeding risk is acceptable). Low clinical probability → wait for confirmation.

⚑ The surgical modifier

The empirical-treatment rule assumes an average bleeding risk. In the recent post-operative patient that assumption may not hold. The decision to anticoagulate empirically must be weighed against post-operative bleeding risk — a senior/surgical judgement. Where bleeding risk is high, a short-acting agent (UFH/LMWH) that can be stopped or reversed is preferred for any interim treatment, and the threshold to wait for confirmation (or to image urgently) is lower.

AgentRegimenNotes
Apixaban (DOAC)10 mg BD for 7 days, then 5 mg BDNo parenteral lead-in needed; lower GI bleeding than rivaroxaban — often preferred[5,6]
Rivaroxaban (DOAC)15 mg BD for 21 days, then 20 mg ODNo parenteral lead-in needed; take with food[5,6]
LMWH (e.g. enoxaparin)Treatment dose ~1.5 mg/kg OD or 1 mg/kg BDPreferred peri-operatively (short-acting, more controllable); first-line in pregnancy; bridge to warfarin if DOAC unsuitable[6]
Unfractionated heparin (UFH) IV infusion80 units/kg IV bolus, then 18 units/kg/hour infusion, titrated to aPTT 1.5–2.5× control (check aPTT at 6 h)More readily available in our setting than LMWH. Short half-life and reversible with protamine — useful peri-operatively and in severe renal impairment. Requires aPTT monitoring; monitor platelets for HIT[6]
Edoxaban / dabigatranRequire ≥5 days LMWH lead-in firstLess convenient than apixaban/rivaroxaban in the acute setting[6]
Warfarin (VKA)Target INR 2–3; needs LMWH/UFH bridge until INR therapeuticWhere DOAC unavailable/unsuitable; requires monitoring[6]

Special situations

  • Cancer-associated DVT: a DOAC or LMWH; caution with rivaroxaban/edoxaban in luminal GI malignancy (higher GI bleeding) — apixaban or LMWH preferred[6]
  • Severe renal impairment: DOACs may be contraindicated — LMWH (dose-adjusted) or UFH
  • Massive iliofemoral DVT / phlegmasia: limb-threatening — urgent senior/vascular referral; may need thrombolysis or thrombectomy

Compression stockings are no longer recommended to prevent post-thrombotic syndrome but may help symptomatically.[6]

⚠ Heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT) — recognise it

HIT is an immune reaction to heparin (UFH more than LMWH) in which anti-PF4/heparin antibodies activate platelets. The trap: despite a falling platelet count, HIT is strongly PRO-thrombotic — patients clot, they do not bleed. Do not transfuse platelets.

Recognise — the "4 Ts":

  • Thrombocytopenia — platelet fall, typically >50% from baseline
  • Timing — platelet fall 5–10 days after starting heparin (or faster if exposed within the last 3 months)
  • Thrombosis — new or extending VTE/arterial thrombosis, or skin lesions at injection sites
  • oTher cause absent — no other clear explanation for the low platelets

Score each 0–2 (max 8): 0–3 low, 4–5 intermediate, ≥6 high probability. A score of ≥4 warrants action.[8]

Manage:

  • Stop ALL heparin immediately — including LMWH, UFH infusions, and heparin line flushes[8]
  • Start a non-heparin anticoagulant at therapeutic intensity (do not just stop — the thrombotic risk persists). Options: argatroban, danaparoid, fondaparinux, or a DOAC, per availability and senior/haematology guidance[8]
  • Send HIT antibody testing (anti-PF4 immunoassay) — but do not wait for the result to act if probability is intermediate/high
  • Do not give platelet transfusions (thrombosis risk) and do not start warfarin until platelets recover (risk of venous limb gangrene/skin necrosis); bridge with the non-heparin agent
  • Flag the heparin allergy clearly; the patient should avoid heparin in future
6
Duration of Anticoagulation
ScenarioDuration
Provoked by surgery (transient major risk factor, now resolved)3 months[6]
Provoked by a persistent factor (immobility, obesity, IBD, heart failure)Consider extended anticoagulation — recurrence risk persists[5,6]
Unprovoked DVTAt least 3 months; extended therapy if bleeding risk low[6]
Active cancerContinue while cancer active (often indefinite)[6]

A DVT provoked by surgery is the classic transient-provoked event — 3 months is usually sufficient. The decision to extend is based on the balance of recurrence risk versus bleeding risk and is usually made at outpatient review.

7
IVC Filter — When (and When Not)

First, the key concept

An IVC filter is not a treatment for DVT. It does not dissolve clot or prevent new DVT — it is a mechanical barrier in the IVC that catches emboli before they reach the lungs. Anticoagulation remains the actual treatment. A filter only has a role where anticoagulation cannot do its job. Where used, a retrievable filter is preferred, removed as soon as the patient can safely be anticoagulated.[6,7]

Scenario 1 — DVT with anticoagulation contraindicated

Clear, agreed indication

A patient with acute proximal DVT (or PE) who has an absolute contraindication to anticoagulation — active major bleeding, or who must come off anticoagulation for urgent surgery — should have a (retrievable) IVC filter. This is the one universally agreed indication across CHEST, AHA, ASH, SIR, and ESC.[6,7] The historic Greenfield filter is the prototype of these devices. Retrieve the filter once anticoagulation can safely resume.

Scenario 2 — new/recurrent DVT despite therapeutic anticoagulation

A filter is NOT the first response — this is counterintuitive but important

The instinct is "anticoagulation failed → add a filter." Current evidence and guidelines advise against that as the first move:

  • First confirm the failure is real — was the patient actually therapeutic? Compliance, dose, timing?
  • Escalate or switch the anticoagulant — the preferred response to genuine anticoagulation failure is an alternative agent or dose escalation, not a filter[7]
  • Hunt for an underlying hypercoagulable driver — active malignancy, antiphospholipid syndrome, HIT, or an anatomical cause (e.g. May-Thurner syndrome). A filter does not treat the cause and can itself act as a thrombotic focus
  • The PREPIC and PREPIC2 trials showed that adding a filter to anticoagulation did not improve outcomes[7]

Guidelines leave only a weak, selective second-line door open for filter use in recurrence-on-anticoagulation (e.g. AHA Class IIa/IIb) — after the steps above have been worked through. It is not a reflex response, and it is a senior/specialist decision.

Bottom line: can't anticoagulate → retrievable filter (yes); clot despite anticoagulation → filter is not the first move — verify true failure, switch/escalate anticoagulation, and look for a hypercoagulable cause first.

8
Escalation Triggers

Call senior staff

  • Suspected DVT with any chest symptoms — breathlessness, pleuritic pain, tachycardia → assess for PE (see PE protocol)
  • Phlegmasia (massively swollen, blue or white, painful leg) — limb-threatening emergency; urgent vascular referral
  • Confirmed/suspected DVT where anticoagulation is hazardous (recent major surgery, active bleeding) — senior decision on timing, dose, or IVC filter
  • DVT in a patient already on anticoagulation — treatment failure; needs review
  • Extensive or unusual-site thrombosis (e.g. upper limb, without catheter)

Blood Adv. 2018 Nov 27;2(22):3360–3392. doi:10.1182/bloodadvances.2018024489 (4Ts score; stop all heparin; non-heparin anticoagulant at therapeutic intensity; avoid platelet transfusion and warfarin until platelets recover)

Ward Topic · Venous Thromboembolism

Pulmonary Embolism

Suspected & confirmed · risk stratification · CTPA · treatment · surgical context
When to Suspect
Clinical Features
Wells Score
Investigation
Risk Stratification
Treatment
Surgical Caveat
Escalation
References
⚠ Potentially Life-Threatening PE ranges from a small, well-tolerated clot to a massive, immediately fatal one. The two priorities are: (1) recognise it — the presentation is non-specific and easily missed; and (2) risk-stratify by haemodynamic stability, because that determines whether the patient needs anticoagulation alone or emergency reperfusion. A haemodynamically unstable PE is a peri-arrest emergency — call for senior help immediately.
0
When to Suspect

Suspect PE in any post-operative or immobile patient with sudden breathlessness, pleuritic chest pain, unexplained tachycardia, hypoxia, or collapse. The risk factors are the same as for DVT (see DVT protocol) — surgery, immobility, malignancy, prior VTE, obesity, pregnancy, oestrogen therapy.

The classic surgical scenario

A patient several days post-operative who suddenly becomes breathless, tachycardic, or desaturates — or who collapses on first mobilisation — has had a PE until proven otherwise. New unexplained tachycardia alone in a post-op patient warrants consideration of PE.

1
Clinical Features
  • Dyspnoea — often sudden onset; the commonest symptom
  • Pleuritic chest pain — worse on inspiration
  • Tachycardia — may be the only sign; do not dismiss unexplained post-op tachycardia
  • Tachypnoea and hypoxia — falling SpO₂, rising respiratory rate
  • Haemoptysis
  • Syncope or pre-syncope — suggests a large, haemodynamically significant PE
  • Signs of a DVT — swollen, painful calf (the embolic source)
  • Haemodynamic instability — hypotension, signs of shock → high-risk PE (peri-arrest)

Baseline tests (support, do not diagnose)

  • ECG: sinus tachycardia is commonest; right heart strain (S1Q3T3, RBBB, T-wave inversion V1–V4) suggests a larger PE but is not sensitive
  • CXR: often normal — its main value is excluding other causes (pneumothorax, pneumonia, effusion)
  • ABG: may show hypoxia and a respiratory alkalosis; a normal ABG does not exclude PE
2
Two-Level Wells Score for PE
Clinical featurePoints
Clinical signs and symptoms of DVT+3
PE is the most likely diagnosis, or equally likely+3
Heart rate >100 bpm+1.5
Immobilisation ≥3 days or surgery within previous 4 weeks+1.5
Previous DVT/PE+1.5
Haemoptysis+1
Malignancy (treatment within 6 months or palliative)+1

Interpretation (2-level)

  • >4 points — PE "likely" → CTPA (give interim anticoagulation if any delay)
  • ≤4 points — PE "unlikely" → in settings with rapid D-dimer access, a negative D-dimer can exclude PE. In our setting D-dimer is not readily available (see Section 6), so clinical judgement and CTPA carry the diagnosis

In the low-risk outpatient, the PERC rule can be used to exclude PE without even a D-dimer. This rarely applies to the surgical inpatient, who almost always fails PERC on the surgery/immobility criteria.[3]

3
Investigation Pathway

D-dimer — understanding the test (educational)

As in DVT, D-dimer is a high-sensitivity rule-out test. In a patient assessed as "PE unlikely" on the 2-level Wells score, a negative D-dimer excludes PE without imaging. Age-adjusted cut-offs (age × 10 µg/L over age 50) improve specificity in older patients, and probability-adapted cut-offs (e.g. YEARS) are used in some pathways[3].

  • A positive D-dimer is non-specific — it only prompts imaging; it is raised by surgery, infection, malignancy, pregnancy, and age
  • Local caveat: D-dimer is not readily available in our setting and is unhelpful post-operatively (see Section 6) — so the practical pathway relies on clinical assessment and CTPA. Understanding D-dimer's role remains important for practice in settings where it is rapidly available

CT pulmonary angiography (CTPA) — the diagnostic test

  • CTPA is the gold standard — directly visualises the embolus and assesses clot burden and RV strain[1]
  • If "PE likely" (Wells >4): proceed to CTPA; if there is any delay, give interim therapeutic anticoagulation unless contraindicated[7]
  • Contrast safety applies — hydration, renal function, allergy

V/Q scan — alternative

  • Ventilation-perfusion scan where CTPA is contraindicated (contrast allergy, renal impairment) or to reduce radiation (e.g. younger patients, pregnancy)

Echocardiography — in the unstable patient

  • In a haemodynamically unstable patient too unwell for CTPA, bedside echo showing RV strain/dysfunction supports a presumptive diagnosis of high-risk PE and can justify emergency reperfusion[1]
4
Risk Stratification — Drives Management

This is the most important step after diagnosis. The single biggest determinant is haemodynamic stability.

CategoryDefining featuresApprox. mortalityManagement direction
High-risk (massive)Haemodynamic instability — hypotension (SBP <90) or shock25–50%Emergency reperfusion — thrombolysis / embolectomy
Intermediate-risk (submassive)Haemodynamically stable but RV dysfunction (echo/CTPA) and/or raised troponin; PESI class III–VIntermediateAnticoagulation + close monitoring; rescue reperfusion if deteriorates
Low-riskStable, no RV dysfunction, normal biomarkers, low PESI~1%Anticoagulation; consider early discharge/outpatient in selected cases

The PESI / simplified PESI score integrates PE severity with comorbidity and reliably identifies low-risk patients. RV dysfunction on imaging plus a raised troponin identifies the higher-risk intermediate group who need closer monitoring.[1,4]

5
Treatment

High-risk (haemodynamically unstable) PE — emergency

  • Call for senior help, resuscitate (oxygen, careful fluids, vasopressor support as needed) — this is a peri-arrest situation
  • Systemic thrombolysis (e.g. alteplase) is the reperfusion treatment of choice unless contraindicated[1]
  • Where thrombolysis is contraindicated (e.g. recent surgery — see Section 6) or fails: catheter-directed therapy or surgical embolectomy[1]

Stable PE (intermediate- and low-risk) — anticoagulation

📋 Empirical vs confirmation-based treatment — where the evidence lies

As with DVT, the guidelines support starting anticoagulation empirically on strong clinical suspicion rather than waiting for the scan — and for PE the time threshold is tighter because the stakes are higher:

  • NICE: if PE is "likely" (Wells >4) and CTPA cannot be done within 1 hour, start interim therapeutic anticoagulation immediately and image as soon as possible[7]
  • CHEST: initiate anticoagulation in patients with suspected PE and high probability; diagnostic investigations should not delay empirical treatment. Anticoagulation reduces PE mortality from ~30% to under 10%[8]
  • Governing principle: a probable PE should not sit untreated waiting for a scan — treat empirically if the scan is delayed, provided bleeding risk is acceptable[7,8]

Practical rule: high clinical probability + any delay to CTPA → treat empirically. Low probability → confirm first.

⚑ The surgical modifier

As with DVT, the empirical-treatment rule assumes average bleeding risk. In the recent post-operative patient, weigh empirical anticoagulation against post-operative bleeding risk — a senior/surgical decision. Prefer a short-acting, reversible agent (UFH/LMWH) for interim treatment, and image urgently rather than commit to prolonged empirical anticoagulation where bleeding risk is high.

Anticoagulation is the mainstay for stable PE, with the same agents and doses as for DVT (see DVT protocol, Treatment section):

  • Apixaban 10 mg BD for 7 days then 5 mg BD, or rivaroxaban 15 mg BD for 21 days then 20 mg OD — no parenteral lead-in[5,6]
  • LMWH (treatment dose) — preferred peri-operatively (short-acting, controllable) and first-line in pregnancy and cancer[6]
  • Unfractionated heparin (UFH) infusion — 80 units/kg bolus then 18 units/kg/hour, titrated to aPTT 1.5–2.5× control; more readily available in our setting than LMWH, short-acting and reversible with protamine, useful peri-operatively and in renal impairment[6]
  • Edoxaban/dabigatran need a ≥5-day LMWH lead-in; warfarin needs LMWH/UFH bridging to INR 2–3[6]

Intermediate-risk — watch closely

Haemodynamically stable but with RV strain and/or raised troponin: anticoagulate and monitor closely in a higher-care setting. If they deteriorate to instability, they become high-risk and need rescue reperfusion. Involve senior staff/medical team early.

Duration of anticoagulation follows the same principles as DVT — 3 months for a surgery-provoked PE, longer if a persistent risk factor, unprovoked, or cancer-associated (see DVT protocol, Duration).[6]

6
The Surgical Inpatient — Key Caveats

Thrombolysis carries major bleeding risk — recent surgery is a contraindication

Systemic thrombolysis dramatically increases bleeding risk, and recent major surgery is a relative-to-absolute contraindication. In a post-operative patient with a high-risk PE who needs reperfusion, thrombolysis may not be safe — catheter-directed therapy or surgical embolectomy should be considered instead. This is an emergency senior/multidisciplinary decision.[1]

D-dimer in our setting — not readily available, and unhelpful post-op anyway

D-dimer is not readily available at CRH (send-out to private labs, often >24 h turnaround) — far too slow for an acute diagnosis. And even where available, it is almost always elevated after surgery and cannot risk-stratify the surgical inpatient. If PE is suspected in a post-operative patient, proceed to CTPA — do not wait on a D-dimer.[1]

Anticoagulation vs post-operative bleeding

As with DVT, balance the need to anticoagulate against post-operative bleeding risk. A short-acting agent (LMWH/UFH) is preferred peri-operatively. Where anticoagulation is contraindicated, discuss a temporary IVC filter with senior staff.

7
Escalation Triggers

Call senior help immediately

  • Any haemodynamic instability with suspected/confirmed PE — peri-arrest; senior + medical/critical care team now
  • Hypoxia not corrected by oxygen
  • Confirmed PE where thrombolysis may be needed but the patient is post-operative — urgent multidisciplinary decision on reperfusion strategy
  • Intermediate-risk PE (RV strain/raised troponin) — needs higher-care monitoring
  • Suspected PE where anticoagulation is hazardous (recent major surgery, active bleeding) — senior decision; consider IVC filter
  • Cardiac arrest with suspected massive PE — follow ALS; thrombolysis during CPR may be considered
§

Ward Emergency · ACLS 2025

Code Blue — Cardiac Arrest

Adult advanced life support · read ahead so the sequence is familiar when it matters
First 60 Seconds
High-Quality CPR
The Algorithm
Shockable
Non-Shockable
Drugs
Reversible Causes
Defibrillation
ROSC
References
⚡ This is pre-reading, not a live reference A code is run from training and muscle memory — you will not have time to read this during an arrest. The purpose here is to make the sequence, the doses, and the rhythm-based decisions familiar in advance. The two highest-value actions are high-quality chest compressions and early defibrillation of a shockable rhythm — everything else is built around minimising interruptions to compressions. This is a summary for familiarisation, not a substitute for certified ACLS training.
0
The First 60 Seconds
1 — Confirm & callCheck responsiveness and breathing/pulse simultaneously (≤10 seconds). If no pulse or not breathing normally → call the code/cardiac arrest team and ask for the defibrillator and crash trolley immediately.
2 — Start compressions nowBegin high-quality chest compressions immediately — do not wait for the trolley. Push hard, push fast (see Section 1).
3 — Attach the defibrillator/monitor as soon as it arrivesThis is the single most time-critical step after compressions — it tells you whether the rhythm is shockable. Minimise the pause in compressions while pads go on.
4 — Assign rolesCompressions, airway/ventilation, defibrillator/monitor, drugs/IV access, and a team leader who stands back and coordinates. Rotate the compressor every 2 minutes.

Everything that follows hangs off one question asked every 2 minutes: is the rhythm shockable or not?

1
High-Quality CPR — the foundation
ParameterTarget
Rate100–120 compressions per minute
DepthAt least 2 inches (5 cm), not more than 2.4 inches (6 cm)
RecoilAllow full chest recoil between compressions
InterruptionsMinimise — keep peri-shock pauses as short as possible
CompressorChange every 2 minutes (or sooner if fatigued) to maintain quality
Ventilation (no advanced airway)30:2 compression-to-ventilation ratio
Ventilation (advanced airway in place)1 breath every 6 seconds (10/min) with continuous compressions; avoid over-ventilation

If quantitative waveform capnography is available and PetCO₂ is low or decreasing, reassess CPR quality — it is a real-time measure of how effective your compressions are.[1,4]

2
The Algorithm — the 2-minute cycle

Cardiac arrest care runs in 2-minute cycles. After each cycle you pause briefly to check the rhythm, deliver a shock if indicated, and immediately resume compressions. The whole algorithm branches on one question:

Is the rhythm shockable?

  • Shockable — Ventricular Fibrillation (VF) or pulseless Ventricular Tachycardia (pVT) → Section 3
  • Non-shockable — Asystole or Pulseless Electrical Activity (PEA) → Section 4
Each 2-minute cycleHigh-quality CPR for 2 minutes → brief pause → check rhythm → shock if shockable → immediately resume CPR (do not pause to check pulse straight after a shock) → give drugs as due → repeat.

Throughout: establish IV/IO access, consider an advanced airway (without interrupting compressions where possible), and actively look for and treat reversible causes (Section 6).

3
Shockable — VF / pulseless VT
Shock immediatelyDeliver one shock (single-shock strategy — no stacked shocks) at the device-recommended energy. Resume CPR instantly without a pulse check.
2 min CPR → rhythm check → shock again if still VF/pVTContinue the cycle.
Epinephrine (adrenaline) 1 mg — after the initial defibrillation attempts failIn shockable rhythms, give epinephrine 1 mg IV/IO after the first shock(s) have not restored a rhythm, then repeat every 3–5 minutes. Priority remains CPR + early defibrillation first.[1,3]
Antiarrhythmic for refractory VF/pVTAfter the third shock, give amiodarone 300 mg IV/IO (may repeat 150 mg once), or lidocaine as an alternative.[1,3]
Treat reversible causesWork through the Hs and Ts (Section 6) throughout.

Key sequencing point

For a shockable rhythm, CPR and defibrillation take priority over drugs — give epinephrine only after initial shocks have failed, not before. This is the opposite of the non-shockable pathway.[1,2]

4
Non-Shockable — Asystole / PEA

Do not shock

Asystole (flat line) and PEA (organised electrical activity but no pulse) are not shockable. A shock has no role — the priorities are excellent CPR, early epinephrine, and finding the reversible cause.

Epinephrine (adrenaline) 1 mg as soon as possibleIn non-shockable rhythms, give epinephrine 1 mg IV/IO as soon as feasible, then repeat every 3–5 minutes. Early epinephrine is the key drug action here.[1,3]
High-quality CPR in 2-minute cyclesRhythm check every 2 minutes. If it becomes shockable, switch to the shockable pathway (Section 3).
Hunt the reversible cause — this is where it is most often foundAsystole/PEA frequently has an identifiable, treatable cause — work the Hs and Ts hard (Section 6).

Key sequencing point

For a non-shockable rhythm, give epinephrine early (the reverse of the shockable pathway, where shocks come first). No antiarrhythmic is indicated for asystole/PEA.[1,2]

5
Drugs & Doses
DrugDoseWhen
Epinephrine (adrenaline)1 mg IV/IO, repeat every 3–5 minNon-shockable: ASAP. Shockable: after initial shocks fail[1]
Amiodarone300 mg IV/IO, then 150 mg once if neededRefractory VF/pVT, after 3rd shock[1,3]
Lidocaine (alternative)1–1.5 mg/kg, then 0.5–0.75 mg/kgAlternative to amiodarone for refractory VF/pVT[1]

What the 2025 guidelines say NOT to do routinely

  • Do not use high-dose epinephrine routinely[1,3]
  • Do not substitute vasopressin for epinephrine[1]
  • Do not give calcium routinely during arrest[1]
  • Do not give sodium bicarbonate routinely during arrest[1,3]

These agents have specific indications (e.g. calcium for hyperkalaemia — see Electrolytes protocol) but are not part of routine arrest management.

6
Reversible Causes — the Hs and Ts

Especially in non-shockable arrest, survival often depends on finding and treating the cause. Surgical patients have specific high-yield causes — hypovolaemia from bleeding and tension pneumothorax should be near the top of your list.

The HsThe Ts
Hypovolaemia (haemorrhage — common in the surgical patient)Tension pneumothorax (decompress immediately)
HypoxiaTamponade (cardiac)
Hydrogen ion (acidosis)Toxins
Hypo/hyperkalaemia (& other metabolic — see Electrolytes)Thrombosis — pulmonary (massive PE — see PE protocol)
HypothermiaThrombosis — coronary (MI)

Surgical-patient priorities

In a post-operative arrest, actively exclude haemorrhage/hypovolaemia (check the operative site, drains, abdomen — give blood/fluids), tension pneumothorax (especially after central line insertion or chest/upper abdominal surgery — needle/finger decompression), and massive PE. These are reversible and time-critical.

7
Defibrillation — practical points
  • Single-shock strategy — one shock, then immediately resume CPR. Do not deliver stacked shocks and do not pause to check rhythm or pulse right after the shock[1,3,4]
  • Biphasic preferred over monophasic where available[1,4]
  • Energy: biphasic — use the manufacturer's recommended dose (e.g. initial 120–200 J); if unknown, use the maximum available. Second and subsequent doses equal or higher[1]
  • Minimise the peri-shock pause — keep compressions going until the moment of charge/clearance and resume the instant the shock is delivered
  • Ensure everyone is clear and oxygen is not flowing over the chest at the moment of shock
8
Return of Spontaneous Circulation (ROSC) & Stopping

If ROSC is achieved

  • Move to post-cardiac-arrest care — this is a senior/critical-care-led phase
  • Support airway, breathing, and circulation; avoid hypotension and hypoxia
  • Obtain a 12-lead ECG (look for STEMI), arterial blood gas, and bloods
  • Targeted temperature management and the cause of the arrest are addressed by the receiving team
  • Arrange ICU/HDU transfer

Deciding to stop

The decision to stop resuscitation is a senior/team-leader decision, taking into account the downtime, the rhythm, response to interventions, reversible causes addressed, and the patient's overall context and wishes. Consider the appropriateness of continued resuscitation at each rhythm check.[1,2]

The junior's role

In a code, the most valuable things a junior can do are: recognise the arrest and call the team early, start and maintain high-quality compressions, get the pads on fast, and help work through reversible causes. You do not need to lead the code to be essential to it.

§
References
  1. Hsu A, Kleinman ME, Kudenchuk PJ, et al. Part 9: adult advanced life support: 2025 American Heart Association guidelines for cardiopulmonary resuscitation and emergency cardiovascular care. Pathology · Biliary

Acute Cholecystitis

Tokyo Guidelines (TG18) · diagnosis · severity · timing of surgery · local adaptation
Core Message
Diagnosis (TG18)
Severity Grading
Initial Management
Timing of Surgery
Local Adaptation
Sickle Cell
Cholecystostomy
Escalation
References
⚑ Core Principle The evidence-based standard for acute cholecystitis is early laparoscopic cholecystectomy — operating during the index admission rather than sending the patient away to return later. Where operative access is limited, a delayed/interval approach (settle the acute event medically, operate later) is a legitimate and commonly used adaptation — but the early-surgery standard should be understood, because it carries better outcomes where it can be achieved.
★
The Big Picture

Acute cholecystitis is inflammation of the gallbladder, usually from a stone obstructing the cystic duct (calculous; ~90–95%). The Tokyo Guidelines (TG18) provide the international framework for diagnosis, severity grading, and management.

The three decisions to make

  • Is it acute cholecystitis? — TG18 diagnostic criteria (Section 1)
  • How severe? — TG18 Grade I/II/III (Section 2)
  • When (and whether) to operate? — early vs delayed, modified by local operative access (Sections 4–5)
1
Diagnosis — TG18 Criteria
CategoryFindings
A. Local signs of inflammation(1) Murphy's sign; (2) RUQ mass / pain / tenderness
B. Systemic signs of inflammation(1) Fever; (2) elevated CRP; (3) elevated WBC
C. Imaging findingsFindings characteristic of acute cholecystitis

Putting it together

  • Suspected diagnosis: one item in A + one item in B
  • Definite diagnosis: one item in A + one item in B + C[1]

Ultrasound is the first-line imaging test. Characteristic findings: gallbladder wall thickening (≥4 mm), gallbladder enlargement (long axis ≥8 cm, short axis ≥4 cm), gallstones or retained debris, pericholecystic fluid, and a sonographic Murphy's sign.[1]

Local resource note: CRP is not routinely available in our setting — the systemic inflammation limb can be met with fever or a raised WBC. Ultrasound is accessible and is the key diagnostic test here.

2
Severity Grading (TG18)
GradeDefinition
Grade I (mild)Acute cholecystitis in a healthy patient with no organ dysfunction and only mild gallbladder inflammation
Grade II (moderate)Any of: WBC >18,000; palpable tender RUQ mass; symptoms >72 h; marked local inflammation (gangrenous/emphysematous cholecystitis, pericholecystic or hepatic abscess, biliary peritonitis)
Grade III (severe)Acute cholecystitis with organ/system dysfunction (cardiovascular, neurological, respiratory, renal, hepatic, or haematological)

Severity grading matters because it predicts mortality, length of stay, and the likelihood of a difficult operation — and it steers the management pathway and the timing/route of surgery.[1]

3
Initial Management — All Patients
  • Resuscitate: IV fluids, correct electrolytes, analgesia, antiemetics, nil by mouth if surgery anticipated
  • Antibiotics: empirical cover for biliary organisms (Gram-negatives, anaerobes), chosen by severity, community vs hospital acquisition, and local susceptibilities. Review and stop early once source control is achieved — a short course is sufficient after cholecystectomy[1]
  • Bloods: FBC, U&E, LFTs (a significantly deranged bilirubin/ALP raises suspicion of CBD stone or cholangitis), amylase/lipase (exclude pancreatitis), group & save, blood cultures if septic
  • Assess for complications and alternative biliary pathology: obstructive jaundice, cholangitis (Charcot's triad), gallstone pancreatitis — these change the pathway

Watch for cholangitis & CBD stones

Jaundice, deranged LFTs, or a dilated CBD on ultrasound suggest a common bile duct stone or ascending cholangitis — which need biliary drainage (ERCP) and change the plan. Cholangitis with sepsis is a separate emergency requiring urgent biliary decompression.

4
Timing of Surgery — The Evidence

Early laparoscopic cholecystectomy is the standard

TG18 recommends early laparoscopic cholecystectomy as the treatment of choice, and importantly removed the old <72-hour time limit — surgery is appropriate within the index admission, ideally within 7 days of symptom onset, in suitable patients.[1,2] Early surgery gives shorter total hospital stay and recovery compared with sending the patient away for delayed surgery, without an increase in complications or conversion in appropriately selected patients.[2,3]

TG18 pathway by grade (risk-stratified by CCI and ASA)

GradeTG18 recommendation
Grade IEarly laparoscopic cholecystectomy if CCI ≤5 and ASA-PS ≤2[4]
Grade IIEarly laparoscopic cholecystectomy by experienced surgeons if CCI ≤5 and ASA-PS ≤2; otherwise medical treatment ± gallbladder drainage, then delayed cholecystectomy[4]
Grade IIIInitial drainage/medical management; cholecystectomy only in selected patients (favourable organ failure, CCI ≤3, ASA-PS ≤2) at an advanced centre. Otherwise urgent drainage then delayed surgery[4]

CCI = Charlson Comorbidity Index — a validated score of a patient's comorbidities that predicts post-surgical mortality (a lower score means fewer/less severe comorbidities). ASA-PS = American Society of Anesthesiologists Physical Status — the anaesthetic fitness grade (lower = fitter). In TG18, low scores on both (good comorbidity profile and good anaesthetic fitness) identify the patients who tolerate early surgery best.

"Early" means during the index admission. The general principle: the fitter the patient and the milder the grade, the stronger the case for operating now rather than later.

5
Local Adaptation — Delayed / Interval Surgery

Our setting: limited operative access

The ideal is early laparoscopic cholecystectomy during the index admission. In our setting, operative time for laparoscopic cholecystectomy is sometimes limited, and so a proportion of patients are managed non-operatively for the acute event and brought back for surgery after the acute episode resolves (interval/delayed cholecystectomy). This is a recognised, legitimate pathway — name the constraint honestly and use the workaround effectively.

How to run the delayed pathway safely

  • Settle the acute event medically — antibiotics, analgesia, fluids, NBM as needed; most mild/moderate cases settle
  • Monitor for failure of conservative management — worsening pain, rising inflammatory markers, spreading peritonism, sepsis, or features of gangrene/perforation mean the patient is not settling and needs escalation (drainage or surgery), not continued waiting (Section 7)
  • Arrange definitive interval cholecystectomy — book the patient for elective laparoscopic cholecystectomy after the acute episode resolves; do not lose them to follow-up, as recurrence while waiting is common
  • Counsel the patient that symptoms can recur before the interval operation, and give clear advice to return if they do

⚠ Keep the standard in view — do not default to delay

Where a patient is fit, the grade is mild, and a theatre slot is achievable during admission, early cholecystectomy remains preferable — it avoids the readmission, the recurrence risk while waiting, and the often-harder dissection of a later operation. Use the delayed pathway as the pragmatic adaptation when operative access genuinely prevents early surgery — not as the automatic default.

🩸
Sickle Cell Disease & Gallstones — a Jamaican priority

Sickle cell disease (SCD) and biliary disease intersect heavily in Jamaica — about 1 in 150 newborns has a form of sickle cell disease, and about 1 in 300 has homozygous HbSS disease, the most common and severe form.[5] Chronic haemolysis produces pigment (bilirubin) gallstones, so cholelithiasis is common and presents young.

🇯🇲 Local data — UWI / MRC Jamaican Cohort

  • Gallstones were present in ~28% of Jamaican adults with HbSS, increasing with age and haemolytic rate (classic UWI/MRC radiograph study)[6]
  • In the Jamaican Cohort Study (followed from birth), gallstones developed in a large proportion of HbSS patients, but specific symptoms necessitating cholecystectomy occurred in relatively few — supporting operating for symptoms/complications rather than for stones alone[7]
  • A UWI cholecystectomy series (n=27, mean age 30.8, mean Hb 7.4 g/dl, all HbSS): 66.7% laparoscopic, 33.3% open, laparoscopic-to-open conversion 27.8% (hepatomegaly, scarring obscuring Calot's triangle). Higher perioperative morbidity has been reported in the Caribbean (up to ~37.5% historically) than in high-volume international centres[8]

Key management principles

Operate for symptoms; be cautious about prophylactic surgery

Symptomatic cholelithiasis/cholecystitis and choledocholithiasis are clear indications for cholecystectomy. Prophylactic cholecystectomy for asymptomatic stones is debated — local cohort data show many HbSS patients with stones never need surgery, so it is not a routine recommendation; weigh it case-by-case (e.g. where future presentation would be hazardous or distinguishing biliary pain from crisis is difficult).[7,8]

The central peri-operative risk: acute chest syndrome & crises

SCD patients have markedly increased peri-operative risk — acute chest syndrome (ACS), vaso-occlusive crisis, infection, and acute-on-chronic complications. Abdominal surgery is particularly associated with post-operative ACS (reported up to ~30% in non-transfused patients). Distinguishing a surgical picture from a vaso-occlusive crisis can be genuinely difficult.[9,10]

Peri-operative bundle

  • Preoperative transfusion — simple, to a target Hb of ~10 g/dl. The landmark RCT showed a conservative simple-transfusion regimen (target Hb 10) is as effective as aggressive exchange transfusion (HbS <30%) in preventing complications, with less alloimmunisation; the TAPS trial showed preoperative transfusion reduced clinically important complications (15% vs 39%) for low-/medium-risk surgery. So simple transfusion to ~10 g/dl is the standard, not routine exchange[9,10]
  • Avoid the sickling triggers throughout: hypoxia, hypothermia, dehydration, acidosis, and pain — give oxygen, keep warm, hydrate (IV fluids), and provide good analgesia[9]
  • Involve haematology early for transfusion planning and crisis management; know the patient's steady-state Hb and usual triggers
  • Timing & approach: elective laparoscopic cholecystectomy after the acute event settles and after optimisation is preferred; anticipate a higher conversion rate (hepatomegaly, adhesions). Emergency surgery during an acute crisis carries higher risk — stabilise and optimise first where the clinical situation allows[8]

This intersection is a genuine Jamaican priority and one of the most-studied areas in Caribbean surgery. The transferable principle: operate for symptoms, transfuse simply to ~10 g/dl, rigorously avoid sickling triggers peri-operatively, and partner with haematology.

6
Gallbladder Drainage (Cholecystostomy)

Percutaneous cholecystostomy (a drain placed into the gallbladder, usually under ultrasound/CT guidance) is the rescue option for the patient who is too unwell or too high-risk for surgery but is not responding to antibiotics.

  • Indication: Grade II/III cholecystitis not responding to medical therapy in a patient unfit for cholecystectomy; a bridge to recovery, with interval cholecystectomy considered later if the patient becomes fit[1,4]
  • It achieves source control without the physiological insult of surgery in a sick patient
  • Where percutaneous drainage is not available locally, this is a point for early senior discussion and possible transfer, or a decision on surgery despite the risk
7
Escalation Triggers

Call senior staff

  • Failure to settle on conservative management — worsening pain, rising WBC, spreading peritonism, ongoing fever/sepsis
  • Features of gangrenous or perforated gallbladder — severe sepsis, generalised peritonitis — needs urgent surgery
  • Cholangitis (Charcot's triad: RUQ pain, fever/rigors, jaundice) — urgent biliary drainage, separate emergency
  • Obstructive jaundice / suspected CBD stone — needs ERCP planning
  • Grade III (organ dysfunction) — resuscitation, drainage, and senior/critical-care involvement
  • Diagnostic uncertainty, or the unwell patient not fitting the usual picture
§

Pathology · Pancreatic

Acute Pancreatitis

Diagnosis · severity · goal-directed fluids · nutrition · ERCP & cholecystectomy timing
Core Message
Diagnosis
Cause & Severity
Fluids
Supportive Care
Antibiotics
Gallstone AP
Escalation
References
⚑ Core Principle Most acute pancreatitis is mild and settles with supportive care. The management has shifted in the last decade and several old habits are now wrong: fluids should be goal-directed, not maximally aggressive; antibiotics are NOT given routinely; feed early rather than keeping nil by mouth; and ERCP is only for concurrent cholangitis. Getting the first 24–48 hours right — sensible fluids, analgesia, early feeding, severity assessment — is what shapes the outcome.
★
What's Changed — Read This First

Four old practices that current guidelines have overturned — these are the common junior errors:

Old habitCurrent standard
Maximally aggressive IV fluidsGoal-directed, moderate fluid resuscitation (over-resuscitation causes harm)[1,2]
Prophylactic antibioticsNo routine antibiotics — only for confirmed/strongly suspected infection or cholangitis[1,2]
"Pancreatic rest" — nil by mouthEarly oral feeding (within 24–48 h) as tolerated, low-fat solid diet[1,2,4]
Routine/early ERCP for gallstone APERCP only if concurrent cholangitis (or obstruction) — not routinely[1,2,4]
1
Diagnosis — 2 of 3

Diagnosis requires at least 2 of the following 3:

#Criterion
1Characteristic abdominal pain — epigastric/LUQ, constant, often radiating to the back
2Lipase or amylase >3× upper limit of normal (lipase preferred — more specific, stays elevated longer)
3Characteristic findings on imaging (CT, MRI, or ultrasound)

Imaging — do not CT everyone on admission

If the diagnosis is made on pain + enzymes, a CT is not needed at admission — most cases are mild and CT adds nothing early. CT is reserved for diagnostic uncertainty or to assess complications, and is best delayed beyond 72 hours, as imaging earlier underestimates the extent of necrosis. Always get an ultrasound to look for gallstones as the cause.[1,2]

2
Cause & Severity Assessment

Find the cause

The two commonest are gallstones and alcohol — together ~70–80%. Others: hypertriglyceridaemia, hypercalcaemia, drugs, ERCP, trauma, autoimmune, idiopathic. Always request an ultrasound (gallstones), and check LFTs, calcium, and triglycerides.

Severity — Revised Atlanta Classification

SeverityDefinition
MildNo organ failure, no local or systemic complications (most cases)
Moderately severeTransient organ failure (resolves <48 h) and/or local complications (fluid collections, necrosis)
SeverePersistent organ failure (≥48 h)[3]

The 48-hour duration of organ failure is the key dividing line between moderately severe and severe. Persistent organ failure is what drives mortality.[3]

Early risk stratification

Atlanta categories are descriptive (assigned after the course plays out), so use an early scoring tool in the first 24–48 h to predict a severe course: BISAP (Bedside Index of Severity in AP — 5 parameters, score ≥3 = higher mortality), APACHE II, or Glasgow. Practical bedside red flags: persistent SIRS beyond 48 h, rising urea/haematocrit, age, comorbidity, obesity, and a falling urine output. Persistent SIRS in particular predicts a worse outcome and signals the need for higher-level monitoring.[2]

3
Fluid Resuscitation — Goal-Directed

The single biggest change — do NOT over-resuscitate

The old teaching of maximally aggressive fluids has been overturned. The WATERFALL trial and current guidelines show aggressive resuscitation causes fluid overload (respiratory failure, abdominal compartment syndrome) without benefit. The standard is now moderate, goal-directed resuscitation.[1,2,4]

  • Fluid of choice: Lactated Ringer's / Hartmann's (balanced crystalloid) in preference to normal saline[1,2]
  • Rate: a moderate maintenance-plus rate (guidelines describe ~1.5 mL/kg/h, or modest goal-directed rates); give a bolus (e.g. 10 mL/kg) only if hypovolaemic/hypotensive[1,2]
  • Titrate to targets, not a fixed volume: urine output ≥0.5 mL/kg/h, heart rate, MAP, and a falling urea/haematocrit. Reassess frequently in the first 6 h and over the next 24–48 h[2]
  • Pull back fluids by 48 h once the patient is resuscitated — ongoing high-rate fluids cause harm[2]

Cross-reference the IV Fluid Management protocol for balanced crystalloid principles. The message here: enough to perfuse, not a flood.

4
Supportive Care & Nutrition
  • Analgesia — treat pain adequately (opioids as needed); good analgesia is central
  • Antiemetics and monitoring of fluid balance, electrolytes, glucose
  • Oxygen to maintain saturations; watch for respiratory compromise (effusions, ARDS in severe AP)

Feed early — abandon "pancreatic rest"

  • Mild AP: start oral feeding early (within 24–48 h) as tolerated — a low-fat solid diet is as safe as a slow liquid-to-solid build-up. Do not wait for pain or enzymes to fully normalise[1,2]
  • Severe AP / can't tolerate oral: use enteral nutrition (NG/NJ) — it reduces infectious complications. Avoid parenteral nutrition unless the enteral route is impossible[1,2,4]
5
Antibiotics — Not Routine

Do NOT give prophylactic antibiotics

Prophylactic antibiotics are not recommended — not even in predicted severe or necrotising pancreatitis. They do not prevent infection and drive resistance.[1,2,4,5]

When antibiotics ARE indicated

  • Concurrent cholangitis (the biliary infection, not the pancreatitis itself)
  • Infected pancreatic necrosis — confirmed or strongly suspected (clinical deterioration, gas in a collection on imaging). Use agents that penetrate necrosis; intervention (when needed) follows a step-up approach — percutaneous/endoscopic drainage first, surgery escalated only if needed, and delayed ≥4 weeks where possible to let the collection wall mature; this is a senior/multidisciplinary decision[4,5]
  • Another identified extra-pancreatic infection (e.g. pneumonia, line sepsis)

A fever or raised inflammatory markers in the first days of AP is usually the inflammatory (SIRS) response of the pancreatitis itself — not infection — and is not in itself a reason to start antibiotics.[2]

6
Gallstone Pancreatitis — ERCP & Cholecystectomy

ERCP — only for cholangitis (or ongoing obstruction)

In gallstone AP without cholangitis, routine early ERCP does not help — manage medically. ERCP is indicated when there is concurrent acute cholangitis (then early ERCP, within 24 h, reduces morbidity/mortality) or persistent biliary obstruction.[1,2]

Same-admission cholecystectomy for mild gallstone AP

For mild gallstone pancreatitis, laparoscopic cholecystectomy during the same admission (once the pancreatitis has settled) is the standard — it prevents recurrent attacks and is supported by the evidence.[1] In severe AP or where there are collections/necrosis, cholecystectomy is deferred until inflammation resolves.

Local adaptation: where same-admission operative access is limited (as for acute cholecystitis — see that protocol), an interval cholecystectomy after discharge is the pragmatic fallback — but counsel the patient on recurrence risk while waiting and ensure they are booked and not lost to follow-up. Same-admission surgery remains preferable where achievable.

7
Escalation Triggers

Call senior staff / consider HDU-ICU

  • Organ dysfunction — hypoxia, hypotension, oliguria/rising creatinine, altered consciousness (severe AP)
  • Persistent SIRS beyond 48 h — predicts a severe course
  • Rising urea/haematocrit despite resuscitation, or signs of fluid overload
  • Concurrent cholangitis (fever, jaundice, RUQ pain) — needs urgent ERCP
  • Clinical deterioration in necrotising AP — suspect infected necrosis
  • Abdominal compartment syndrome — tense distended abdomen, rising airway pressures, oliguria
§
References
  1. Tenner S, Vege SS, Sheth SG, et al. American College of Gastroenterology guideline: management of acute pancreatitis.   2. Crockett SD, Wani S, Gardner TB, et al. American Gastroenterological Association Institute guideline on initial management of acute pancreatitis. Pathology · GI Haemorrhage

Upper GI Bleeding

Acute management · resuscitation · risk scoring · consent while stable · limited-endoscopy pathway
Core Message
Recognise & Resuscitate
Consent While Stable
Risk Scoring
Transfusion
Drugs
Endoscopy & Local Pathway
Surgery & Escalation
H. pylori
References
⚑ Core Principle The acute management of upper GI bleeding is resuscitation first, risk-stratify, then definitive haemostasis. Endoscopy within 24 h is the standard for diagnosis and treatment — but where endoscopy is not readily available, the emphasis falls on excellent resuscitation, risk-stratification, pharmacological and supportive measures, early consent while the patient is stable, and a lower threshold for surgery when blood resources or endoscopic access run short.
★
The Local Frame

Upper GI bleeding (UGIB) = bleeding proximal to the ligament of Treitz — presenting with haematemesis, coffee-ground vomiting, or melaena. Commonest causes: peptic ulcer, oesophagitis/gastritis, varices, and Mallory-Weiss tears.

Three local realities that shape our pathway

  • Endoscopy is not readily available in the public sector — usually a private arrangement, with limited access. Patients are stabilised and endoscopy is arranged on discharge, or occasionally during admission if a private endoscopist can facilitate
  • Consent is taken while the patient is stable — not in shock (Section 2)
  • A lower threshold for surgery — because the two usual safety nets for continued non-operative management (endoscopic haemostasis and ample blood) are both constrained (Section 7)

🇯🇲 Local data — University Hospital of the West Indies (UHWI), Jamaica

A Jamaican series of 104 patients with UGIB (UHWI, 2006–2008; mean age 55, predominantly male) found leading causes of duodenal ulcer 28%, erosive gastritis 20%, and gastric ulcer 13% — i.e. peptic ulcer disease dominates locally, consistent with international data and with the high regional prevalence of H. pylori. PPIs were used in 91%, transfusion in 40%; mortality 5.7%, rebleeding 4.8%, surgery 5%. Notably only 40% had endoscopy within 24 h (median time to EGD 24 h, mean 46 h) — reflecting the access constraints described above.[3]

The local mortality (5.7%) is in line with international series. The delay-to-endoscopy figure directly reflects our access reality and reinforces the resuscitate-risk-stratify-and-maximise-medical-therapy emphasis of this protocol.

1
Recognise & Resuscitate
A–B–C and two large-bore cannulaeTwo wide-bore IV lines (14–16G). Bloods: FBC, U&E (urea rises in UGIB), LFTs, coagulation, group & crossmatch. Note: in acute bleeding the initial haemoglobin can be normal before haemodilution.
Resuscitate to perfusionCrystalloid to restore circulation; activate the massive haemorrhage protocol if exsanguinating. Catheterise and monitor urine output as a perfusion marker.
Identify shock & ongoing bleedingTachycardia, hypotension, postural drop, cool peripheries, altered mentation. These drive the urgency of everything that follows.
Correct coagulopathyReverse anticoagulation and correct clotting/platelets per the clinical picture and local massive haemorrhage protocol.

Resuscitation comes before any attempt at definitive diagnosis or haemostasis — a stable patient is safer for endoscopy, transfer, consent, and surgery alike.

2
Consent While Stable — a Firm C principle

Meet, assess, and consent the patient while they are stable

When a referral comes — often from the emergency department or our internist colleagues — meet the patient, assess them, and discuss consent while they are stable. It does not make sense to try to obtain consent from a patient who is in shock.

  • Explain the diagnosis, the plan, and — importantly — the realistic possibility of surgery if they become unstable during admission, if blood resources limit continued non-operative management, or if endoscopy is not available to secure haemostasis
  • Consent should be taken by a member of the team who is familiar with the surgical procedure(s) and can properly explain the risks, benefits, and alternative options to the patient
  • Taking consent early, while the patient has capacity and is not compromised, is both safer and more valid than a rushed consent during deterioration
  • Document the discussion clearly, including the contingency of surgery

This is a deliberate Firm C practice: the calm, informed conversation happens up front, so that if the situation deteriorates the team can act without scrambling for consent at the worst possible moment.

3
Risk Scoring
ScoreUse
Glasgow-Blatchford (GBS)Pre-endoscopy risk tool. Uses urea, haemoglobin, systolic BP, pulse, melaena, syncope, hepatic/cardiac disease. GBS 0–1 = very low risk — may be managed as outpatient. Higher scores predict need for intervention (transfusion, endoscopy, surgery)[1,2]
RockallPredicts rebleeding and mortality; the full score uses post-endoscopy findings. Useful where endoscopy has happened[2]

Why scoring matters more when endoscopy is limited

When you cannot reach for urgent endoscopy in every patient, the risk score does heavier lifting — it identifies who is genuinely low-risk and safe for a stabilise-and-arrange-outpatient-endoscopy pathway, versus who is high-risk and needs admission, aggressive resuscitation, and early senior/surgical involvement. Use GBS at first assessment on every patient.

4
Transfusion Strategy
  • Restrictive threshold: transfuse at haemoglobin <7 g/dL, targeting roughly 7–9 g/dL, in the haemodynamically stable patient — a restrictive strategy reduces rebleeding and death[1]
  • Higher threshold (~8 g/dL) is reasonable in pre-existing cardiovascular disease[1]
  • The restrictive threshold applies to the stable patient — the actively exsanguinating patient is resuscitated clinically with blood/products, not by waiting for a number
  • Platelets if actively bleeding and platelets <50×10⁹/L; correct coagulopathy per the clinical picture

Blood as a limited resource

Where blood supply is constrained, the restrictive threshold is doubly important — but recognise that if a patient needs ongoing transfusion to stay ahead of the bleed and the supply cannot sustain it, that is itself a trigger to move toward definitive control (endoscopy if available, otherwise surgery — Section 7).

5
Pharmacological Measures

Non-variceal (suspected peptic ulcer)

  • PPI: guidelines note pre-endoscopy PPI does not improve hard clinical outcomes, but it is widely used and reduces high-risk stigmata at endoscopy. After endoscopy, give IV PPI (e.g. high-dose) if high-risk stigmata are found[1,2]
  • Erythromycin (a prokinetic, e.g. 250 mg IV ~30–90 min before endoscopy) improves gastric views by clearing blood[1]

Suspected variceal bleeding (known/suspected liver disease)

  • Vasoactive drug (terlipressin or octreotide) at presentation — reduces portal pressure[2]
  • Prophylactic antibiotics (e.g. ceftriaxone) — reduce infection and mortality in variceal bleeding[2]
  • Definitive treatment is endoscopic band ligation; balloon tamponade (Sengstaken-Blakemore) is a temporising bridge in exsanguinating variceal bleeding when endoscopy is delayed or unavailable

Where endoscopy is delayed, maximise medical therapy

If endoscopic haemostasis cannot be obtained promptly, pharmacological measures become more important as a holding strategy — full-dose PPI for suspected ulcer bleeding, and vasoactive drug + antibiotics + consideration of balloon tamponade for suspected variceal bleeding — while arranging definitive control or transfer.

6
Endoscopy — Standard & Local Pathway

The standard of care

  • Endoscopy within 24 hours of presentation for admitted, resuscitated patients — both diagnostic and therapeutic[1,2]
  • Haemodynamically unstable despite resuscitation, or suspected variceal bleed → earlier (after resuscitation; variceal ideally within 12 h)[2]
  • Endoscopic haemostasis for high-risk ulcer stigmata (active spurting/oozing, non-bleeding visible vessel)[1]

Local adaptation — limited endoscopy access

In our setting endoscopy is not readily available in the public sector. The practical pathway:

  • Resuscitate and risk-stratify every patient (GBS). Most UGIB stops spontaneously, which makes a stabilise-first approach viable for the majority
  • Low-risk, stopped bleeding: stabilise and arrange endoscopy on/after discharge as a private/outpatient procedure
  • During admission: occasionally access in-admission endoscopy if a private endoscopist can facilitate — prioritise this for higher-risk or rebleeding patients
  • High-risk / ongoing bleeding / rebleeding who cannot get timely endoscopy: escalate early — this is where the surgical threshold is lower (Section 7), and where transfer should be considered if feasible
7
Surgery & Escalation — Lower Threshold

Our threshold for surgery is lower than in a well-resourced centre

In a centre with 24/7 endoscopy and an unlimited blood bank, the team can persist with non-operative management through rebleeds, repeating endoscopic haemostasis and transfusing freely. We have neither safety net reliably — so when endoscopic control is unavailable or has failed, or when blood resources cannot sustain ongoing losses, surgery moves up the algorithm earlier.

Triggers to escalate to senior staff / consider surgery

  • Haemodynamic instability not responding to resuscitation — exsanguinating bleed
  • Ongoing transfusion requirement that blood supply cannot sustain
  • Rebleeding after initial stabilisation where repeat endoscopic control is not available
  • No access to endoscopic haemostasis in a patient who needs it to stop bleeding
  • Failure of endoscopic therapy where it was achievable

Because surgery is a realistic early option here, the early consent conversation (Section 2) is what makes acting on these triggers smooth and safe. Involve senior staff early — the decision to operate is theirs, made better by a patient already assessed and consented while stable. Interventional radiology (embolisation) is an alternative to surgery where available.

8
H. pylori — Eradication (see Ward Topics)

Key points for the bleeding ulcer

  • H. pylori eradication is the single most effective measure to prevent recurrent ulcer bleeding — it reduces ulcer recurrence from ~80%/year to <5%, and the risk of rebleeding falls from ~33% (infection persisting) to near-zero after successful eradication
  • Test sensitivity is reduced during the acute bleed (active blood + PPI), so a negative test during the bleed cannot be trusted
  • Firm C approach: start eradication empirically once the patient tolerates oral intake (before discharge), then confirm eradication with a test of cure after the course — the confirmation is mandatory after a bleeding ulcer

↗ Full protocol

For the complete eradication protocol — test-vs-empirical detail, the BQT regimen and alternatives, the local bismuth-availability workaround, local prevalence data, and the test-of-cure timing (≥4 weeks post-antibiotics, ≥2 weeks off PPI) — see H. pylori Management in Ward Topics. The eradication protocol is identical whether the ulcer bled, perforated, or presented uncomplicated.

§

  2. Mullady DK, Wang AY, Waschke KA. AGA clinical practice update on endoscopic therapies for non-variceal upper gastrointestinal bleeding. Gastroenterology. 2020;159(3):1120–1128. doi:10.1053/j.gastro.2020.05.095 — with NICE CG141 (acute UGIB) and Baveno VII (variceal). (risk scoring Blatchford/Rockall; timing of endoscopy incl. earlier for variceal/unstable; variceal vasoactive drug + prophylactic antibiotics + band ligation; balloon tamponade as bridge)
  3. Soares D, Lue A, McFarlane M, et al. Upper gastrointestinal bleeding: a Jamaican perspective [UHWI, Jamaica, 2006–2008, n=104]. West Indian Med J. 2014. (Jamaican/Caribbean local data: duodenal ulcer 28%, erosive gastritis 20%, gastric ulcer 13%; PPI use 91%; transfusion 40%; mortality 5.7%, rebleeding 4.8%, surgery 5%; only 40% had EGD within 24 h — peptic ulcer disease dominates, high regional H. pylori prevalence)
