Pathology · GI Haemorrhage

Lower GI Bleeding

Acute management · exclude UGIB · risk scoring · CT angiography · surgical threshold
Core Message
Recognise & Resuscitate
Exclude UGIB
Consent While Stable
Risk & Transfusion
Diagnosis & Localisation
Local Pathway & Surgery
Escalation
References
⚑ Core Principle Most lower GI bleeding (LGIB) stops spontaneously, so the majority are managed by resuscitation, risk-stratification, and arranged (non-urgent) colonoscopy. The two acute priorities are: exclude a brisk upper GI source masquerading as LGIB, and identify the minority with severe ongoing bleeding who need urgent localisation (CT angiography) and intervention. As with UGIB, limited endoscopy access, consent while stable, and a lower surgical threshold shape our local pathway.
★
The Local Frame

LGIB = bleeding distal to the ligament of Treitz, usually presenting with haematochezia (fresh/dark red blood per rectum). Setting aside anorectal disease (haemorrhoids and anal fissures — extremely common sources of minor bleeding, but not usually the cause of significant acute LGIB), the causes in approximate order of frequency are:

CauseApprox. frequencyTypical feature
1. Diverticular bleeding — the commonest cause~26–50%
(varies by population)		Acute, painless, often large-volume haematochezia; usually self-limiting but recurs
2. Angiodysplasia (angioectasia)~3–30%Recurrent, painless; commonest vascular lesion; elderly; can cause iron-deficiency anaemia
3. Colitis — ischaemic, infective, or IBD~9–21%Bloody diarrhoea ± pain; ischaemic in elderly with cardiovascular risk, left colon
4. Colorectal neoplasia / post-polypectomy~malignancy in ~2.5% of LGIB colonoscopies; massive bleeding from cancer ~5–20% in seriesSlow chronic loss, change in bowel habit, anaemia; post-polypectomy bleeding can be acute

Important — frequencies are population-dependent

The 2023 ACG guideline is explicit that the ranking varies by population. Diverticular bleeding is the commonest cause in Western series (e.g. ~26% in a UK audit, but up to ~64% in a Japanese cohort with near-universal inpatient colonoscopy), whereas colitis and haemorrhoids have been the leading causes in studies from India.[1] So treat the order as a Western-derived guide, not a universal law — and keep colitis and anorectal sources high on the list in our own population. The source cannot be definitively identified in up to ~25% of cases.[1,2]

🇯🇲 Local data — University Hospital of the West Indies (UHWI), Jamaica

A Jamaican series of 189 hospitalised patients with acute LGIB (UHWI, 2007–2012; mean age 67, 55% female, 80% with comorbidity) found a profile close to the Western pattern: diverticular disease 56%, colorectal cancer 11%, haemorrhoidal disease 11%, and colonic polyps 8.5%. Blood transfusion was required in 43%, recurrent bleeding occurred in 3.7%, and 19% required emergency surgery.[8]

This local data is reassuring — it confirms diverticular disease is also the commonest cause of significant LGIB in our own Jamaican population, so the Western-derived ranking applies here. The notable local figure is the relatively high emergency-surgery rate (19%) and transfusion rate (43%), consistent with the resource constraints (limited endoscopy/embolization) discussed in this protocol.

Bottom line: outside of anorectal disease and haemorrhoids, the most common cause of significant lower GI bleeding (in Western data) is diverticular bleeding, followed by angiodysplasia, colitis, and neoplasia — with the caveat above on population variation.[1]

The reassuring majority

Approximately 80–85% of acute lower GI bleeds stop spontaneously with resuscitation and supportive care alone, and overall mortality is low (~2–4%).[2] This is what makes resuscitate-stabilise-and-arrange-colonoscopy the right approach for most patients, and fits a setting where urgent endoscopy is hard to obtain. The job is to identify the minority who keep bleeding.

The same three local realities (see UGIB)

Limited colonoscopy access in the public sector (stabilise → arrange outpatient/private colonoscopy; occasionally in-admission if facilitated); consent taken while stable; and a lower surgical threshold when blood resources or endoscopic access are constrained.

1
Recognise & Resuscitate
A–B–C, two large-bore cannulae, bloodsFBC, U&E, LFTs, coagulation, group & crossmatch. Resuscitate with crystalloid to normalise heart rate and blood pressure before any diagnostic or therapeutic procedure.
Assess severityHaemodynamic instability, volume and rate of ongoing bleeding, comorbidity. Catheterise and monitor urine output if significant.
Correct coagulopathy judiciouslyReversal agents are discouraged unless the patient remains unstable despite resuscitation; endoscopy is generally safe with INR ≤2.5. For severe bleeding, target platelets >30×10⁹/L (>50 before endoscopy).[1]
2
Exclude a Brisk Upper GI Source

Haematochezia + haemodynamic instability may be an UPPER GI bleed

A brisk upper GI bleed can present as fresh red blood per rectum. Haematochezia with haemodynamic instability should prompt suspicion of an upper GI source — if suspicion is high, an upper endoscopy (or at least upper GI evaluation) should be performed to exclude it.[1] A raised urea:creatinine ratio also points upstream.

The urea-to-creatinine ratio — a cheap, underused pointer

Why it works: in an upper GI bleed, blood is digested as it passes through the small bowel — the protein/haemoglobin is broken down and the nitrogen is absorbed, raising urea (the numerator). There is also often some pre-renal effect from volume loss. Both push urea up disproportionately to creatinine, so the ratio rises. In a lower GI bleed the blood is passed out relatively fresh — it is not digested and absorbed in the same way, so there is no extra nitrogen load to raise the urea; the ratio therefore stays low / near-normal — i.e. lower than in an upper GI bleed. In short: a high ratio points to an upper source; a low/normal ratio does not point upstream (though it does not exclude it — see accuracy below).

How to interpret it (mind the units):

  • Using urea and creatinine in SI units (urea mmol/L, creatinine µmol/L): a urea:creatinine ratio >100:1 suggests an upper source
  • Using the BUN:creatinine ratio (both in mg/dL, as in US labs): the equivalent threshold is >30:1
  • The higher the ratio, the more likely an upper source

It is a rule-in, not a rule-out test — a high ratio is fairly specific for an upper source, but a normal/low ratio does not exclude one (low sensitivity). Use it to raise suspicion, not to reassure. It is cheap and readily available locally and is worth calculating on every significant bleed.[1]

How accurate is it? (cited evidence) The diagnostic-accuracy literature confirms the rule-in/rule-out asymmetry with hard numbers. In the largest single diagnostic study (621 patients), at a BUN:creatinine cut-off of 35: specificity ~90%, PPV ~89%, positive likelihood ratio ~8 — but sensitivity only ~20% and NPV ~22%, with a modest overall discrimination (AUC ~0.63).[9] A 2026 systematic review/meta-analysis confirmed this: raising the cut-off to 40 improves specificity to ~94%, but the PPV is highly prevalence-dependent — falling to ~0.18 in low-prevalence settings.[10]

What the numbers mean at the bedside

  • A high ratio (>35, or >100 in SI units) rules IN an upper source with high probability — high specificity and PPV[9,10]
  • A normal/low ratio does NOT rule OUT an upper source — sensitivity is poor (~20%), so most upper bleeds will not have a raised ratio; a normal value is non-diagnostic and still warrants upper evaluation if suspicion is high[9]
  • Its usefulness rises with pre-test probability — most helpful in the unstable haematochezia patient where suspicion of an upper source is already real

(One 2024 study using a lower cut-off of 23.3 reported much higher sensitivity ~89% and specificity ~94%, but this is an outlier against the larger body of evidence and the meta-analysis, which support the high-specificity/low-sensitivity picture.)[10]

This is the single most important early branch point — missing a brisk UGIB presenting as haematochezia is a classic and dangerous error. Consider it in every unstable "lower" GI bleed.

3
Consent While Stable — a Firm C principle

Meet, assess, and consent while the patient is stable

As with UGIB: when the referral comes, meet the patient, assess them, and discuss consent while they are stable — including the realistic possibility of surgery if they become unstable, if blood resources limit continued non-operative management, or if colonoscopy is not available to localise and treat the bleed. Consent should be taken by a member of the team who is familiar with the surgical procedure(s) and can properly explain the risks, benefits, and alternative options to the patient. Obtaining consent from a patient in shock does not make sense; the calm, informed conversation belongs up front. Document it, including the surgical contingency.

4
Risk Stratification & Transfusion
  • Risk tool: the Oakland score identifies low-risk patients (Oakland ≤8) who may be suitable for early discharge and outpatient evaluation — supplement, do not replace, clinical judgement[1]
  • Restrictive transfusion: threshold haemoglobin <7 g/dL in the stable patient (higher, ~8 g/dL, with cardiovascular disease)[1]
  • For severe bleeding: platelets >30×10⁹/L (and >50 before endoscopy); avoid over-transfusion of platelets, which increases mortality[1]
  • NSAIDs should be avoided after LGIB, particularly diverticular or angioectasia bleeding[1]
5
Diagnosis & Localisation

The standard

  • Colonoscopy is the first-line diagnostic test in the haemodynamically stable patient, after adequate bowel prep — and can treat (clip, thermal, adrenaline)[1]
  • Timing: in stable patients, non-urgent colonoscopy within ~14 days is as effective as urgent (<24 h) for the outcomes that matter. Urgent colonoscopy does find the bleeding source/stigmata more often (higher diagnostic yield), but this does not translate into lower rebleeding, lower mortality, fewer operations, less interventional radiology, or fewer transfusions — and rebleeding may even trend higher with early scoping. So earlier diagnosis does not change the hard clinical outcomes. This is reassuring where access is limited[1,3,4]
  • CT angiography is the test of choice for ongoing, haemodynamically significant bleeding — it localises the source; a positive CTA can be followed by transcatheter embolisation[1]

CT angiography — accuracy, signs, and local availability

Where the guidelines place it (by haemodynamic status): CTA is included in the major guidelines — ACG 2023, the European ESGE 2021, and the British BSG — which converge on a defined position relative to colonoscopy:[1,6,7]

First — what "stable" means here

"Stable" means haemodynamically stable — normal/maintained blood pressure and heart rate after initial resuscitation (the BSG operationalises instability as a shock index >1, i.e. heart rate ÷ systolic BP >1, persisting despite resuscitation). Crucially, stability refers to vital signs/perfusion, NOT to whether bleeding has stopped — the guidelines specify "haemodynamically stable, with or without continued bleeding." So a patient can still be actively bleeding yet remain "stable" if their vitals are maintained; that patient still goes the colonoscopy route. "Unstable" means the vitals cannot be stabilised despite resuscitation.[1,7]

  • Unstable / ongoing significant bleeding → CTA is the first-line diagnostic test — you cannot safely prep and scope an exsanguinating patient, and CTA rapidly localises to direct embolization or surgery. ESGE makes this a strong recommendation: instability + suspected ongoing bleeding → CTA before endoscopic or radiologic treatment[1,6,7]
    Local note: in the absence of colonoscopy or interventional radiology — and particularly where blood products are limited — this unstable, actively-bleeding patient will likely require operative/surgical management, as the non-operative localisation and haemostasis options are unavailable and ongoing transfusion cannot be sustained. Localise by whatever means are available before theatre where possible (Section 6), involve senior staff early, and consider transfer if feasible.
  • Stable patient (vitals maintained) → colonoscopy is first-line (most non-urgent, within 14 days), whether or not bleeding is continuing. CTA is not a routine first test here — because CTA only detects active, brisk bleeding (≥0.3–0.5 mL/min) at the moment of the scan, and the stable patient has often slowed or stopped bleeding (most LGIB is intermittent and self-limiting), so a CTA would frequently be negative and unhelpful. Colonoscopy, by contrast, both diagnoses and treats and does not depend on the patient bleeding at that instant[1,5,6]
  • Exception — stable but actively, briskly bleeding: CTA can be considered first-line where suspicion of active bleeding is high. But once bleeding has subsided, CTA has little yield and colonoscopy is the right test[1,5]
  • CTA also has a role when colonoscopy fails to achieve haemostasis or cannot localise the source; if CTA is negative in an unstable patient, the guidelines advise considering upper GI endoscopy[1,6]

Accuracy: for active, ongoing bleeding, CTA is highly performant — pooled sensitivity ~85–90%, specificity ~92%, accuracy ~94–95% for detecting and localising overt GI bleeding.[1,5] The critical caveat: this depends on the patient actively bleeding at the moment of the scan. CTA detects bleeding rates of roughly ≥0.3–0.5 mL/min; if the bleeding has paused (as diverticular bleeding often does), the scan can be negative despite a real source. So a positive scan is very useful, but a negative scan does not exclude a bleed — its yield is highest when the patient is actively, briskly bleeding. (Reassuringly, ~80% with a negative CTA have no rebleeding and need no further urgent evaluation.)[1]

What a positive scan looks like: the direct sign is extravasation of intravenous contrast into the bowel lumen — a focus of contrast on the arterial phase that increases/changes in size and density on the delayed (portal venous) phase and may pool or track within the lumen. A triphasic protocol (non-contrast, arterial, portal venous) is used so this active extravasation can be distinguished from other hyperdensities.[5]

Local reality

We can obtain CT angiography at our regional hospital to localise the bleed — but angioembolization is not routinely available here and usually requires referral to the university hospital. The guideline pathway after a positive CTA is prompt referral to interventional radiology for transcatheter embolization;[1] in our setting that means a referral decision. So in practice CTA serves us mainly as a localisation tool: a positive scan guides whether to refer for embolization, or — where the patient is bleeding significantly and embolization is not accessible in time — directs a targeted segmental resection rather than a blind colectomy.

What this means locally

The 14-day equivalence for colonoscopy timing is genuinely helpful here: a stable patient whose bleeding has stopped does not need urgent colonoscopy, so the stabilise-and-arrange-colonoscopy pathway is evidence-concordant, not a compromise. CT angiography (where available) becomes the key acute test for the unstable, actively bleeding patient — and where neither CTA/embolisation nor colonoscopy can be obtained for ongoing significant bleeding, that pushes toward surgery (Section 6).

Why doesn't earlier haemostasis change mortality/surgery? Endoscopic therapy does work on a lesion you can see and treat — but the population benefit is diluted (most bleeds stop spontaneously), limited (an active target often isn't found — diverticular bleeding is intermittent and frequently already stopped), and not durable: colonic/diverticular rebleeding after endoscopic haemostasis is high (early rebleed ~20–24%, 1-year recurrence ~20–35%). Deaths are mostly driven by comorbidity, not exsanguination. So earlier scoping doesn't move the hard outcomes.[3,4]

🔑 Why earlier localisation still matters in our setting — it determines the operation

The trials showing "early colonoscopy doesn't change outcomes" assume that if bleeding continues, CT angiography and embolisation are available to localise it. Where those tools are not readily available, that assumption breaks — and the diagnostic yield of colonoscopy becomes the only localisation you may get before theatre. This changes the size of the operation:

  • Source localised → segmental resection — a targeted, smaller operation with lower morbidity and preserved colon
  • No localisation → a blind subtotal/total colectomy if the patient is exsanguinating and must be operated on — a bigger operation, greater physiological insult, and worse functional outcome. (Blind segmental resection is condemned — you risk leaving the true bleeding source behind and the patient rebleeds.)

So in our environment, the value of timely colonoscopy is not the hard-outcome benefit the trials looked for (and didn't find in well-resourced systems) — it is that localisation directly determines whether a patient who needs surgery gets a segmental resection or a colectomy. That is a strong, setting-specific reason to prioritise obtaining colonoscopy in the patient who is bleeding significantly and may come to theatre.

6
Local Pathway & Surgery — Lower Threshold

The practical pathway

  • Stable, bleeding stopped (the majority): stabilise, risk-stratify (Oakland), arrange colonoscopy — outpatient/private, or in-admission if facilitated. The 14-day equivalence supports this
  • Ongoing significant bleeding: CT angiography to localise (if available) → embolisation; if not available, early senior involvement

Lower surgical threshold

As with UGIB, when the safety nets of endoscopic/radiological haemostasis and ample blood are constrained, surgery moves up the algorithm earlier. Surgery is considered for the patient with severe ongoing LGIB who cannot be controlled or localised by available means, or whose transfusion requirement outstrips supply. Localise before resecting where at all possible — blind or segmental resection without localisation carries a high rebleeding/complication rate, so this is firmly a senior decision. The BSG advises that no patient should proceed to emergency laparotomy unless every effort has been made to localise the bleeding by radiological and/or endoscopic means, except in exceptional circumstances.[7]

The numbers — why localisation matters for the operation

The evidence makes the case for localising before resecting concrete:

OperationRebleedingMortalityComment
Directed segmental resection (source localised first)~4% (range 0–14%)~10%The goal. Morbidity falls from ~37% to ~8.6% when localisation guides the resection[11,12]
Blind segmental resection (no localisation)up to 63–75%up to 60%Condemned — you may leave the bleeder behind. Morbidity up to ~83%[11,12]
Subtotal colectomy (unlocalised fallback)~2%up to ~22% (some series to 33%)Controls bleeding but a bigger insult; morbidity ~37%, higher cardiac/renal complications and worse function, especially in the elderly[11,12]

Does it matter whether localisation is by colonoscopy or by CTA/angiography? No — the evidence treats them as equivalent for this purpose: localisation by either method enables a directed segmental resection, and what drives the better outcome is whether the source is confidently localised, not how. So in our setting, whichever localisation tool is available (colonoscopy or CTA) serves the same surgical purpose.[11,12]

So localisation converts a high-mortality, high-rebleed problem into a targeted operation with a ~4% rebleed rate. Where conventional localisation (colonoscopy/CTA) is unavailable, intra-operative localisation methods are worth knowing — including a Caribbean-described concept, the PEEP test (positive endoluminal erythrocyte presence). Its principle: because blood shows limited retrograde reflux up the colon, finding fresh blood that has reached the caecum (the most proximal colon) implies the bleeding source is at or proximal to that point — helping localise the bleed and direct a segmental resection rather than removing the whole colon. It involves no appendicostomy or appendicectomy. In the reported Trinidadian (UWI) series it guided successful segmental resection in 93% of cases with no mortality; if the assessment is equivocal, a blind total/subtotal colectomy (not blind segmental) is the safer default, with early completion colectomy if rebleeding occurs.[13] Surgeons intending to use this technique should read the primary paper for the exact intra-operative method of assessing caecal blood.

7
Escalation Triggers

Call senior staff

  • Haemodynamic instability — and consider/exclude an upper GI source (Section 2)
  • Ongoing significant bleeding needing CT angiography / embolisation / possible surgery
  • Transfusion requirement the blood supply cannot sustain
  • Rebleeding after initial stabilisation
  • Suspected colitis with systemic upset, or a mass/neoplastic cause needing planned work-up

As throughout, the early consent-while-stable conversation makes acting on these triggers safe and unhurried.

§

Ward Topic · Post-operative

Surgical Site Infection

CDC definitions · source control first · when to swab · when to give antibiotics
Core Message
Definitions
Recognise
Source Control
When to Swab
When to give Antibiotics
Local Caveat
Escalation
References
⚑ Core Principle The primary treatment of most surgical site infection is source control — open the wound and drain the pus, not antibiotics. Antibiotics are an adjunct for systemic or spreading infection, not a substitute for drainage, and not needed for every infected wound. A wound swab does not change the immediate management (which is drainage); it guides antibiotic choice when antibiotics are actually indicated. Getting these priorities the right way round is the commonest junior error in SSI.
★
The Two Common Errors
Common errorCorrect approach
Reaching for antibiotics first while leaving pus undrainedSource control first — open the wound and drain; antibiotics are an adjunct for systemic/spreading infection[1,2]
Swabbing every wound and treating whatever growsSwab selectively (purulent/opened wounds), interpret in context, and don't treat a contaminant in a well patient[2,3]
1
Definitions (CDC/NHSN)

An SSI is an infection related to an operation, occurring at or near the surgical incision, within 30 days of surgery (or within 90 days where an implant/prosthesis is involved). The CDC classifies SSI by depth:[1]

TypeInvolves
Superficial incisionalSkin and subcutaneous tissue of the incision only
Deep incisionalDeep soft tissues — fascia and muscle
Organ/spaceAny part opened or manipulated during surgery other than the incised layers (e.g. an intra-abdominal collection/abscess)

The depth matters because it changes management: superficial infections are usually handled by opening the wound at the bedside; organ/space infections often need imaging and image-guided or surgical drainage and carry the greatest morbidity.

2
Recognise
  • Local signs: increasing pain/tenderness, erythema, warmth, swelling, purulent discharge, wound breakdown/dehiscence
  • Timing: typically days 3–7 post-op (earlier, fulminant infection suggests an aggressive organism — see escalation)
  • Systemic signs: fever, tachycardia, malaise — these indicate the infection is more than local and shift you toward antibiotics ± imaging
  • Bloods: FBC, and inflammatory markers where available; blood cultures if systemic involvement/sepsis
  • Imaging: if the systemic picture outweighs the wound appearance (sick patient, very raised markers, but an unremarkable wound), consider an organ/space source — ultrasound or CT to look for a deep collection[2,5]

Red flags — not a simple wound infection

Severe pain out of proportion, rapidly spreading erythema, crepitus, skin discolouration/blistering, or systemic toxicity suggest necrotising soft tissue infection — a surgical emergency needing immediate senior involvement and debridement, not ward management.

3
Source Control — the Primary Treatment

Open the wound and drain the pus

For an incisional SSI with pus, the definitive treatment is to open the wound, release and drain the collection, remove sutures/clips as needed, and allow drainage — with wound packing and dressing as required. This is the single most important step and often resolves the infection without antibiotics at all.[1,2,5]

  • Remove sutures/clips over the infected segment to release pus
  • Explore gently for dead space, undermining, and deep collection
  • Pack/dress; plan regular dressing changes and healing by secondary intention
  • Organ/space collections need drainage too — usually image-guided percutaneous drainage or surgical drainage, not bedside opening (senior/radiology involvement)
  • Remove infected foreign material (e.g. mesh, non-essential lines) where it is the focus — senior decision[2,5]

"Where there is pus, let it out." Antibiotics cannot sterilise an undrained collection — drainage is the intervention that changes the course.

4
When to Swab — and Does It Change Management?

What the swab does — and doesn't — do

A wound swab/culture does not change the immediate management, which is drainage and source control. Its value is to guide or tailor antibiotics when antibiotics are indicated — i.e. it matters for the subset with systemic/spreading infection, or where empirical therapy is failing. It does not decide whether to drain (the wound appearance decides that), and a positive culture in a well patient with a drained wound does not by itself mandate antibiotics.[2,3]

When to take a culture

  • Purulent discharge present, or a wound deliberately opened/drained — send pus or tissue for culture & sensitivity[2,3,5]
  • Systemic infection / sepsis — culture the wound and take blood cultures[2]
  • Empirical antibiotics are failing, or the patient is immunocompromised / unusual organism suspected (consider fungal/atypical)[3]
  • Where antibiotics will be started for a spreading/systemic infection — swab before the first dose where feasible

How to take it (to make the result meaningful)

  • Sample pus or deep tissue, not the skin edges — avoiding the wound margins reduces contamination with skin flora that produces misleading growth[2]
  • A swab of intact, dry, simply erythematous skin is low-yield and prone to contaminants — don't routinely swab a wound that is not discharging

The practical rule

Swab the purulent or opened wound (from the depth, avoiding edges) — and do it when the result will actually influence antibiotic therapy. Don't swab every wound, and don't treat a culture result in isolation — treat the patient. The swab informs antibiotic choice; it does not replace drainage and does not, on its own, justify antibiotics.

5
When to Give Antibiotics

Antibiotics are an adjunct — for systemic or spreading infection

A localised incisional SSI that has been opened and drained, in a well patient, often needs no antibiotics — source control alone is sufficient. This is guideline-backed: the IDSA states that adjunctive systemic antibiotics are not routinely indicated after incision and drainage, but are beneficial where there is a significant systemic response.[2,4] The IDSA's quantified thresholds for adding antibiotics are:

  • Erythema/induration extending >5 cm from the wound edge
  • Temperature >38.5°C
  • Heart rate >110/min
  • WBC >12,000

More broadly, antibiotics are indicated when the infection is more than local:[2,4]

  • Systemic signs — fever, tachycardia, sepsis
  • Spreading cellulitis beyond the wound margins
  • Deep incisional or organ/space infection
  • Immunocompromise (e.g. diabetes mellitus, patients on chemotherapy, long-term corticosteroids or other immunosuppressants, HIV, chronic kidney or liver disease, malnutrition), prosthetic material, or significant comorbidity
  • Start empirically per local guidelines — the likely organisms depend on the surgery (e.g. skin flora/Staph for clean cases; coliforms and anaerobes for a laparotomy/colorectal wound)[2]
  • Then tailor to culture & sensitivity once available — this is where the swab earns its place
  • Consider MRSA cover per local prevalence/risk

Antibiotics without drainage for a collection is the classic failure mode — they will not clear undrained pus. Drain first; add antibiotics for the systemic/spreading component.

6
Local Adaptation — Culture Turnaround

Cultures are available — but results take a day or two

Wound cultures and Gram stain are available in our setting in appropriate cases, but the results often do not return within 24 hours — typically a day or two. This shapes how we use them:

  • Do not delay source control or empirical antibiotics waiting for the result. Open and drain the wound, and start empirical antibiotics (if systemic/spreading) immediately per local guidelines
  • Take the culture before the first antibiotic dose where feasible, so the delayed result is still useful for tailoring therapy
  • Use the result when it returns to de-escalate or switch — its value is in tailoring, not in the acute decision to drain or to start treatment

The slow turnaround reinforces the core principle rather than undermining it: management is driven by the clinical picture and source control, with the culture refining antibiotics after the fact. This is exactly why a swab does not change the immediate management.

7
Escalation Triggers

Call senior staff

  • Any suspicion of necrotising soft tissue infection — pain out of proportion, crepitus, rapidly spreading erythema, skin necrosis, systemic toxicity — surgical emergency
  • Sepsis or systemic deterioration
  • Organ/space infection — needs imaging and image-guided/surgical drainage
  • Deep incisional SSI with fascial dehiscence — risk of burst abdomen; do not manage on the ward alone
  • Infected prosthetic material/mesh, or failure to improve despite drainage and appropriate antibiotics
§

Ward Topic · Peptic Ulcer Disease

H. pylori Management

The single eradication protocol for bleeding ulcers, perforated ulcers, and uncomplicated PUD
Core Message
Test vs Empirical
Local Prevalence
Regimen
Test of Cure
Failure
References
⚑ Core Principle H. pylori eradication is the single most effective measure to prevent recurrent peptic ulcer disease and its complications. The protocol is the same whether the ulcer bled, perforated, or presented uncomplicated — this module is the single source of truth, cross-referenced from the Upper GI Bleeding and Perforated Peptic Ulcer protocols. Without eradication, ulcer recurrence reaches 60–80% within a year; with successful eradication it falls below 5%.
★
Why It Matters

Eradication prevents the next ulcer — and the next bleed or perforation

Most peptic ulcers are H. pylori- or NSAID-related. Eradicating the infection is more effective than acid suppression alone at preventing recurrence and recurrent complications.[1,2]

  • Without eradication: ulcer recurrence 60–80% within one year[1]
  • With successful eradication: ulcer recurrence <5%[1]
  • Separately, the risk of recurrent ulcer complications (rebleeding/re-perforation) is sharply reduced — e.g. rebleeding falls from ~33% (infection persisting) toward near-zero after eradication[2]
  • In a complicated ulcer (bleeding or perforation), confirmation of eradication is mandatory — a recurrent complication is likely if the infection persists[2,3]

This is not optional follow-up. Every patient with a peptic ulcer — bleed, perforation, or uncomplicated — gets an eradication course and a confirmatory test of cure.

1
Test First vs Treat Empirically

Two valid routes, chosen by access: (a) test first (biopsy at endoscopy — rapid urease test/histology — or a non-invasive test) and treat positives; or (b) treat empirically and confirm eradication afterwards.

🇯🇲 Firm C protocol — treat empirically, then confirm

Because H. pylori testing is not reliably available to us in hospital — and test sensitivity is reduced in an acute bleed (active blood plus the PPI already running, so a negative result during the bleed cannot be trusted) — our practical protocol is:

  • Start eradication empirically once the patient is tolerating oral intake — justified because virtually all complicated peptic ulcers are H. pylori- or NSAID-related, and the local background prevalence is high (Section 2)[2,3]
  • Confirm eradication after the regimen is complete (test of cure — Section 4). The step that must not be skipped[2,3]
  • If an intra-operative CLO test or biopsy/histology was positive, treat without waiting for any further test[2]

Where testing is readily available, testing first is equally valid. Either way: every ulcer patient gets a course, and every patient gets a confirmatory test of cure.

Start before discharge: begin the course once oral intake is tolerated rather than deferring to an outpatient visit that may not happen — this reduces recurrence risk during the vulnerable recovery window.[2]

2
Local Prevalence — supports empirical treatment

🇯🇲 Jamaican & Caribbean data

  • Jamaica — community seroprevalence ~70% (suburban study, n=202), rising with age: ~27% in children under 5, up to ~93% in those aged 45 and over[4]
  • Jamaica — earlier endoscopic study: ~55% of symptomatic adults H. pylori-positive[4]
  • Caribbean/Latin America — meta-analysis of 14 countries: ~69% in adults (≈48% in children/adolescents); Barbados blood donors ~70%[5]
  • A recent (2023) Jamaican histology-based study suggests prevalence may be declining — so it is not universal, which is exactly why confirming eradication afterwards remains essential[4]

With adult prevalence ~55–70% — and far higher in older patients, who are also those most likely to bleed or perforate — the pre-test probability in a complicated ulcer is high enough that empirical eradication is a reasonable, evidence-concordant local strategy. The test of cure then catches the minority who were negative or not eradicated.

3
Eradication Regimen (2024 ACG)

Avoid clarithromycin/levofloxacin-based regimens unless susceptibility is known

The 2024 ACG guideline removed standard clarithromycin triple therapy as a preferred first-line option because of rising clarithromycin and levofloxacin resistance.[6]

RegimenComponentsDurationEradication
Bismuth quadruple (BQT) — preferred first-linePPI BD + bismuth QDS + tetracycline QDS + metronidazole TDS/QDS14 days~85–90%[6]
Standard triple — only if bismuth unavailable & low resistancePPI BD + amoxicillin 1g BD + clarithromycin 500mg BD14 days~72% (lower where resistance high)[6]
Concomitant therapyPPI BD + amoxicillin 1g BD + clarithromycin 500mg BD + metronidazole 500mg BD14 days~89%[6]
  • Do not substitute doxycycline for tetracycline in BQT — it lowers eradication by 10–17%[6]
  • BQT is safe in penicillin allergy (no amoxicillin); take the PPI before meals[6]
  • Rifabutin triple therapy and vonoprazan/PCAB-based regimens are newer first-line alternatives where available[6]
  • Continue the PPI after the eradication course to complete ulcer healing — a deep/large or complicated ulcer needs ≥4–8 weeks of acid suppression (e.g. omeprazole 40mg, often BD for a complicated ulcer)[2,6]

⚑ Local resource caveat

Bismuth compounds may not be readily available in our setting. Where BQT is not achievable, standard triple therapy (PPI + amoxicillin + clarithromycin, 14 days) is the fallback — but its eradication rate is lower where clarithromycin resistance is high, which makes the confirmatory test of cure even more important. Where available, a concomitant four-drug regimen is an alternative to BQT.[6]

4
Test of Cure — Confirm Eradication

When and how to retest

  • Mandatory after a complicated ulcer (bleed or perforation) — symptom resolution does not prove eradication[2,6]
  • Timing: at least 4 weeks after completing antibiotics[6]
  • PPI washout: stop the PPI for at least 2 weeks before the test — a PPI on board causes false-negatives. Bridge symptoms with an H2-receptor antagonist if needed[6]
  • Which test: urea breath test (UBT) or monoclonal stool antigen test (SAT) — both non-invasive, sensitivity/specificity >90%. Do not use serology for test of cure — antibodies stay positive long after eradication[6]

Local adaptation: where the UBT is not readily available, the monoclonal stool antigen test is an accessible, accurate alternative. The disciplines are the same regardless of test: ≥4 weeks after antibiotics, ≥2 weeks off PPI.

5
If Eradication Fails
  • A positive test of cure = treatment failure. Re-treat with a different regimen, avoiding antibiotics used in the failed course[6]
  • If BQT was first-line, rifabutin triple therapy is a suggested salvage option[6]
  • After repeated failure, susceptibility testing (culture or molecular) guides therapy where available[6]
  • Reinforce NSAID/aspirin avoidance and adherence — both contribute to apparent failure and recurrence[2]
§
References
  1. Lanas A, Chan FKL. Peptic ulcer disease. Lancet. 2017;390(10094):613–624. doi:10.1016/S0140-6736(16)32404-7 — and Narayanan M, et al. Peptic ulcer disease and H. pylori. Am J Med. 2019;132(4):447–456. (H. pylori eradication and recurrence prevention — 60–80% recurrence without eradication, <5% with successful eradication)
  2. Laine L, Barkun AN, Saltzman JR, et al. ACG clinical guideline: upper gastrointestinal and ulcer bleeding.   3. MSD Manual (Professional): Helicobacter pylori Infection — and bleeding-ulcer test sensitivity literature. Ward Topic · Asplenia

Post-Splenectomy Management

Preventing OPSI · vaccination (elective vs emergency) · antibiotic prophylaxis · patient education
Core Message
Splenic Trauma Context
OPSI
Vaccination Timing
Vaccine Schedule
Antibiotic Prophylaxis
Education
References
⚑ Core Principle A patient without a functioning spleen is at lifelong risk of overwhelming post-splenectomy infection (OPSI) — uncommon but rapidly fatal (mortality ~40–70%). The three pillars of prevention are vaccination, antibiotic prophylaxis, and patient education. The single commonest failure is discharging an asplenic patient without setting these up — make it a fixed part of the post-splenectomy checklist.
★
The Three Pillars

Every asplenic patient needs all three

  • Vaccination — against the encapsulated organisms (pneumococcus, meningococcus, Hib) plus annual influenza (Sections 3–4)
  • Antibiotic prophylaxis — and a "rescue" antibiotic supply (Section 5)
  • Education — the patient must understand their lifelong risk and what to do when unwell (Section 6)

This applies to anatomical asplenia (splenectomy) and functional asplenia (e.g. sickle cell disease — see the sickle cell content in the Acute Cholecystitis protocol).

1
Splenic Trauma — Salvage vs Splenectomy

The spleen is one of the most commonly injured organs in blunt abdominal trauma. Management has shifted strongly toward splenic preservation where safe, precisely because of the lifelong OPSI risk that follows removal.

  • Non-operative management (NOM) is the standard for the haemodynamically stable patient — success rates ~85–95% overall, best in lower AAST grades[1]
  • Splenectomy is indicated for haemodynamic instability, failure of NOM, or a shattered spleen/hilar injury. NOM failure rises with injury grade (highest in AAST grade 4–5)[1]
  • Splenic preservation techniques (splenorrhaphy, partial splenectomy) and, where available, splenic artery embolization can salvage the spleen in selected higher-grade injuries

Local note

Where angioembolization and reliable critical-care monitoring for NOM are limited, the threshold to proceed to splenectomy in the unstable or higher-grade injury is lower. That makes this post-splenectomy protocol all the more relevant — if we remove more spleens, we must be rigorous about OPSI prevention.

2
Overwhelming Post-Splenectomy Infection (OPSI)
  • What it is: fulminant sepsis in an asplenic patient, often from a trivial-seeming start, progressing to shock and death within hours[2]
  • Risk: incidence ~0.2–0.4% per year, lifetime risk ~5%; highest in the first 2 years after splenectomy and in children, but the risk is lifelong[2]
  • Mortality ~40–70% — among the highest of any infection a surgical patient faces[2]
  • Organisms: encapsulated bacteria — Streptococcus pneumoniae (50–90% of cases), Neisseria meningitidis, Haemophilus influenzae type b[2]

Why it drives everything else

OPSI is the reason the spleen is salvaged where possible, the reason every splenectomy patient is vaccinated and counselled, and the reason an unwell asplenic patient is treated as a medical emergency until proven otherwise. A febrile asplenic patient gets immediate empirical antibiotics — do not wait.

3
Vaccination Timing — Elective vs Emergency

The key principle: vaccines work best when there is a ~2-week window separating them from the splenectomy, to allow an adequate antibody response.[3,4]

SettingTiming
Elective splenectomyVaccinate at least 14 days BEFORE surgery (do not delay surgery for it, but plan for the 2-week lead where possible)[3,4]
Emergency splenectomy (e.g. trauma)Vaccinate at least 14 days AFTER surgery — waiting the 2 weeks gives a better functional antibody response than vaccinating immediately. In practice, give before discharge if follow-up is uncertain[3,4]

The practical reality for trauma

Most of our splenectomies are emergencies. The evidence (trauma-patient RCTs) supports vaccinating at ~14 days post-op for the best response. But the competing risk is losing the patient to follow-up — if you cannot be confident they will return at 2 weeks, vaccinate before discharge rather than not at all. A vaccine given early is better than a vaccine never given.[3,4]

4
Vaccine Schedule
VaccineSchedule
PneumococcalPCV (conjugate — e.g. PCV20, or PCV15 then PPSV23 ≥8 weeks later). If only PPSV23 available, give it. Revaccinate with PPSV23 (booster) per schedule (e.g. a booster after 5 years)[3,4]
MeningococcalBoth MenACWY and MenB — both are needed. MenACWY 2-dose primary then boosters (e.g. every 5 years)[4]
Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib)Single dose if not previously vaccinated[3]
InfluenzaAnnual inactivated influenza vaccine for life — reduces secondary bacterial sepsis (not the live nasal-spray form)[4]

Vaccines can be co-administered at different injection sites. Exact products and revaccination intervals vary with what is locally available and with national schedule updates — confirm against the current schedule and what your pharmacy stocks. The principle is fixed: pneumococcal + both meningococcal + Hib + annual influenza, with pneumococcal and meningococcal boosters over time.[3,4]

5
Antibiotic Prophylaxis & Rescue

There are two separate antibiotic strategies, and they are easy to confuse — they are different things with different purposes:

(A) Daily prophylactic antibiotic — taken every day

A low-dose antibiotic taken every day to continuously reduce the risk of infection. The debate over how long to continue it applies to this daily prophylaxis:

  • Duration is genuinely debated. Many guidelines advise daily prophylaxis for at least the first 2 years (some say 3–5 years) — the period of highest OPSI risk — while others note the risk never fully disappears, so favour continuing longer[2,5]
  • Lifelong daily prophylaxis is advised for the highest-risk patients: children, those with prior OPSI, and the immunocompromised[2,5]
  • Agent: penicillin V (phenoxymethylpenicillin) 250–500 mg BD, or amoxicillin; in penicillin allergy, a macrolide (e.g. erythromycin/clarithromycin). Note penicillin does not reliably cover Hib — which is why vaccination matters[2,5]

(B) Rescue ("standby") antibiotic — taken only when unwell

This is not a daily medication and has no "duration" in the same sense. It is a course the patient keeps at home at all times (replacing it when used or expired) and takes immediately at the first sign of fever or feeling unwell, while making their way to hospital — a "pill-in-pocket" for suspected OPSI. Typical: co-amoxiclav (or amoxicillin) started at once.[5]

Every asplenic patient should have a rescue supply regardless of whether they are on daily prophylaxis — the two strategies are complementary, not alternatives. A patient may be off daily prophylaxis (e.g. years after splenectomy) yet must still carry a rescue antibiotic for life.

6
Patient Education & the Discharge Checklist

Before the asplenic patient leaves — fixed checklist

  • Explain asplenia and OPSI — lifelong risk, that a fever is not to be ignored, and the early warning signs
  • Vaccinations given or scheduled (and documented), with the booster plan
  • Prophylactic antibiotic prescribed, and a rescue antibiotic supplied with clear take-immediately instructions
  • Medical alert — advise a medical-alert bracelet/card stating asplenia
  • Travel advice — heightened risk with certain travel (e.g. malaria, animal bites — an animal bite needs prompt co-amoxiclav)
  • Notify the primary-care provider of the asplenic status and the vaccination/booster schedule for ongoing follow-up
  • Document all of the above in the discharge letter

The discipline that prevents deaths is making this a fixed, non-negotiable part of every splenectomy discharge — vaccination, prophylaxis + rescue antibiotic, education, alert identification, and primary-care handover. An asplenic patient who knows to act on a fever and has antibiotics to hand is far less likely to die of OPSI.

§
References

Ward Topic · Infective Colitis

Infective Colitis & C. difficile

From medical diarrhoea to surgical emergency — diagnosis, severity, treatment, and when to operate
Core Message
Causes
Diagnosis
Severity
Medical Rx
When It Turns Surgical
Recurrence & IPC
References
⚑ Core Principle Most infective colitis is a medical problem — the surgical team's job is to recognise the minority that is becoming a surgical emergency (fulminant colitis, toxic megacolon, perforation) and to operate before the patient is too unwell to survive it. The commonest lethal error is operating too late. C. difficile is the organism that most often crosses from ward diarrhoea to theatre.
★
Why a Surgical Team Cares About Diarrhoea

The job is to catch the surgical minority early

  • C. difficile progresses to fulminant disease in ~3–5% of cases — and that minority carries very high mortality[1]
  • Surgical patients are high-risk: recent antibiotics, abdominal surgery, advanced age, comorbidity
  • The decision that saves lives is timely colectomy — outcomes are dramatically worse once shock, multi-organ failure or perforation supervene[1,2]

A post-operative or post-antibiotic patient with new diarrhoea, rising white cell count and abdominal distension is a C. difficile patient until proven otherwise — and is a patient the surgical team must watch closely.

1
Causes of Infective Colitis
  • C. difficile — the key one for the surgical patient; almost always follows antibiotic exposure, disrupts colonic flora, toxin-mediated
  • Bacterial: Campylobacter, Salmonella, Shigella, enterohaemorrhagic/invasive E. coli, Yersinia
  • Parasitic: Entamoeba histolytica (amoebic colitis — relevant regionally; can mimic and can perforate), Schistosoma
  • Viral: CMV colitis (immunocompromised)

Don't anchor on infection

Infective colitis is a diagnosis of confirmation, not assumption. The mimics matter surgically: ischaemic colitis, inflammatory bowel disease (first presentation of UC can be fulminant), and neutropenic enterocolitis. A bloody diarrhoeal illness that isn't responding, or an unusual CT pattern, should prompt you to widen the differential rather than escalate antibiotics blindly.

2
Diagnosis

Who to test for C. difficile

  • Test only diarrhoeal (loose/unformed) stool, in a patient with clinically significant diarrhoea (≥3 loose stools in 24h) with no obvious alternative explanation[3]
  • Do not test formed stool and do not test as a cure — many people are colonised; testing the wrong patient produces false-positive "infection"[3]

How to test

  • A two-step algorithm is preferred: a sensitive screen (GDH antigen or NAAT/PCR) followed by a toxin assay to confirm active disease. PCR-positive but toxin-negative often = colonisation, not disease — interpret with the clinical picture[3]

Imaging in the unwell patient

  • CT abdomen/pelvis for the toxic or distended patient — looks for colonic wall thickening, pancolitis, ascites, megacolon, or perforation[2]
  • Plain abdominal film is a quick bedside surrogate for colonic dilatation where CT is delayed — colonic diameter >6 cm (caecum >9 cm) defines megacolon
  • Flexible sigmoidoscopy may show pseudomembranes but is not required for diagnosis and carries perforation risk in fulminant disease — use cautiously
3
Severity Staging (IDSA/SHEA)

Severity drives treatment — and the surgical team most needs to recognise the jump to fulminant.[4]

StageDefinition
Non-severeWBC ≤15,000 cells/µL and creatinine <1.5 mg/dL (<133 µmol/L)[4]
SevereWBC >15,000 cells/µL and/or creatinine ≥1.5 mg/dL (≥133 µmol/L)[4]
Fulminant (the surgical one)Severe CDI plus any of: hypotension/shock, ileus, or megacolon[4]

"Fulminant" is the word that should bring the surgeon to the bedside

Hypotension, ileus, or megacolon on a background of severe CDI = fulminant colitis. This patient needs ICU-level care, maximal medical therapy, and an early surgical review — not another day of watching.[2,4]

4
Medical Treatment

First, the universal steps

  • Stop the precipitating antibiotic wherever possible — this alone may resolve mild disease
  • Stop antimotility agents (e.g. loperamide) in significant colitis — they promote toxin retention and megacolon
  • Resuscitate, correct electrolytes, review all drugs (including PPIs), isolate the patient (Section 6)
StageFirst-line treatment
Non-severeFidaxomicin 200 mg BD × 10 days (preferred, lower recurrence) or oral vancomycin 125 mg QDS × 10 days. Metronidazole is now second-line — only if neither available[4,5]
SevereSame: fidaxomicin 200 mg BD or oral vancomycin 125 mg QDS × 10 days[4,5]
FulminantOral (or NG) vancomycin 500 mg QDS plus IV metronidazole 500 mg TDS. If ileus, add vancomycin retention enema (500 mg in ~100 mL saline PR QDS). Fidaxomicin is not recommended in fulminant disease (not studied)[4,5]

🇯🇲 Local resource note

Fidaxomicin is expensive and frequently unavailable — oral vancomycin is the practical first-line here and is highly effective. The key local point is using the enteral (oral/NG/PR) route for vancomycin — IV vancomycin does not treat colitis (it doesn't reach the colon lumen). IV metronidazole, by contrast, is useful in fulminant disease because it is excreted into the inflamed gut. Where oral access is blocked by ileus, the vancomycin enema is the workaround.[4,5]

5
When Colitis Turns Surgical

Indications for surgery

  • Colonic perforation or peritonitis — absolute[2]
  • Toxic megacolon not improving with maximal medical therapy[2]
  • Fulminant colitis deteriorating despite medical therapy — end-organ failure, rising vasopressor requirement[2]
  • Clinical deterioration over 24–48h of maximal therapy — do not wait for the patient to become unsalvageable[2]

Markers that should lower the threshold to operate

These predict high mortality and should prompt early surgical involvement before collapse:

  • Serum lactate ≥5 mmol/L and WBC >50,000 cells/µL — strongly associated with peri-operative mortality; operating before these is better[2,6]
  • Other adverse markers: age >65, vasopressor requirement, end-organ failure, marked lactate rise, immunosuppression[6]

The operations

  • Subtotal/total colectomy with end ileostomy (rectum preserved) is the standard of care — removes the disease burden and allows later reconstruction[2,6]
  • Diverting loop ileostomy + antegrade colonic lavage (with vancomycin) is a colon-preserving alternative in selected patients — comparable survival with lower morbidity in some series, but requires the colon to be salvageable (no perforation/necrosis)[2]

🇯🇲 Local note — early decision, early referral

With limited ICU capacity, the margin for "watchful waiting" in fulminant colitis is small. Decide early, resuscitate hard, and if the patient is deteriorating, operate or refer before lactate and white count reach the lethal range. Subtotal colectomy with end ileostomy is the safe, definitive operation when in doubt — it does not depend on advanced post-operative facilities the way a colon-preserving approach does.

6
Recurrence & Infection Prevention

Recurrence

  • Recurrence occurs in ~15–25% after a first episode and rises with each subsequent episode[4]
  • First recurrence: fidaxomicin, or a vancomycin tapered/pulsed course; bezlotoxumab (anti-toxin B monoclonal) may be added to reduce further recurrence in high-risk patients[4]
  • Multiple recurrences: consider faecal microbiota transplantation (FMT) after ≥2 recurrences treated appropriately[4]

Infection prevention & control

Spores are not killed by alcohol gel

  • Wash hands with soap and water after contact — alcohol hand rub does not kill C. difficile spores[3]
  • Contact precautions and single-room isolation with dedicated equipment
  • Antibiotic stewardship is the single most effective preventive measure — review every antibiotic, narrow and stop early
§
References

Ward Topic · Pleural Disease

Spontaneous Pneumothorax

Non-traumatic pneumothorax — the British Thoracic Society (BTS) symptoms-based approach, chest drainage, suction, and bottle systems
Core Message
Primary vs Secondary
Assessment
Management
Chest Drain & Suction
Bottle Systems
Recurrence & Surgery
References
⚑ Core Principle Spontaneous pneumothorax is air in the pleural space with no trauma. Modern management (2023 BTS) is driven by the patient's symptoms and physiology, not the size on the film — a major shift from the old size-based algorithm. Tension pneumothorax is the exception: it is a clinical emergency needing immediate decompression before any imaging.
★
The Shift to Symptoms-Based Management

What changed, and why it matters

  • The 2023 BTS guideline manages by symptoms and physiological compromise, not pneumothorax size[1,6]
  • A minimally symptomatic primary pneumothorax — even a large one — can be managed conservatively[1,2,6]
  • Intervention (aspiration or drain) is for those who are symptomatic or compromised, or who have a secondary pneumothorax[1]

The one thing that overrides everything: tension

Tension pneumothorax — respiratory distress, hypotension, tracheal deviation away from the side, raised JVP, absent breath sounds — is a clinical diagnosis requiring immediate needle decompression then a chest drain. Do not wait for a chest X-ray.[1,5]

1
Primary vs Secondary

The distinction drives how aggressively you treat — a secondary pneumothorax in a diseased lung is far less well tolerated.

TypeDefinition
Primary (PSP)No clinically apparent underlying lung disease. Classically tall, thin, young men, often smokers; due to rupture of apical subpleural blebs[2]
Secondary (SSP)Underlying lung disease present — COPD (commonest), asthma, prior TB, interstitial lung disease, Pneumocystis, lung cancer. The diseased lung tolerates it poorly, so the threshold to intervene and admit is lower[1,2]

Why the smoking and TB history matters here

Smoking sharply raises PSP risk and recurrence, and is the single modifiable factor. A history of tuberculosis — relevant in our population — can leave structural lung damage that converts what looks like a "primary" event into a secondary one. Always ask, and counsel cessation at every contact.

2
Assessment

Clinical

  • Sudden pleuritic chest pain and breathlessness; reduced expansion, hyper-resonance, reduced breath sounds on the affected side
  • Assess physiology first: respiratory rate, oxygen saturations, heart rate, blood pressure — these define "symptomatic/compromised" and drive the decision

Imaging

  • Erect chest X-ray is the standard first investigation (except in suspected tension — treat first)[1]
  • Size still gets measured (BTS: the interpleural distance — the rim of air between the lung edge and the chest wall — measured at the level of the hilum; ≥2 cm is "large"; the ACCP uses a different threshold — ≥3 cm apex-to-cupola — underlining that size cut-offs are arbitrary) but it informs rather than dictates — the decision is symptom-led[1,5]
  • CT is the most accurate where the film is ambiguous, to size complex/loculated collections, or to identify underlying disease[1]
  • Thoracic ultrasound can rapidly confirm/exclude pneumothorax at the bedside in trained hands

The 2 cm is the lateral rim of air between the lung margin and the inner chest wall, measured at the level (height) of the hilum — not a measurement taken at the hilum itself (the hilum is the medial lung root, marked above; it only sets the height at which you measure). ≥2 cm defines a "large" pneumothorax (≈50% by volume). In the 2023 approach this informs the decision — it does not override symptoms.

3
Management Pathway (2023 BTS)

Primary (PSP) — symptom-led

  • Minimally symptomatic / asymptomatic, any size: conservative management — observation, high-flow oxygen if admitted, review. Most resolve without intervention[1,2,6]
  • Symptomatic and suitable: needle aspiration first-line, or ambulatory management with a one-way valve device where the pathway and follow-up exist[1,5]
  • Not suitable for conservative/ambulatory, or aspiration fails: intercostal chest drain[1,5]

What "suitable" means — selecting for aspiration vs ambulatory management

"Suitable" is a deliberate clinical judgement with two layers — the patient and the system — plus explicit exclusions.

Patient is suitable when:

  • Haemodynamically stable and not significantly hypoxic — no tension, no severe breathlessness at rest
  • True primary pneumothorax — no significant underlying lung disease (an SSP is generally not suitable for ambulatory discharge)
  • Reliable and able to engage with a safety-net — understands warning signs, can return promptly if worse, and (for ambulatory) can manage the device

Ambulatory management additionally needs the system to be suitable: a purpose-made one-way-valve device available, staff trained in its use, and a reliable outpatient follow-up pathway to review the patient within days. Without those, ambulatory care is not suitable however good the patient.[1,5]

Generally NOT suitable for ambulatory / requires admission & drainage

Tension pneumothorax; significant hypoxia or breathlessness at rest; bilateral pneumothorax; secondary pneumothorax / significant underlying lung disease; haemodynamic instability; pregnancy or lactation; an unreliable safety-net or no access to follow-up. These are the patients for whom needle aspiration or a chest drain with admission is the safer route.[1,5]

How high-flow oxygen helps a pneumothorax resolve — nitrogen washout

Supplemental oxygen does not act on the trapped air directly. It works by changing the gas gradient between the pleural air pocket and the blood:

  • The air trapped in the pleural space is mostly nitrogen (~78%), which is inert and absorbed only slowly
  • Breathing a high concentration of oxygen lowers the partial pressure of nitrogen in the alveoli and therefore in the pulmonary capillary blood (the blood is "washed" of nitrogen)
  • This steepens the diffusion gradient for nitrogen to move out of the pleural space and into the blood, so the trapped air is reabsorbed faster — and the lung re-expands sooner
  • The key asymmetry: nitrogen is inert — once it enters the blood it is carried to the lung and exhaled for good. Any oxygen that drifts into the pocket is consumed by the tissues and never accumulates. So the pocket loses nitrogen permanently and only borrows oxygen briefly — its total pressure falls and it shrinks (it does not just swap one gas for another)

Magnitude & honesty about the evidence: high-concentration oxygen is often quoted as increasing the reabsorption rate up to ~fourfold versus breathing air — but that figure rests on a small 1971 study, and recent reviews note the overall evidence base is thin. The physiological rationale is sound and the intervention is cheap and low-risk, which is why it is recommended for admitted patients being observed. Caveat: in patients at risk of hypercapnic respiratory failure (e.g. COPD — i.e. many SSP patients), use controlled/titrated oxygen to target saturations rather than unrestricted high-flow.[1,2]

Secondary (SSP) — lower threshold

Because the diseased lung tolerates a pneumothorax poorly, SSP patients are generally admitted, given oxygen, and most will need aspiration or a chest drain rather than pure conservative management. Involve respiratory/senior staff early.[1,5]

🇯🇲 Local note

Ambulatory devices and reliable outpatient follow-up pathways are often not available to us. Where they aren't, the practical choice for a symptomatic patient is needle aspiration or a chest drain with admission, rather than ambulatory discharge. Conservative management of a minimally symptomatic PSP remains entirely valid and avoids an unnecessary procedure — but requires the patient can return readily if they deteriorate.

4
Chest Drain & Suction

The underwater seal — the principle

A chest drain works by an underwater seal: the drainage tube sits ~2 cm below the water surface, which acts as a one-way valve — air and fluid leave the chest and bubble out, but air cannot be sucked back in during inspiration.[3,4]

  • Insertion site: the "safe triangle" in the axilla (lateral border of pectoralis major, anterior border of latissimus dorsi, a line at the level of the nipple/5th intercostal space), just above the rib to avoid the neurovascular bundle[3]
  • Swinging: the fluid level in the seal tube rises and falls with respiration — confirms the drain is patent and in the pleural space
  • Bubbling: indicates an active air leak. Continuous bubbling = ongoing leak (or a bronchopleural fistula if persistent)[4]
  • Keep the bottle upright and always below the patient's chest — lifting it above chest level can siphon fluid/water back into the pleural space[4]
  • Never clamp a bubbling drain — it can produce a tension pneumothorax

Suction — start passive, escalate to active only on criteria

A chest drain is first run on passive underwater-seal drainage (no suction). Active suction is not applied routinely at insertion — it is escalated to only if the lung does not re-expand or an air leak persists. The key safety rule when suction is used: high-volume, low-pressure thoracic suction (≈ −10 to −20 cmH₂O; roughly 1–2 kPa). High-pressure wall suction applied directly is unsafe.[3,4]

When to convert passive (underwater seal) → active (suction-led) drainage

Add suction when, typically at ~48 hours after insertion, the drain shows either:

  • Persistent air leak — continued bubbling in the water-seal chamber at 48 h[3]
  • Failure of the lung to re-expand — incomplete re-expansion on chest X-ray despite a correctly sited, patent, swinging drain[3]

Why not sooner? Applying suction too early — especially to a lung that has been collapsed for some time, in a young patient, or a large pneumothorax — risks re-expansion pulmonary oedema (RPO). RPO is uncommon (<1% overall, but up to ~14% in some case series of large/chronic pneumothoraces) and the classically-quoted mortality is ~20% (from a 1988 review of 53 cases — the figure cited in the older BTS guidance); more recent data suggest it is more common but less often fatal than that classic figure implies, so ~20% is best read as a historical worst-case rather than today's expected rate.[2,7] Risk rises with a chronic (>72 h) and larger pneumothorax. Most primary pneumothoraces have no ongoing leak and resolve on seal alone, so early routine suction adds risk without benefit.[2,3]

Before calling it a "failure" and adding suction, exclude a mechanical problem: check the drain is not kinked, blocked, clamped, or dislodged (a non-swinging drain that isn't bubbling may simply be blocked or out of the pleural space). Where suction is started, build it up gradually and manage the patient where staff are experienced with chest drains. Secondary pneumothorax (COPD/fibrosis) tends to leak longer and may warrant earlier suction and senior/respiratory involvement.[2,3]

🇯🇲 Practical point — simple bottle systems

Our setups are typically simple bottle systems rather than digital units. When suction is escalated to, it is delivered through the system's suction port — but a bare two-bottle system has no built-in limit on the negative pressure transmitted to the pleura, which is exactly the problem the three-bottle (suction-control) system solves. Understanding how the bottles regulate (or fail to regulate) pressure is therefore essential before applying suction — see the suction-led bottle systems below.[4]

5
Chest Drain Bottle Systems — Illustrated

Three classical configurations. The one-bottle is a passive (gravity/seal) drain; the two- and three-bottle diagrams below are drawn as active, suction-led systems — because once you escalate to suction (per the criteria above), it is these configurations you are using. Understanding the bottles explains both how the drain works and where the dangers lie. In each diagram, blue = water, the long tube is the one whose lower end sits under water, and the red line traces the suction connection.

One-bottle system (compact pre-made unit)

A single bottle is both the collection chamber and the water seal. The tube from the patient runs to a length of tubing whose end sits ~2 cm under the water; air bubbles out through the seal and escapes via the vent. The compact pre-made disposable units we often use are essentially this — one chamber doing both jobs, with sterile water filled to a marked line.[4]

  • Pro: simplest, fewest connections, ideal for a pure pneumothorax
  • Limitation: as fluid drains in, the water level rises, the tube sits deeper, and it gets progressively harder to expel further air/fluid — so it is poor for large effusions/haemothorax[4]

Two-bottle system (generic — separate collection + seal)

The job is split: bottle 1 collects fluid, bottle 2 is the dedicated water seal. Because the draining fluid no longer raises the seal's water level, the seal depth stays constant and drainage doesn't become progressively harder — so this handles significant fluid (effusion/haemothorax) far better than the one-bottle.[3,4]

  • Pro: fluid and seal are separate, so the seal depth is stable and drainage is recorded accurately in bottle 1
  • Limitation: if suction is applied to the seal bottle's vent, there is no mechanism to limit the negative pressure reaching the pleura — for controlled suction you need the third bottle[4]

Three-bottle system (adds regulated suction control)

Adds a suction-control bottle after the seal. The mechanics rest on three separate tubes in this third bottle:

  • The connecting tube (from bottle 2 → 3) — carries the seal bottle's air across; its end sits above the water in bottle 3
  • The atmospheric tube — a separate tube open to the air at the top and submerged to a set depth in the water. This tube is the regulator.
  • The suction tube — connects the top of bottle 3 to the wall suction

How it limits the pressure: the wall suction pulls a vacuum on the whole closed system. The depth the atmospheric tube is submerged sets the maximum negative pressure that can reach the pleura. If the wall pulls harder than that depth of water, room air is simply drawn down the open atmospheric tube and bubbles up through the water — venting the excess vacuum. So the suction at the pleura can never exceed the water-column height, no matter how high the wall dial is set.[3,4]

  • Set the limit by the water column — e.g. a 15–20 cm submerged depth caps suction at ~15–20 cmH₂O. Gentle continuous bubbling in this bottle confirms suction is at the set level (it is not an air leak)[4]
  • The water level is the suction dial. The pressure the pleura experiences rises and falls with the depth the open tube is submerged — i.e. with the water level in bottle 3. Raise the water level → deeper submersion → more suction delivered to the patient; lower it → less. To change the suction the patient receives, you change the water level in the suction-control bottle — turning the wall suction up only makes it bubble more vigorously and does not increase what reaches the pleura[3,4]
  • Modern integrated disposable units (the single moulded "three-chamber" devices) build all three into one housing — same physics, one unit[4]

Can't I just regulate a two-bottle system with the wall suction setting?

Only unreliably — and that is the reason the third bottle exists. Wall suction regulators are designed for airway/NG suction and are often imprecise at the low pressures the pleura needs; a dial can be set wrongly, drift, or be turned up by someone unaware. The pressure shown at the wall is also not the same as the pressure delivered at the pleura once tubing and any air leak are accounted for — with an air leak the wall unit keeps pulling harder to hold its set vacuum. The three-bottle water column is a physical, self-limiting safeguard: the physics cap the pressure regardless of the dial. Regulating by the wall dial alone trusts a calibration; the water column makes the limit intrinsic.[3,4]

Safety essentials common to all systems

  • Keep the system upright and below chest level at all times
  • If suction is turned off, the suction tubing must be opened/disconnected to air — a closed suction line with no vent can build up pressure and cause a tension pneumothorax[4]
  • Never clamp a bubbling drain; if the drain disconnects, re-establish the underwater seal immediately
6
Recurrence & Surgery
  • Recurrence after a first PSP is common — roughly ~30%, most within the first year; risk rises with each subsequent episode[2]
  • Smoking cessation is the single most effective way to reduce recurrence[2]

When to involve thoracic surgery

  • Persistent air leak (typically >3–5 days) or failure of the lung to re-expand[1,2]
  • Second ipsilateral pneumothorax, first contralateral, or bilateral[2]
  • High-risk occupations/activities (divers, pilots) — often after a first episode
  • Operation: VATS bullectomy + pleurodesis (mechanical/chemical) to obliterate the pleural space and prevent recurrence; chemical pleurodesis alone is an option for SSP-recurrence prevention where surgery is unsuitable[1,2]

🇯🇲 Local note

VATS may require referral to a centre with thoracic surgical capability. Where a persistent air leak cannot be managed surgically on site, prolonged drainage ± chemical pleurodesis via the chest tube and timely referral are the realistic pathway. Decide early and involve senior staff.

§
