Ward Topic · Nutrition

Surgical Nutrition

Screening & assessment · who needs intervention · enteral & parenteral support · access techniques
Core Message
Why It Matters
Screening & Assessment
Who Needs Intervention
The Feeding Hierarchy
Enteral Nutrition
Parenteral Nutrition
Combined (SPN)
Access Techniques
Refeeding Syndrome
Algorithm
References
⚑ Core Principle Malnutrition is common in surgical patients and is an independent risk factor for surgical-site infection, anastomotic failure, and death — yet it is routinely missed because no one screened. The discipline is simple: screen everyone, identify who is at nutritional risk, feed early, and feed by the gut whenever the gut works. "If the gut works, use it."
★
The Four Questions

Every surgical patient, four questions

  • 1. Is this patient at nutritional risk? → screen with a validated tool (NRS-2002)
  • 2. Do they need intervention? → risk score + clinical context decides
  • 3. Which route? → oral → enteral → parenteral, in that order of preference
  • 4. How will I deliver it? → the access technique (tube, gastrostomy, jejunostomy, central line)

This module follows those four questions in order. The single commonest failure is never asking the first one.

1
Why It Matters
  • Severe malnutrition is an independent risk factor for 30-day mortality and surgical-site infection after elective colorectal or gastric cancer surgery[1]
  • In high-risk patients, adequate preoperative nutrition cuts complications — e.g. in gastrectomy for severe nutritional risk, SSI fell from ~45% to ~17% with ≥10 days of adequate energy support[1]
  • The patients most affected: upper-GI and pancreatic cancer, prolonged ileus, sepsis, the elderly, and anyone with a long pre-hospital history of poor intake

The surgical-specific stakes

Nutrition is not a "medical" afterthought on a surgical patient — protein-energy malnutrition directly impairs collagen synthesis and anastomotic healing, immune function (infection), and respiratory muscle strength (failure to wean, pneumonia). Feeding the malnourished surgical patient is part of the operation's success, not separate from it.[1]

2
Screening & Assessment — the tools

There is a deliberate two-step logic: screen everyone quickly to find those at risk, then assess/diagnose the positives in more depth.

ToolRole & what it uses
NRS-2002 (screening — preferred for surgery)The ESPEN-recommended screening tool for surgical patients. Combines impaired nutritional status (weight loss, low BMI, reduced intake) + severity of disease (metabolic stress) + an age adjustment (≥70 y adds a point). Score ≥3 = at nutritional risk; ≥5 = high risk[2]
MUSTMalnutrition Universal Screening Tool — simple community/ward screen (BMI + weight loss + acute disease effect). Good general screen; NRS-2002 is preferred in surgery[2]
MNAMini Nutritional Assessment — validated specifically in the elderly
GLIM (diagnosis)Global Leadership Initiative on Malnutrition — the international consensus diagnosis: a positive screen, then ≥1 phenotypic (weight loss, low BMI, reduced muscle mass) + ≥1 aetiologic (reduced intake, disease/inflammation) criterion. Robust, but leans on a scale/BMI and ideally body-composition measurement[3]
SGA (diagnosis — preferred locally)Subjective Global Assessment — clinician's structured history (intake, weight change, GI symptoms, function) + exam (muscle/fat loss, oedema). A strong bedside diagnosis and grading that needs no labs, no scale, and no body-composition kit — the practical choice in our setting

★ The most useful single tool for deciding who needs intervention

For the surgical inpatient, NRS-2002 is the practical answer — it is the tool ESPEN recommends for surgery, it explicitly builds in disease severity (which matters for the operated patient), and its threshold directly triggers action: a score ≥3 identifies the patient at nutritional risk who should have a nutrition plan; ≥5 marks high risk who benefit most from preoperative optimisation. Use SGA to confirm and grade the diagnosis where the screen is positive.[2]

Bedside assessment to record in every at-risk patient: unintentional weight loss (% over 3–6 months), BMI, recent oral intake, GI symptoms, functional status, and a focused exam for muscle/fat wasting and oedema. Albumin is a marker of inflammation/severity, not a reliable nutritional marker — do not use it to diagnose malnutrition.[2]

The mental model — screen, then confirm

Think of it like breast cancer: a mammogram screens the population to find who needs a closer look, and pathology confirms the actual diagnosis. Here, NRS-2002 is the screen (who is at risk?) and SGA confirms and grades the malnutrition you then act on. The screen is fast and sensitive; the diagnosis is more detailed and drives the plan.

Tool 1 — NRS-2002 (the screen)

Total score = nutritional-status score (0–3) + disease-severity score (0–3) + 1 if age ≥70. Range 0–7. For each of the two components, take the single highest-scoring row that applies.[2]

ScoreImpaired nutritional status (any one)Severity of disease (≈ metabolic stress)
1 — mildWeight loss >5% in 3 months, or food intake 50–75% of normal in the past weekHip fracture; chronic patient with acute complication (cirrhosis, COPD, chronic dialysis, diabetes, cancer)
2 — moderateWeight loss >5% in 2 months, or BMI 18.5–20.5 + impaired general condition, or intake 25–50%Major abdominal surgery; stroke; severe pneumonia; haematological malignancy
3 — severeWeight loss >5% in 1 month (>15% in 3 months), or BMI <18.5 + impaired general condition, or intake 0–25%Head injury; bone marrow transplant; ICU patient (APACHE >10)

Acting on the NRS-2002 score

≥3 → at nutritional risk: start a nutrition care plan. <3 → rescreen weekly (and consider a preventive plan if major surgery is scheduled). ≥5 → high risk — the group that benefits most from preoperative optimisation.[2]

Tool 2 — choosing the diagnostic tool: GLIM vs SGA

Why we use SGA in preference to GLIM here

GLIM is the international consensus diagnosis (positive screen, then ≥1 phenotypic — weight loss, low BMI, reduced muscle mass — plus ≥1 aetiologic — reduced intake, inflammation/disease). It is rigorous, but two of its phenotypic criteria depend on equipment we cannot rely on: BMI needs a working scale (often unavailable on the ward) and muscle-mass grading ideally needs body-composition measurement (BIA/DXA/CT).[3]

For that reason this protocol uses SGA as the working diagnostic tool: it reaches the same endpoint — confirm and grade malnutrition — using only a structured history and a hands-and-eyes examination, with no scale, labs, or body-composition kit required. The standard remains GLIM; SGA is our resource-appropriate route to the same decision.

Tool 2 (used here) — SGA (Subjective Global Assessment — bedside diagnosis, no labs)

A structured history + examination that classifies the patient into one of three grades. No bloods needed — ideal where labs/body-composition tools are limited.

HistoryExamination
Weight change (past 6 months & 2 weeks); dietary intake change; GI symptoms >2 weeks (nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea, anorexia); functional capacity; disease & metabolic demandLoss of subcutaneous fat (triceps, chest); muscle wasting (quadriceps, deltoids, temples); oedema (ankle/sacral); ascites
SGA gradeMeaning
AWell nourished (stable or improving)
BModerately / suspected malnourished (weight loss, reduced intake, mild wasting)
CSeverely malnourished (marked wasting, oedema, ongoing loss)

Put together: NRS-2002 ≥3 flags the patient → SGA confirms and grades → the grade drives how aggressively you intervene. Both are bedside tools needing no labs, scale, or body-composition kit — so the whole screen-to-diagnosis pathway works at the bedside in our setting. (GLIM remains the international-standard diagnosis where the equipment is available.)

3
Who Needs Intervention

Indications for nutritional support

  • At nutritional risk on screening (NRS-2002 ≥3), especially before major GI/cancer surgery[2]
  • Already malnourished (SGA-confirmed — grade B or C)
  • Cannot/will not meet needs orally — anticipated inadequate intake (e.g. <50% of requirements) for >5–7 days, or no oral intake expected for that long[2]
  • High-risk surgery in a depleted patient — upper-GI, pancreatic, prolonged or complicated course

Preoperative optimisation — when to delay surgery to feed

For severe nutritional risk, ESPEN supports delaying major elective surgery to give 7–14 days of preoperative nutrition, because the reduction in complications outweighs the delay. This is a balance against tumour progression and must be a senior, multidisciplinary decision — but the principle is that a brief, targeted prehabilitation window is justified in the severely malnourished.[1,2]

Targets (start point — individualise)

  • Energy: ~25–30 kcal/kg/day[2]
  • Protein: ~1.2–1.5 g/kg/day in the surgical/catabolic patient[2]
  • Build up to target over a few days; in high refeeding-risk patients start low (Section 8)
4
The Feeding Hierarchy — "if the gut works, use it"
1. Oral
normal/therapeutic diet, fortified food, oral nutritional supplements (ONS) — always first choice if safe to swallow and the gut works[2]
2. Enteral (tube)
if intake inadequate but the gut is functional and accessible — NG/NJ tube, or gastrostomy/jejunostomy for the longer term[2]
3. Parenteral
if the gut cannot be used or cannot meet needs — supplemental (alongside some enteral) or total (TPN)[2]

Why enteral over parenteral whenever possible

  • Maintains gut mucosal integrity and reduces bacterial translocation
  • Fewer infectious and metabolic complications, and far cheaper
  • More physiological — preserves gut-associated immune function

Even a small amount of "trophic" enteral feed to keep the gut working is valuable when full enteral feeding isn't yet possible. Parenteral nutrition is for when the gut genuinely cannot be used — not a default for convenience.[2]

Start early

Early oral feeding is preferred and should not routinely be withheld after GI surgery. Where oral intake won't meet needs, enteral nutrition should be started within 24 h rather than waiting.[1,2]

5
Enteral Nutrition

Routes (short vs long term)

  • Nasogastric (NG): first-line for short-term (<4 weeks) feeding when the stomach can be used and there is no high aspiration risk
  • Nasojejunal (NJ): when gastric feeding fails or is unsafe — gastroparesis, high aspiration risk, pancreatitis, or to feed past an upper anastomosis
  • Gastrostomy / jejunostomy: for long-term (>4 weeks) feeding — see Access Techniques (Section 7)

Practical points

  • Confirm NG position (pH aspirate ≤5.5, or X-ray) before feeding — a misplaced NG into the lung is lethal
  • Feed by continuous pump or bolus; sit the patient up to reduce aspiration
  • Polymeric feed for most; semi-elemental/peptide feeds for malabsorption

★ Step-by-step: initiating NG feeding (gut intact, e.g. the ICU patient)

A practical protocol

  • Step 1 — Confirm the tube. Check NG position (pH ≤5.5 or X-ray) before the first feed and after any displacement. Sit the patient up ≥30–45°
  • Step 2 — Choose feed & target. Standard polymeric feed (~1 kcal/mL); set the daily goal from ~25–30 kcal/kg and ~1.2–1.5 g/kg protein (Section 3)
  • Step 3 — Start. A common, safe start is continuous feed at ~20–30 mL/hr. Advance by ~10–20 mL/hr every 4–8 h as tolerated until the goal rate is reached (typically over 24–48 h). (In a stable, non-high-risk patient with an intact gut, starting at or near goal is increasingly supported — but a graded start is the safe default, and is preferred in refeeding risk.)[2,6]
  • Step 4 — Assess tolerance clinically every few hours: nausea/vomiting, abdominal distension or pain, bowel function. These — not a residual number — define intolerance[2,7]
  • Step 5 — If intolerant: slow or pause briefly, ensure the patient is sat up, review constipating/sedating drugs, and start a prokinetic (metoclopramide ± erythromycin) where aspiration risk or delayed emptying is the issue. Consider post-pyloric (NJ) feeding if gastric feeding repeatedly fails[2,7]
  • Step 6 — Refeeding check. In anyone starved for days, start low and slow and follow the refeeding protocol (Section 8) — give thiamine first, replace K/PO₄/Mg

Gastric residual volume (GRV) — what the evidence actually says

This is an area where practice has changed and a lot of older teaching is now out of date. The traditional habit of checking residuals every few hours and stopping feed at a low threshold (30 mL, 200 mL) is not evidence-based:

  • Elevated GRV does not correlate with aspiration, pneumonia, ICU/hospital length of stay, or ventilation duration[7]
  • GRV measurement is inaccurate (varies with tube bore, position, syringe, patient posture) and ~80% of the time a high GRV is an isolated event; it is the commonest reason feeds get stopped and goal is never reached[7]
  • ASPEN/SCCM: do not use GRV as part of routine care. If it is used at all, do not hold feed for GRV <500 mL in the absence of other signs of intolerance. (ESPEN: delay only if GRV >500 mL.)[7]
  • Removing GRV monitoring and starting at goal led to more nutrition delivered, less prokinetic use, and fewer GI complications[7]

Bottom line for your question: the residual number does not reliably tell you whether the patient is "absorbing." Judge tolerance clinically — vomiting, distension, pain. If you do measure GRV (e.g. limited monitoring in a high-aspiration-risk patient), use a 500 mL threshold, not 30 or 200 mL, and don't stop feeding for an isolated high value. The old low-threshold practice mainly causes underfeeding.[7]

Common complications

Tube: misplacement, blockage, displacement. GI: diarrhoea (commonest — review feed rate/osmolarity, medications, infection), nausea, high gastric residuals. Metabolic: hyperglycaemia, electrolyte shifts, refeeding syndrome (Section 8). Aspiration pneumonia — head up, check residuals, consider post-pyloric feeding.

Feeding patterns — continuous, cyclical/overnight, intermittent, bolus

PatternWhat it is & when
ContinuousPump-delivered over ~24 h at an hourly rate. Best tolerated when intake is marginal; mandatory for post-pyloric (NJ/jejunostomy) feeding, where boluses cause cramps/dumping[2,6]
Cyclical / overnightPump-delivered over a shorter window — classically overnight while the patient sleeps — freeing the day for oral intake, therapy and mobility. Useful to top up a patient who eats some but not enough[6]
IntermittentOver 20–60 min every 4–6 h, with or without a pump
Bolus200–400 mL by syringe over 15–60 min, 4–6 times daily — gastric tubes only; more physiological, no pump needed[6]

🇯🇲 Local context — NG feeding (incl. around-the-clock) as the practical mainstay

This is an important point for our setting, especially the elderly post-emergency or post-elective surgical patient who needs support. Where TPN is costly, intermittently available, and limited, the answer is usually not to ration parenteral nutrition — it is to use the gut harder and better, because enteral feeding is already the evidence-preferred, more physiological, cheaper, and safer route. NG feeding is not a second-best substitute for TPN; it sits above it in the hierarchy.

  • Around-the-clock / overnight continuous NG feeding is a described, guideline-endorsed approach — a slow pump infusion, including while the patient sleeps, to accumulate the daily target a frail patient cannot manage by mouth[2,6]
  • The continuous-vs-intermittent choice is tolerance-driven, not outcome-driven — a 2025 review of 17 RCTs found no significant difference in aspiration, mortality or length of stay (continuous had slightly more constipation). So choose by what the patient tolerates and what equipment you have[6]
  • Practical kit: a feeding pump gives precision, but where pumps are scarce, gravity-drip continuous or intermittent/bolus gastric feeding achieves the same target — the principle (use the gut) matters more than the device
  • Combine with oral intake (eat by day, feed by night) and add oral nutritional supplements — reserve PN for genuine intestinal failure

So your instinct is correct and evidence-backed: for the depleted elderly surgical patient where TPN is impractical, continuous/overnight NG feeding via a gastric tube is the right first-line approach, not a compromise. It is cheaper, more available, more physiological, and supported by the guidelines.[2,6]

6
Parenteral Nutrition (PN / TPN)

Indications — when the gut cannot be used

  • Intestinal failure — short bowel, high-output fistula, prolonged ileus, bowel obstruction, severe malabsorption
  • Gut inaccessible or unsafe to feed — and needs not met by enteral route within ~5–7 days
  • Supplemental PN — added when enteral feeding cannot reach the target[2]
  • Central vs peripheral: standard hyperosmolar TPN needs a central line (PICC or central venous catheter); lower-osmolarity formulations can run peripherally short-term
  • Composition: an "all-in-one" bag of glucose, amino acids, lipid, electrolytes, vitamins and trace elements — usually with pharmacy/dietitian input

Complications — why PN is not the easy option

  • Catheter-related: bloodstream infection (CRBSI) — the major hazard — plus pneumothorax/thrombosis at insertion. Strict aseptic line care; a dedicated lumen for PN
  • Metabolic: hyperglycaemia, electrolyte derangement, refeeding syndrome, hypertriglyceridaemia
  • Hepatobiliary: deranged LFTs, steatosis, and with long-term PN, intestinal-failure-associated liver disease and gallstones (gut disuse)

🇯🇲 Local note

TPN is resource-intensive — pharmacy compounding, central access, biochemical monitoring, and line-care expertise. Where these are limited, the case for preserving and using the gut is even stronger, and PN should be reserved for genuine intestinal failure with senior/multidisciplinary oversight. Plan central access and monitoring before starting.

6b
The Combined Approach — Supplemental Parenteral Nutrition

The routes are not mutually exclusive

A patient who is eating (or enterally fed) but cannot reach target does not have to be switched all-or-nothing to parenteral nutrition. You can keep the oral/enteral intake going and top up the deficit with parenteral nutrition — this is supplemental parenteral nutrition (SPN), and it is evidence-supported.[2,5]

This is exactly the situation of, for example, a patient with an entero- or colocutaneous fistula who can take an oral diet but stays depleted because intake is inadequate — poor appetite, fatigue, low motivation, or the disease itself. Rather than wait for them to fail, you preserve and use whatever gut function they have and make up the shortfall intravenously.

When to add SPN — the threshold

  • ESPEN (surgery): if oral + enteral intake cannot meet requirements (<50% of caloric requirement) for >7 days, add parenteral to the enteral — a combined approach[2]
  • ASPEN: in the patient at nutritional risk with insufficient intake (<60% of energy + protein requirement), initiate PN sooner — within 3–5 (to 7–10) days[5]
  • The practical rule: if a genuinely depleted patient is persistently and substantially below target despite optimising oral/enteral intake, SPN is indicated — don't let them coast in a chronic deficit[2,5]

Evidence — and an honest nuance

In high-nutritional-risk patients after major abdominal surgery with poor enteral tolerance, a randomised trial found early SPN (added to enteral) reduced nosocomial infection versus late SPN, and a 2022 meta-analysis found combining enteral + parenteral improved nutritional intake without worse outcomes.[5] The nuance: in the general critically-ill ICU population the timing is debated — one large trial (EPaNIC) suggested harm from very early SPN, while others (TOP-UP) found no added infection risk — so SPN is targeted at the genuinely depleted/high-risk patient who is under-target, not applied reflexively to everyone.[5]

How to run it

  • Maximise the gut first — optimise oral intake (ONS, fortify, treat nausea, address the reasons for poor intake) and enteral feed as far as tolerated
  • Calculate the deficit — target minus what they are actually absorbing — and provide that as PN, rather than full TPN
  • Taper the PN down as oral/enteral intake improves, and stop it once the gut alone meets target — SPN is a bridge, not a destination
  • Watch for overfeeding when counting both routes together, and for refeeding syndrome in the depleted patient (Section 8)

🇯🇲 Note for the fistula patient

In a high-output entero-/colocutaneous fistula, the combined approach is often the realistic path: use the gut where it is safe and absorptive, supplement intravenously to cover losses and the deficit, and manage fluid/electrolyte losses alongside. Where TPN resources are limited, even partial parenteral support added to maximised oral/enteral intake is better than leaving the patient in deficit — and reserves full TPN for when the gut genuinely cannot be used. Senior/multidisciplinary input.

7
Access Techniques — Gastrostomy, Jejunostomy, Central Line

Gastrostomy — feeding into the stomach (long-term)

  • When: long-term (>4 weeks) enteral feeding with a functional stomach and acceptable aspiration risk — e.g. neurological dysphagia, head & neck cancer, prolonged inability to swallow
  • How: PEG (percutaneous endoscopic gastrostomy) — endoscopic, the usual first choice where endoscopy is available; radiologically-inserted (RIG); or surgical/open (Stamm) gastrostomy when endoscopy is not possible or the abdomen is already open
  • Complications: wound/site infection, tube dislodgement, buried bumper, peritoneal leak; rare colonic injury at insertion

Jejunostomy — feeding past the stomach (long-term, post-pyloric)

  • When: feeding is needed distal to the stomach/duodenum — after major upper-GI surgery (oesophagectomy, gastrectomy, pancreatoduodenectomy), gastric outlet obstruction, high aspiration risk, or to feed beyond a proximal anastomosis or leak
  • How: surgical feeding jejunostomy (often placed at the time of the index operation — e.g. a Witzel tunnel), or percutaneous/endoscopic (PEJ / direct PEG-J)
  • Practical: the jejunum tolerates only continuous, lower-rate feeding (no reservoir like the stomach) — bolus feeding causes cramps/dumping. Complications: tube displacement/blockage, leak, and rarely small-bowel obstruction or volvulus around the fixation point

Choosing the route — the logic

Gut works + short term → nasal tube (NG or NJ). Gut works + long term + safe to feed the stomach → gastrostomy. Gut works + long term but must feed beyond the stomach (aspiration risk, upper-GI surgery/anastomosis) → jejunostomy. Gut does not work → parenteral. The deciding questions are always: does the gut work, for how long will I need access, and can I safely feed the stomach?[2]

Central venous access — for TPN

  • PICC (peripherally-inserted central catheter) or a tunnelled/non-tunnelled CVC; tip in the SVC. Use a dedicated lumen for PN and strict aseptic care to prevent CRBSI
8
Refeeding Syndrome

The danger of feeding the starved patient too fast

In a chronically starved patient, reintroducing carbohydrate drives an insulin surge that pushes phosphate, potassium and magnesium into cells — causing potentially fatal hypophosphataemia, hypokalaemia and hypomagnesaemia, fluid shifts, and arrhythmias. Hypophosphataemia is the hallmark.[4]

Who is at high risk (NICE)

One or more of: BMI <16; unintentional weight loss >15% in 3–6 months; little/no intake >10 days; low baseline potassium, phosphate or magnesium before feeding. Or two or more of: BMI <18.5; weight loss >10%; little/no intake >5 days; history of alcohol or drugs (insulin, chemotherapy, diuretics, antacids).[4]

Prevention & management

  • Start low and go slow — begin at ~10 kcal/kg/day in high-risk patients (lower, ~5 kcal/kg/day, in the extreme), building up over ~4–7 days[4]
  • Thiamine 100 mg (and B-vitamins) before and during feeding — give before the first feed[4]
  • Check and replace potassium, phosphate, magnesium — low baseline levels should not delay feeding; correct in parallel[4]
  • Monitor electrolytes daily through the first week; monitor fluid balance

This is the one nutrition emergency a surgical team must not miss — it is iatrogenic and preventable. Any patient who has eaten little or nothing for days is at risk the moment you start feeding, by any route (oral, enteral, or parenteral).[4]

⎇
Clinical Algorithm

A bedside summary of the pathway above — screen, confirm, then feed by the most physiological route the patient can use, escalating only as needed.

This algorithm is a navigational summary; the detail, thresholds and evidence are in the sections above. Senior/dietitian input for complex cases.

Trauma · Assessment

Trauma — Primary Survey (xABCDE)

ATLS 11th edition · the assessment phase · find and treat what kills first
Core Message
Before the Patient
x — Hemorrhage
A — Airway
B — Breathing
C — Circulation
D — Disability
E — Exposure
Adjuncts & Re-assess
References
⚑ Core Principle The primary survey is a rapid, ordered hunt for what kills first — done in sequence, treating each life threat as you find it before moving on. ATLS 11th edition formalises a key shift: massive external haemorrhage is now addressed first, ahead of the airway. The mnemonic is xABCDE. The order is the priority — it ranks injuries by how quickly they kill.
★
The Core Idea — order = priority

xABCDE — and why it is in this order

  • x — exsanguinating haemorrhage: catastrophic external bleeding kills in minutes — control it first[1,2]
  • A — Airway (with C-spine protection): an obstructed airway kills in minutes
  • B — Breathing: failure of oxygenation/ventilation kills next
  • C — Circulation: shock from ongoing bleeding
  • D — Disability: neurological status / brain injury
  • E — Exposure: fully expose, find hidden injuries, prevent hypothermia

Work through it in order, and if the patient deteriorates, go back to the start (x, then A) and reassess. Treat each threat as you find it — do not move to B until A is secured.[1]

What changed in ATLS 11 (2025)

The traditional ABCDE became xABCDE — putting control of exsanguinating external haemorrhage before the airway. The change is drawn from military (MARCH / TCCC) experience, because massive external bleeding kills faster than airway obstruction and is a leading cause of preventable trauma death. The edition also emphasises damage-control resuscitation — early blood products, less crystalloid, and permissive hypotension (Circulation).[1,2,3]

1
Before the Patient Arrives — preparation & triage
  • Team & roles: assign airway, circulation/access, scribe, team leader; the leader stands back and directs
  • PPE — universal precautions for every trauma
  • Prepare kit — airway equipment, warmed IV fluids/blood, monitoring, suction, chest-drain set, tourniquets, warming
  • Triage: prioritise by severity and by resources available — a single critical patient versus a mass-casualty situation are handled differently
  • Handover (current ATLS format): S-xABCDE-BAR — a structured trauma handover (Situation → xABCDE findings → Background, Assessment, Recommendation)[1]

🇯🇲 Local note

Preparation matters most where resources are finite: have tourniquets and a clear massive-transfusion / blood-access plan ready before arrival, know your nearest definitive-care and imaging pathway, and pre-assign roles so the resuscitation is coordinated even with a small team.

x
x — Exsanguinating Haemorrhage (control bleeding first)

Why it comes first

Catastrophic external haemorrhage — a spurting limb wound, a mangled extremity, a junctional bleed — can exsanguinate a patient in minutes, faster than an obstructed airway. ATLS 11 places its control before A. If you see torrential external bleeding, stop it now, then proceed to the airway.[1,2]

How — in escalating order

1. Direct pressure
firm, sustained manual pressure on the wound
2. Tourniquet
for a limb bleed not controlled by pressure — apply high and tight, note the time[2]
3. Wound packing
haemostatic or plain gauze packed into junctional wounds + pressure
4. Pelvic binder
for suspected pelvic-fracture haemorrhage

This step is only for compressible, exsanguinating external haemorrhage. Internal/cavity bleeding is addressed under Circulation and definitive surgery — but external catastrophic bleeding you can see and compress is dealt with immediately.[2]

A
A — Airway with C-spine protection

Assess

  • Can the patient talk? A normal voice means a patent airway and adequate breathing for that moment. Talk to them
  • Look/listen/feel for obstruction: stridor, gurgling, snoring, agitation, accessory muscle use, see-saw breathing, cyanosis
  • Causes in trauma: reduced consciousness (tongue), blood/vomit/secretions, foreign body, direct facial/laryngeal injury, expanding neck haematoma

Act

  • Manual manoeuvres: chin lift / jaw thrust (jaw thrust preferred with C-spine concern)
  • Suction; remove visible foreign body
  • Adjuncts: oropharyngeal (if no gag) or nasopharyngeal airway
  • Definitive airway (intubation) for: failure to maintain/protect the airway, GCS ≤8, impending obstruction (burns/expanding haematoma), or need for ventilation. Plan for a surgical airway if intubation fails

C-spine

Assume a cervical-spine injury in any significant blunt trauma. Maintain in-line immobilisation during airway work. ATLS 11 favours spine motion restriction rather than rigid prolonged backboard use — protect the spine while avoiding the harms of a hard board.[1,3]

B
B — Breathing & ventilation

Airway open ≠ breathing adequate. Expose the chest and assess oxygenation and ventilation. Give high-flow oxygen.

Assess — look, feel, listen, percuss

  • Respiratory rate, effort, symmetry of chest movement, SpO₂
  • Tracheal position, neck veins, chest wall (wounds, flail, bruising), percussion note, breath sounds

Immediately life-threatening chest injuries to find NOW

  • Tension pneumothorax — hypotension + absent breath sounds + hyperresonance ± tracheal deviation → immediate decompression, do not wait for an X-ray
  • Open pneumothorax (sucking chest wound) → three-sided occlusive dressing + chest drain
  • Massive haemothorax → large-bore chest drain + resuscitation/blood; surgery for ongoing loss
  • Flail chest / pulmonary contusion → oxygen, analgesia, support ventilation
  • Cardiac tamponade (technically circulation, found here) → muffled sounds, distended neck veins, hypotension → senior help, eFAST, drainage

These are diagnosed clinically and treated immediately — they are the conditions that kill at "B" before any imaging.[1]

C
C — Circulation (with haemorrhage control)

Assess for shock — assume haemorrhagic until proven otherwise

  • Pulse rate/character, BP, capillary refill, skin colour/temperature, conscious level
  • Hypotension in adult trauma = systolic <90 mmHg — but tachycardia and poor perfusion precede a falling BP, so don't wait for hypotension[2]
  • Find the bleeding: "blood on the floor and four more" — external; chest; abdomen; pelvis; long bones. Use eFAST and pelvic/chest X-ray as adjuncts

Act — damage-control resuscitation (ATLS 11)

  • Two large-bore IV cannulae (or IO); send bloods + crossmatch
  • Early blood products in balanced ratios; minimise crystalloid — large-volume saline worsens coagulopathy[2,3]
  • Permissive hypotension until surgical control (caution / avoid in traumatic brain injury, where perfusion must be maintained)[2,3]
  • Tranexamic acid (TXA) early — ideally within 1 hour, and within 3 hours of injury[2]
  • Stop the bleeding: definitive control is surgery / interventional radiology — resuscitation buys time, it is not the treatment
  • Keep the patient warm — hypothermia worsens the lethal triad (hypothermia, acidosis, coagulopathy)
D
D — Disability (neurological)
  • GCS (eyes/verbal/motor, /15); note the best motor response and any change over time
  • Pupils — size, equality, reaction (a blown pupil suggests a mass lesion / herniation)
  • Lateralising signs; gross limb movement
  • Check glucose — hypoglycaemia mimics injury; and remember hypoxia and hypotension are the commonest reasons a trauma patient is confused — fix A, B, C before blaming the brain

GCS ≤8

A GCS of 8 or less means the patient cannot protect their airway → return to A and secure a definitive airway. This is the loop-back in action: D can send you straight back to the start.[1]

E
E — Exposure & environment
  • Fully undress the patient to find every injury — wounds, deformities, bruising on the back (log-roll with spine control)
  • Prevent hypothermia — warm blankets, warmed fluids, raise the room temperature; cover the patient again once examined
  • This completes the primary survey — proceed to adjuncts and reassessment, then the secondary survey when the patient is stabilising

Exposure is a balance: you must see everything, but a cold trauma patient bleeds worse. Expose, examine, then re-cover and keep warm.

2
Adjuncts & Reassessment

Adjuncts to the primary survey

  • Monitoring — ECG, SpO₂, BP, capnography; urinary catheter (output) and gastric tube unless contraindicated
  • Imaging — eFAST, chest and pelvic X-ray; bloods including lactate/base deficit and crossmatch
  • Analgesia and TXA as indicated

Reassess — the survey is a loop, not a line

Reassess continuously, and any deterioration sends you back to the start (x, then A). The patient who drops their saturations after you "finished" B has a problem at B again. Only move to the secondary survey (head-to-toe examination + AMPLE history) once the primary survey is complete, resuscitation is underway, and vital signs are responding.[1]

AMPLE history: Allergies · Medications · Past history/Pregnancy · Last meal · Events/Environment of injury.

§
References
  1. American College of Surgeons, Committee on Trauma. Advanced Trauma Life Support (ATLS) Student Course Manual, 11th ed. Chicago: ACS; 2025 — and ACS Brief, "Trauma Care Gets Major Upgrade with Launch of ATLS 11" (Sept 16, 2025). (primary survey refined to xABCDE; exsanguinating external haemorrhage controlled before airway; spine motion restriction; structured handover S-xABCDE-BAR; secondary survey after primary complete and vitals responding; GCS ≤8 → definitive airway)
  2. Advanced trauma life support 2025: a brief review of updates. Injury. 2026;57(4):113079 — with the xABCDE / MARCH / TCCC literature. (xABCDE rationale — massive external haemorrhage a leading preventable trauma death, kills faster than airway; tourniquets, wound packing, pelvic binder; damage-control resuscitation — early balanced blood products, reduced crystalloid, permissive hypotension; TXA ideally <1 h and within 3 h; hypotension defined as SBP <90 mmHg)
  3. Galvagno SM, et al., and contemporary trauma-resuscitation reviews on damage control resuscitation and permissive hypotension (e.g. Scand J Trauma Resusc Emerg Med; EAST/WSES trauma guidance). (corroborates early transfusion over crystalloid, permissive hypotension with TBI caveat, and spine-motion-restriction practice)

Built on ATLS 11th edition (2025). This is a teaching summary of the assessment phase — it does not replace formal ATLS certification. Further trauma topics (secondary survey, specific injuries, damage-control surgery) to follow.

Acute Presentation · Emergency Department

Infected Diabetic Foot

Emergency management · sepsis & glycaemic control · resident quick reference
Triage
DKA
Sepsis 6
Glycaemic
Antibiotics
Surgical
Escalation
Bloods
References
⚠ First 10 minutes Establish whether the patient is septic, in DKA, or both. These run in parallel — do not let glucose management delay sepsis source control, and do not let the foot distract from DKA resuscitation.
0
Rapid Triage — Septic or DKA?
AssessDKASepsis
Glucose>11 + ketonesOften >14, variable
KetonesPositive (>1.5 blood)Usually negative
pH / HCO₃pH <7.3, HCO₃ <15Normal unless late
HaemodynamicsMay be stable earlyTachycardia, hypotension
PathwayStep 1AStep 2 (Sepsis 6)

Both can co-exist — run the DKA protocol and Sepsis 6 simultaneously when so.

1
If DKA is Present

DKA = glucose >11 + ketones >1.5 (blood) or 2+ urine + pH <7.3 or HCO₃ <15.

Fluid

Lactated Ringer's (Hartmann's): 1 L first hour, then 1 L over 2 h, then 1 L over 4 h, then reassess. Adjust if cardiac/renal compromise — discuss with senior staff.

Insulin

Fixed-rate IV insulin infusion 0.1 units/kg/hour (soluble insulin in 0.9% NaCl). Start after fluids. No bolus. No sliding scale in DKA.

Potassium

Once K⁺ known & urine output established: K⁺ 3.5–5.5 → add 40 mmol/L; K⁺ <3.5 → senior review urgently; K⁺ >5.5 → no KCl yet. Recheck every 2 h.

Targets / resolution

Ketones <0.6, venous pH >7.3, HCO₃ >15, glucose 6–12. Transition to SC basal-bolus with 30–60 min overlap. Consult senior before transition.

Warning: If glucose <14 before ketones clear, add 10% dextrose at 125 ml/hour alongside the crystalloid. Do NOT reduce insulin rate. Avoid glucose falling >5 mmol/L/hour (cerebral oedema risk).

2
Sepsis 6 — within 1 hour
  • Blood cultures — before antibiotics, ≥2 sets
  • Wound / deep tissue cultures — guides de-escalation
  • IV antibiotics — see Section 4
  • IV fluid bolus — 500 ml lactated Ringer's over 15–30 min if hypotensive or lactate >2
  • Serum lactate — >4 = severe sepsis, call senior staff immediately
  • Urine output — catheterise, target >0.5 ml/kg/hour
3
Glycaemic Control (Non-DKA)

Target 8–12 mmol/L acutely in the ED. Do NOT chase normal range — hypoglycaemia is dangerous.

GlucoseAction
<4.0Treat immediately: 15–20g oral glucose if conscious; 150–200 ml 10% dextrose IV if not. Recheck 15 min. Notify senior staff.
4–8No insulin needed. Monitor 2-hourly. Ensure eating.
8.1–14Start basal insulin if not already. Continue oral agents if eating. Avoid sliding scale alone.
14.1–20Fixed-rate IV insulin 0.1 u/kg/h. Hourly glucose. Aim ↓3–4 mmol/L/h.
>20FRIII + check ketones (may be DKA → Section 1) + IV fluids. Senior staff review.

Evidence: NICE-SUGAR — very tight control (4.5–6) increased mortality. Target 6–10 on ward / 8–12 acutely is safe. Basal-bolus > sliding scale alone (RABBIT 2 Surgical).

4
Empiric Antibiotics

Classify severity. Take cultures before antibiotics. Follow local sensitivity patterns.

Mild Localised

Superficial, erythema <2 cm, no systemic signs. Oral co-amoxiclav 625 mg TDS (or clindamycin 300 mg QDS if penicillin allergic). 5–7 days.

Moderate Spreading

Erythema >2 cm / deep tissue, no systemic toxicity. IV co-amoxiclav 1.2 g TDS (or cefazolin 1 g TDS + metronidazole 500 mg TDS). 7–14 days, step down when improving.

Severe Limb/life threatening

Systemic sepsis / gas / deep abscess / exposed bone / ischaemia. IV piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g TDS (or meropenem 1 g TDS if resistant organisms suspected). Add vancomycin if MRSA risk. Surgical source control essential.

MRSA risk (prior colonisation, recent hospitalisation, failed antibiotics): add vancomycin 25 mg/kg IV load, then 15–20 mg/kg q8–12h (renal dose adjusted).

5
Surgical Assessment
  • Probe the wound — probe-to-bone has high PPV for osteomyelitis (~89%)
  • Tissue viability — necrosis? gas? (necrotising fasciitis risk)
  • Vascular — DP & PT pulses, ABI if available; cold pulseless mottled foot = vascular emergency
  • Imaging — plain X-ray for gas / bony erosion; CT if nec fasc suspected but don't delay obvious surgery
  • Operative urgency — gas gangrene / nec fasc = theatre within hours; abscess = urgent drainage; stable ulcer = admit, antibiotics, plan debridement
6
Escalation Thresholds

Call senior staff immediately

  • Lactate >4 mmol/L
  • Suspected necrotising fasciitis / gas gangrene
  • Glucose >25 unresponsive to initial treatment
  • Absent pedal pulses / ischaemic limb
  • Exposed bone or joint on probing
  • Spreading infection despite IV antibiotics at 24h

Consider HDU / ICU

  • Septic shock not responding to 2 L fluid
  • DKA with altered consciousness
  • Hypoglycaemia unresponsive to treatment
  • Respiratory compromise / anuria despite resuscitation
7
Investigations on Arrival
  • Capillary blood glucose — act on result
  • Blood / urine ketones — rule in/out DKA
  • Venous blood gas — pH, bicarb, lactate, K⁺
  • FBC, U&E, HbA1c
  • Blood cultures ×2 + wound swab (before antibiotics)
  • Plain X-ray foot — gas, bony erosion, foreign body
  • Serum lactate, coagulation, group & save; CRP/ESR if available — CRP not routinely available in our setting
§
